## Volume 2 — The Reservoir

### Ch 51 — The Held Band
Morning after the Review. The plate office socket prints a **third line** it has never printed in twenty-nine years: standing 8,138, band six, floor 8,141, **carried on a Review ruling, number 112**. Faber reads it aloud to a queue of thirty. The room learns that the band sits on a decision and not on the figure, and that any person with a plate can now read both sentences. Relief curdles into exposure — the 11,862 gap is now public property. He recognises ruling 112 as the number a clerk wrote in red beside the thirty-first register line on the Review day, in front of him. **Ledger rises 91 → 97 with cause stated** (his name in the largest type in the city; strangers stand nearer than the queue requires). Ends on a danger: a runner in a grey coat with no badge leaves a buff square at the counter — attend, bring the plate, bring the ruling — **four boxes marked and a fifth that nobody could write**. Three days' notice. A missed attendance marks a lapse against the band.

### Ch 52 — Ink In The Margin
The Reading clerk who owns his query can no longer help him: the query closed on a finding and a finding does not sit with the office that asked the question. At the Wing's brass counter the notice is **unstamped**, which the counter clerk says is worse than stamped. In the corridor above, **Anneke Roos**, a junior clerk, is transcribing rulings onto the observation register and has been two days on the thirty-first line, which carries a ruling number in red ink with nowhere to go: twenty-nine columns, all about accounts with a figure, an interval and a state. She sends the sheet upstairs; the mark will come back as enter, refer, hold or query, and for a new shape it is **hold**, which does not go away. He learns the printed public sheet is not the book. She tells him in a stairwell — and admits she is not allowed to — that a **Veil-Research consult line** on the docket has carried the initials **S.M.** against a three-year-old memo about a transfer that carried no name, and that the initials are the only thing on the answer.

### Ch 53 — The Thirty-First Line
Lantern Row, evening. Ines prices it: every office closes a file by **describing** the person in it; an open file is not safe, it is a place people keep coming to, with a new question every eight days. She cannot tell him what an audit does with a line it has never had to read. She states plainly that she has **not filed the himself line and cannot decide whether to**, and that she has drawn a four-sided sheet — the line, the arithmetic, and the one question — and shown it to nobody. She takes shared custody by putting it where he can find it. Then the Vestry cold store: **Tessa's rack is eighteen, nineteen before nightfall**, the stippling is pale blue above the joint and four inches has begun on the right forearm, and three charts are on the rack in a row — Nell Ardery, Garrick Neve, the yard checker — turned in four seconds. Tessa refuses thanks before he offers it and asks for the monthly number said aloud. **Second planting of Sun by name**: a woman from Veil-Research, years ago, asking after a man who came in shaking at 2,640 and went out shaking, date the second of May, no face. Tessa will not be thanked and says so first. Ends on his decision to attend the Wing **in person**, which closes the option of distant compliance.

### Ch 54 — The Inventory Summons
The irreversible decision of the batch. **Silas Drant**, a Wing attendance clerk of nineteen years, lays out the four shapes the office has for a man who is not where his plate says, and he fits none: the band was not struck from the figure, it is not carried by merit, the reading did not end in a lapse or in an interval, and he is not a held account. The machine makes the sound of a key that will not turn. Drant asks, openly, for the **names behind the observation entries** — eighty-one rows, nine years, and one empty row with his date already on it. Lin Hao refuses: **no**, flat and small, and the refusal is minuted, read back, signed, and dated in a room. Drant's three points: the refusal is an event, not a work item; a man who says nothing keeps a drawer shut for years; and by refusing he has given the office the only reason it has to keep him. The counter stamps the attendance twice — the second date **six days on** — and names the third sub-level room with no window, the paper prepared and unsigned. Ends on a cost incurred: he is now owned by a calendar.

### Ch 55 — The Question She Kept
Veil-Research side room, first court, the iron stair. Sun Meiling does not answer the memo: it was asked three years ago about a transfer with no destination column, and her four-line answer is in her own drawer and has not changed in three years. She shows him two sheets of **sets** — loops grouped in threes and fives from two different buildings — and says the Review is an event about a person that was copied onto an account line because it had to go somewhere. Then she asks a **wrong question on purpose**, a plausible and moving false account of his figure, to see whether he corrects her usefully or honestly. He corrects her badly and at cost, and names what spending on himself did. She stops him on the word *transfer*: what he described was not an accident collecting in a man, and a shape in three-year-old data has the same geometry. **First scene, immovable.** Four true statements before anything starts, both of them hers first; adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated; the tender arm respected; violence and sex in separate registers. Her terms before anything happens: **not sworn, not housed, not filed**, with three reasons — inside a house that keeps no registers; a roll makes her the most reachable person in the house; and she wants to be the one on the page who cannot be reached by reading it. He is wanted for what he carries rather than audited for it, for the first time since the Review. Ends on an emotional consequence with pull: she keeps one page back and will not say it until he has stood still long enough for a mass to go flat — **a condition, not a date**, because a man who aims at a date does not stand still at all.

### Ch 56 — A Name Before Her
A normal shift, and nothing happens, which is the alarming part. Nine corridors, the figure moving, no new faces, no turns. A transcript of the ruling is taped inside the washroom door at eye level with Ola's question on it: **what does a finding number do on a register line** — and Ola names the answer herself, which is the join: the ruling number is on his plate and on a Wing register line, and that is the only thing anybody needs to come and ask. The third floor is quiet by agreement. **Colm Deveraux's door opens two inches for the first time since the 9th of the season.** Through the gap, on a bad shoulder: a woman in a dark coat with no paper came on the second day of the week and asked him one question at the counter of his own floor — *whether anybody had asked him for names* — said *that was very useful* when he said no, and then got a second answer out of him about arithmetic that he has held for four months because of a stair at eleven o'clock. Nadia refuses in advance to be spent to fix his calendar and puts on the record that she chose, unasked. Ends on the consequence: **his refusal in the Exchange protected nobody**, because a frightened man gives the office answers for nothing, and the list is being assembled out of other people's mouths.

### Ch 57 — Cutler's Row After
Afternoon at the yards. **Nell Ardery** on the landing of number 14 asks him plainly to make the cold stop, and he refuses and gives the reason without dignity: four in five days, and a second one into a person who has had one is a reportable episode. **Garrick Neve** — who lives at number 22, one of the thirty-one requisitioned dwellings, because of him — has worked out to the minute what happened on the Review day, and asks whether to write it up, and decides in public that a rumour is worse than a fact. The **checker at the yard gate** has never given anybody an address; Lin Hao tells him to write his own episode up anyway, which is the first time anyone has ever told him to put something on his own record. **Halvard Rees**, the control who never turned, watches him spend nothing on the entire row and refuses to call it the right decision, because the cost of it is sitting on three people and not on him. Physical risk and cost: a loaded barrow comes down the line of the last six doors on the hoist wire and he takes it on his forearm, the second time in the arc, and the grey comes about a second and a half behind it. Ends on a danger: the checker's docket has a **buff Wing attendance slip** under the week's gate tallies, unfolded, rewritten, his name on it, no date and no box marked — and the man at the gate works out the whole of it and finishes his own chart that night.

### Ch 58 — The Refusal At The Desk
Second attendance, a different room, a Field officer present. Drant produces the **wall map**: a printed district map with a newsprint rectangle over the Reading square, thirty-one pencilled marks on the Ferrier arc and one in the middle of the square, and three dots ringed in a second colour. Ruta Vesely, the analyst, had his plate, his band, his band line and the Review date and nothing else. Ines's chapter-49 sentence is paid on the page: the dot is that room. The request is put a second time in front of a Field witness and Lin Hao **dictates his own minute**, as he did on the 23rd: the faces are not instruments and an office that wants a name is describing him and he will not be described by an instrument; the three on the map are his cost and not his evidence; the himself line exists, is unfiled, and will not be offered. Runa Sato answers only for her own file, in writing, and refuses to answer for him. Drant mentions a thin dated sheet in a drawer, unsigned, and does not turn it round. The refusal is minuted and the schedule lengthens to **every nine days, three dates written**. Ends on a reversal: the Office's ally is not a form but a calendar, and in five years the only thing that has ever moved one of these is the account going quiet.

### Ch 59 — Three Colds At Once
Vestry cold store after hours; Ines in the corridor, an auditor may stand in a corridor. **Tessa refuses thanks before he offers it** — she did this, it is her inventory, thanking her is thanking her for her own invoice — and asks for the monthly number said aloud, the number and not the names. He says **twenty-two**; the rack is at twenty-two, twenty-three before nightfall, with a gap above it that she describes as where the next eight go. Right forearm begun, four inches in three weeks, and she is not short of forearm, she is short of room: four walls, three and a half, and the half is a chart rack. Then the drawer: **two hundred and sixty-one bodies over eleven years that present better than their own histories say they should**, under a third traceable to anybody. And the medicine: **a settled mass reads as too well** — a fed body gets better and stays better, which is not a mistake a machine can make; a quarter of the district improving faster than medicine can do it is not a set of charts, it is a line. She gives a date without hedging: **the twenty-fifth of next month, six weeks from the Review**, and says that is when she would put her hand out, and that the instrument has been able to read it since four days before. Ends on the revelation with cost: standing still is not the absence of danger, it is the Office's ally, and it needs nothing from him.

### Ch 60 — The Standing Inquiry
Thursday box at the Exchange with the queue out of the doors and turning at the corner. **Garrick Neve** puts the weekly question, and then spends four seconds saying in public that he was one of the three, that he wrote his own episode up unexplained, that it cost him, and that a decision made quietly in a corridor is not a decision but a piece of administration. Reading: 8,138, three under a floor of 8,141, three grains is the whole margin. Drant attends to observe and does not interfere, logs the discharge as an attendance kept, and gives him three dates in front of him — **the third, the twelfth and the twenty-first of next month** — and tells him he does not have to come on the first two and does have to come on the third. The square is entered for the first time in six weeks: the black six, four figures under the name, the fifth still the largest number in Ostara, a woman and a child standing in front of it. The visibility produces **no conversation at all**. **T2 touched and left shut**: the query clerk steadies a corner of the week's docket that was lifting, with her thumb, for a moment longer than the hand-over needed, and says nothing. **B5 opened and not pulled**: Ines says her sister's name once, in the queue, for the first time in her working life outside two offices — Ding put her own name on an intake sheet for a chest, because Ines told her in April not to see anybody else about it — and Lin Hao asks not one question. The Reading Office has asked her formally for the source of the unfiled line and she has answered with a question. **Ledger rises 97 → 103 with cause stated.** Ends on an earned hook: the fifth written attendance is four days inside the date Tessa gave him, and Sun Meiling's page is still unsaid.


### Ch 61 — The Ledger Quayle Keeps
The 28th. **The branch corridor board gains a second line, typed**: his Thursdays, the inventory room, and *attendant*. The same first notice that was unstamped in Ch 51 comes back **stamped** — the interval has gone from nine days to **seven**, and it carries three ruled lines the Office could not put in a box: **days attended, persons spoken with, addresses**. The runner says plainly that the third line is the join and that she does not need to explain it. **Anneke Roos**, met again at the register desk, counts the Wing's books and admits there is a **fourth**, out of a drawer since before his first attendance, with a page for every entry the register could not take, five fixed headings and a ruled space where a sixth should be. Its page for him has carried twenty days of dates since the eighth. Her gift to him: **she does not read it, she keeps it**, which is the only difference she has found in twenty days. **Ola Bright** has taped nothing and posted nothing and says so first: a typed line gets copied by people who cannot type and read out by people who cannot help it. Change of situation: the Office has stopped asking him to describe himself and started asking him to describe where he has been, and the place that gets answered is a corridor he has to walk through five days a week to get his wages.

### Ch 62 — The Shape In Data
The 3rd, the first weekly attendance, the bargain. **Silas Drant** lays out a card of two columns: fifty-one of his figures since the start of the season (8,140, 8,139, 8,138, 8,138, 8,138) against every fourth ordinary plate at the same counter, which goes up two hundred, down ninety, up four hundred, up eleven. **A standing that does not feed is evidence.** He offers the strip: the branch plate-office strip, taken weekly on a Thursday, on his shift, in his own corridor, **not a copy**, forwarded to the Wing, which discharges all three ruled lines — **attendances, persons spoken with, and addresses** — and holds the interval at seven days. Drant reads it back and adds the price aloud: a held band carries a tolerance of three grains, he is at 8,138 against a floor of 8,141, and **from today the figure is printed in public with a bell on the machine.** He signs with a pen he was handed, four seconds before he realises it is the first thing he has ever given that office. Cost on the page: two days a fortnight is the cheapest thing on the table and he took it because he was tired. Ends offstage: **Alma Quayle** reads a flat strip a week for four weeks and then a month, and writes the sixth heading herself, in her own hand, on the twentieth, and it is two words long.

### Ch 63 — What Was Sent
The 5th, the fortnight she asked for. **The wall is bare** — two hundred sheets off the wall and into a drawer nine feet away, because she has been looking at pictures of him for three years and a picture of a shape is the safest thing a woman with her trouble can put on a wall. She gives the sentence whole, in instalments he is not permitted: **what is in him was sent; a sent thing has a sender; the file it went into is One, One is the bottom of the Wing's list, there is no Zero, and the sender chose the lowest address that would hold it.** The consult that came up to Veil-Research three years ago was headed with that number. He understands four bells separately: it was never his, somebody gave away 214,000 grains on purpose, and **his brother was right in a public hall three years ago** — so what he has been carrying is not a question about a figure but a receipt. She names the head of the Office and does not soften it. **Second scene, immovable, and a different direction from Ch 55:** in September she refused to audit him; here she asks to *read* him, out loud, with her hands on him, for an hour, and he wants it. Four true things, hers first and longer, then his, including the true one — *I do not know what I am doing* — said on arrival. Adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, no draw spent, no impaired state, violence and sex in separate registers; she narrates him aloud throughout and it is for her, not for him. Aftermath, in her two findings: **he is a shape with no place in it**, which means a map cannot hold him and a dot on a printed district map will never be him — and that a man with no address can be neither found nor cleared. She re-pins two hundred sheets and says she is going to stop asking about the data and start asking about him.

### Ch 64 — The Address Book
Lantern Row, the 6th. **Ines reads him the four-sided sheet** and will not let him read it, because he would correct the arithmetic. Side one is the line reading himself. Side two is the arithmetic and a heading, *why it is there and what it cost*, with no names. **Side three is new**, written on the third after the strip, and it is *places I have watched you stand in* — eleven of them with times — and it is two-thirds useless and one-third lethal, because the register keeps addresses and no faces and his ledger keeps faces and no addresses and the third ruled line on the buff square is the join. She gives him no addresses because she has one and it is not hers. Side four now carries two questions, and the second is hers: *what does an Office do with a man it cannot put an address to* — and the answer she arrives at is that he is the only man in Ostara who cannot be found, and an account that cannot be found cannot be claimed, and that is the whole reason it landed on him. **The decision:** she hands the sheet in on the seventh rather than filing it, on her own hand, so that it becomes the only true document in the city about a man with no address and cannot be described away by the next person with the file; and she puts it in his pocket so that the minute will read *attendee volunteered receipt of document*. Under the twelve lines there is a twelfth, empty, with one word printed over it: **RECIPIENT**. [One hundred and nine lines. Eight turned.] Ends on what she cannot unask: on the seventh a woman is going to say a true sentence into a room and an office is going to be able to write down that she said it, and for the first time in three years there will be a piece of paper in Ostara that describes none of the five things paper describes here. It describes a man with no address, and **a heading is the only thing about a document that cannot be unasked.** Last beat, across the city: Sun Meiling re-pinning two hundred sheets in the order she always puts them up in, having stopped asking about the data.

### Ch 65 — The Ungiven Word
The Ferrier line, the 9th. **Beatrix has taken him off her list** — off it since the thirteenth, and he has never asked why, which she has been waiting four weeks for. The price of the Review is not a quarrel: if he is on a column she tracks what a minute costs him, and if he is off it he is a passenger, and a passenger is somebody she is glad to see and somebody she could refuse, and she tells him she would, on a bad night, and that it went through her like a needle when she said it. She has lost her map of him and kept her map of the line. **The ungiven word:** she uses one about four hundred times since June and has never used it about him, because a word is how a person commits to an answer in this city before they have the evidence, and there are two answers and she does not have the right one. In exchange she gives a date — **the ninth of September**, hers since June, never told to anybody, not a favour — and takes it back by telling him it is not after anything of his. **The threat:** four turns last week on her own line, a woman with a notebook and a booked seat, asking operators for their counts and whether they can feel it when it goes, calling it a survey and not an audit; at the Yard end she asked a signalman for the attendant at the branch office and got him in eleven seconds, because Beatrix spent four months asking eleven signalmen to put a light on for him. Seen from the car: the woman is at the yard gate and the checker is answering her.

### Ch 66 — What September Still Holds
The 10th, a Thursday, in two halves. **The Exchange box, and the first consequence of Ch 62's signature:** the figure has been flat for an **eighth** week and a man in the queue says the word *flat* out loud, and the wrong answer assembles itself in the largest room in the district — a standing that does not rise is a standing with nothing going into it — and **Garrick Neve decides not to correct it**, which Lin Hao is grateful for and feels sick about. The strip is on a spike in his own corridor with a bell on the machine: he knew what he was buying and did not work out what he was selling. **The household above the chop-house.** **Marguerite's three answers** for the strip — invisible, meaningful, valuable — and then her refusal to use any of them: a thing she has touched is a thing she has a standing to call in, and she is not going to have a standing in him before there is a document; and the real reason, which is that four months and two houses and eight women have not got him, and she would rather be the woman who never knew than the woman who made him knowable. **Nadia came up four flights** because she cannot take the lift anywhere and would not be a thing about herself, and tells the room that the woman with the notebook sat in her car and that she gave her his name, on purpose, having worked it out first, because she is not frightened of a notebook and he is — nine hundred people already know, it is the only thing she is in any record about, and strangers talk. The last good news of the batch, and the batch's best plain sentence: the ledger cannot be held shut with his hands; it happened in front of strangers. Marguerite, from the stairs: his porters are being asked too, and a date he has washed off twice is the only asset he has that is not in his own hands. Nadia makes the room's rule about September and will take the car down and not come back up for a month if he says the word.

### Ch 67 — The Wall Map
The 12th, fourth attendance, and **Ruta Vesely is met for the first time**. She has eleven minutes of office time and no authority, and asks for the box at the bottom of the map — *place of event confirmed by subject* — to be left empty. Her deduction, done off his plate and his band and the date of a room: **the thirty-one marks along the Ferrier arc are not a pattern of him, they are a pattern of where he walks**, and the dot is a spend, and there has been exactly one spend since the start of the season, so the map has one event on it and an office cannot keep a finding open on one event. If the dot is a person the map has a place and the Office has him; if it is a spend the file closes. **She does not believe the map and has written that down in the third colour.** He gives her the true thing rather than the useful thing: the room was the room, it will happen again only if he spends again, and the map will be wrong either way — **and he refuses to correct it, because a corrected map would be a description of where he goes, and he would rather have a wrong map.** The cost is named and it falls on other people: an unconfirmed box means the Office attends that room forever, and nine clerks set it up and take it down, and one of them has already been asked about a Thursday. The chapter's turned line, in his own handwriting and over her initials: *Place of event confirmed by subject. Subject confirms the room and declines to confirm any recurrence.* Drant says in advance that the Office has read an empty box as a refusal for nine years and this time it will be wrong in the correct direction. Asked whether he will spend again, he answers: **I do not know**, and it goes up as *unknown*, which is the only word the standing schedule has to keep answering.

### Ch 68 — The Cold Store Count
The 16th, the cold store, two degrees, lamp on a cord, couch nobody uses. **Twenty-six**, said aloud, and he is told off for wrapping a number in a sentence. **The ladder to date: 17 (Ch 50) → 18 (Ch 53) → 22 (Ch 59) → 26.** **The drawer has gone down**, 261 to **258**, because she went back through the bottom hundred where her own handwriting is worst and found three ordinary people who got better; and the rate of the rest has not moved in a month, and the reason is that the only man in this city doing it has not done it once. **The Office has reached the clinic**: an anonymised request form at the ward for *the number of patients presenting in a manner inconsistent with recorded history*, no names, perfectly within the rules — arrived by the chain of Ch 62's flat strip, then Ruta's finding, then a line, then an address. Her answer will be **258, cause: not established, not one of them** — the first thing she has ever put in writing — **and one case: her own.** One page, no symptoms, no history, and a column she has had to invent, and the reason in her own words: *the treating physician's own record is consistent with the same finding, without cause.* She is the only body in the city with a settled mass and a clean history, and if she goes up as a control then two hundred and fifty-eight of her patients stop being a line and start being a coincidence with a doctor in it. She will send it on the twentieth and has no idea who will write back.

### Ch 69 — The Fourth Box
The 17th, and **there are two of him**: the seven-day standing on one sheet and Drant's nine-day schedule on the other, and *a standing supersedes a schedule, a schedule does not supersede a standing*, so the third, the twelfth and the twenty-first Drant wrote in a column on the eighteenth of last month were never cancelled. **Three dates after this one** — the twenty-first on the schedule, the twenty-fourth and the thirty-first on the standing, four days apart then three then seven — and Drant tells him the thirty-first existed before he knew it, so he cannot later say nobody told him. **The second kind of attendance:** the Office has never in nine years attended anybody off an analyst's sheet, and now it has, and **three buff squares are on the table with Nell Ardery, Garrick Neve and the yard checker on them, dated the nineteenth**, and the fourth box on each is empty. **The irreversible decision:** the acknowledgement reads that the three episodes *were caused at his hand* and are his cost and not evidence against the persons in whom they occurred. Drant refuses to argue and states the price: **it is what his brother did** — a thing said out loud in a room is an instrument, and an institution handed an instrument has to do something with it — and the first document in the building saying a man did a thing to three people on purpose is now in his handwriting and cannot be unsaid. Unsigned, three people are asked what they felt by a man with a pen, and a woman who has had four in five days gives a bad answer, and the bad answer is the only version of that afternoon in the city. He signs. **Runa's warning:** he has made himself a thing that must be decided again, and the last person in Ostara to describe him is now a clerk with a rubber stamp. Ends on the file widening in both directions: a map with three names goes up on the twentieth, and the woman in the dark coat with no paper at all is being asked the same question by the same office.

### Ch 70 — The Man Who Filed
The 21st, the schedule's fifth date. **The signature created a referable thing** — an instrument with no box cannot be closed on this floor — and two things left the office in an envelope on the twentieth. Everything that leaves the floor is registered in a thin cloth-bound book on the attendance desk, and Drant turns it round because he is entitled to show a man where his own document went. The outgoing line carries the receiving house, **Veil-Research**, and a name printed on the form by the house so that the reply need not be looked for: **PERRIN SOWE, RESPONSIBLE.** He says the name before he has decided to; Drant then closes the door and tells him what it is — **a man who says a name out loud in a room, in four seconds, unasked, has given the office an instrument, and he is the reason.** He files nothing and tells nobody and enters a note that he was not asked. **Perrin Sowe is on the page as a consult line and a respondent's name and is in no room.** Up the iron stair, **Sun Meiling says it first**, because he came in with the face of a man being told something: Sowe has an office on the third floor with a window on the yard, has been there nine years, and has **never once come down her corridor in three years**, and she took that for a man being busy. **What went back three years ago was not her four lines.** Her initials are on the transmission because a junior drafts and a responsible officer answers, and the four lines underneath them are his, and she has not read them and will not. She also reminds him that she has had a branch strip in her hand since the fourth — the corridor beat where a filing clerk on her own floor took it off a colleague, looked at it, gave it back and said nothing, and none of the four other people in that corridor looked up is **Ch 63, where she first tells it**, and it is referred back to here and not retold. **And then the door that does not open:** she offers him one true question, any question, and the only one he wants is whether that man knows where his brother is, and the price is a brother's name said out loud in a room with a woman in it, so he asks her instead about the rota, and gives her a day of the week. She keeps it, does not spend it, and tells him that not asking is a habit and a habit is not a fault and is also not a shield.

### Ch 71 — The Unasked Question
The 22nd, the room above the chop-house, five women and two calendars on the table and neither calendar mentioned for half an hour. **Nadia Ferrous volunteers first and out loud:** she gave a stranger his name in the Vestry lift **twelve days ago, on the tenth, on purpose, having worked it out about four seconds before she was asked**, because the woman asked about operators four times and never once about a clinic. Then **Ines Duarte comes up three steps and stops**, and puts the question on the table and does not help it: **who writes the sixth heading?** Not what it says — who owns it. Anneke Roos has said twice that the head of that office is wrong about the sixth heading and right about the arithmetic, and when she gets it right the first twenty days of his page move under a sentence he never saw and cannot take out. **She could stop it and will not, and says so before anybody accuses her.** A rank-two observation is added at the end: four strips, four identical figures, a margin of three grains printed weekly with a bell on the machine, and nobody has yet asked the machine a question that would change the answer. **He does not answer with a speech.** He says he will write it down on the 27th and gives a date for the first time, on an ordinary day with nothing written on it. Tessa says twenty-seven on the wall — **five days** — and Beatrix says the ungiven thing plainly: every calendar in the room is a document somebody else wrote. Ola copies the question down and dates it.

### Ch 72 — The Grey At His Temple
The 23rd, six past twenty, the second-floor stair of his own branch. The woman at that stair has read his plate off strangers for four years and **has never once said his name**. He puts his hand over hers on the plate and pushes. **Nothing moves.** Nothing happens at all — no cold in her hand, no cold in his chest — and the whole apparatus produces nothing, like a machine turned with the belt off. He files the strip a day early that afternoon, in his own hand, because a day a man puts in himself is a day nobody can take off him. **The cost arrives at half past nine and it does not go.** The grey is at the left temple, where it has always been, and since the Review it has always gone inside a day and a half; this time it stays, and by the second day it has come forward along the ridge above his ear. Two nights of eleven minutes at four in the morning. He does not tell anybody until the 25th, on the steps of the Vestry, in eleven words with the cause attached. **The woman at the stair is kind about it** — *you're grey, you should go and sit down* — and asks him no question, and the kindness is worse than a question. **What the chapter proves is the negative case and then stops:** he has assumed for four years that the thing works, that it is a thing he does, and he gets as far as *it was not that he had been unlucky* — **and lets it stop, in his own words, because the negative case does not come with an answer fastened to it.** The conclusion is deliberately withheld; it is said once in the batch, in Chapter 75, to an analyst, at cost. Ends in the corridor at seven on the 25th with forty people who have been looked through by him for four years and never once looked at by him.

### Ch 73 — The Plate Without Merit
The 24th, a Thursday. Drant enters the strip as received on the day before, because a clerk who corrects a man into his own calendar has taken over that man's calendar. **The strip is a forwarding, so the actual paper has been leaving the branch every Thursday and then leaving the Wing**, and the thin register says where: **the Exchange**, four times running, with a clerk's name printed under the receiving house he cannot name. **The Office's other arm has read the flat figure and written the only sentence an office can write** — *a standing which does not rise indicates an absence of input at the member's place of score* — and it is **exactly right about the surface and wrong about the cause**, which Drant makes him sit with for ten seconds. The form goes to the holding branch. **Ottilie Prebble, three years and four months at the plate-office counter, has to sign the weekly return.** She writes the true thing — office aggregate is not small and not nothing, cause not at the counter and referred — and it will be read as a confession of a drain. **The harm is named and it is not his fault**: an Exchange reading it as absence of input triggers a place-of-score correction and an audit of the branch aggregate, and the name at the bottom is a junior clerk's, and Faber, twenty-nine years at that counter and never once asked to account for a machine, was called into the front office on the twenty-third and came back and did the fourth-floor washrooms himself. She signs it anyway, because an unsigned return is an unanswered return and an unanswered return is a lapse, and a lapse against a held band is a finding against the band. **Ledger panel: 112 lines, eight turned.** Ends with the woman at the stair saying his name for the first time in four years.

### Ch 74 — The Holding Ward
The 25th — **Tessa Okonkwo's readable-by date, and her second scene.** It opens on the four quiet rooms at the back of the Vestry and the chapter's title: **a person is held because a date is open.** Ilse Korvin has been in the second room since the fifteenth because she did not use a berth she paid for in March, and the standing does not close until she turns up, and she can get to the Exchange and stood outside it for an hour and a half on the seventeenth. Tessa: **a date does not close itself, and while it is open there is nowhere on paper for her to be discharged to.** Four rooms, two full since the spring, and nobody in this building is holding her. She says she has not opened the drawer with his reading since the twenty-second, and asks him to come back on the twenty-ninth. **Then the scene**: she shows him the inventory made flesh — the stippled forearm, the flat shoulder she cannot lift, a handprint on the fourth and fifth rib, a needle mark — and four true statements, hers first and five long, then his four, and the fourth is the true one again. The direction is new: **she wants to be looked at as the thing that is failing**, not read, not audited, and she has never let anybody touch the inventory. **No draw is spent.** He asks for something — her hands, and not to be let go of — and she takes him at his word for an hour. Afterwards she refuses to let it become a transaction and makes the chapter's change: **a standing clinical debt.** A day book, monthly, his name and a date and no number, for the month her rack runs out, so that when an office asks what she was holding and for whom she has a name and a date instead of a sentence she made up at two in the morning. He writes L HAO on the first line. **Twenty-seven on the wall for eight days is the best clinical result of her working life and she is not going to thank him for it.**

### Ch 75 — The Turned Room
The 26th, the Reading's third floor. **The nine chairs are stacked in the corridor with their legs up**, and Ruta Vesely has stopped the room attendance herself: her own finding, *a distribution of one event is not a distribution*, office attendance discharged, no further attendance at any address on the map. She has spent the two facts she carried alone since the twelfth and names the woman they were about: **Sabine Rooke, who has set that room up for eleven years and was asked twice about a Thursday.** **The arithmetic, done standing on the bare boards:** three episodes, three buildings three streets apart, four seconds. Three ordinary causes is not a thing that happens. One thing leaving this room and arriving in three streets is not a thing he can do, because in four seconds his hands are in this building and no other. **Therefore nothing left this room; relief does not travel and never has; therefore there is no point; therefore the dot on the map is not a person.** **The proof is on the Office's own paper:** the acknowledgement he signed on the seventeenth joins the word *addresses* to a man by his own hand, and a hand cannot be in three places, so the Office's own instrument falsifies the Office's own instrument and the fourth box is empty because there is no source, because there is no point. **The cost:** he asks for the room attendances to be paid off in full and in the open, and then he says the account's number out loud — **One, the lowest file in Ostara, no Zero** — and says *I am the address and I have never been the source*. Ruta understands what she has been handed: one word and a digit, the only shape a description can take, and a paper that will leave the building with a name printed on it. She closes the file and loses the ability to defend her finding. He says the two words **aloud, to an empty room, so that he would have said them.**

### Ch 76 — What Settling Means
The 27th, the register corridor at ten past four. **Anneke Roos lets him into the register room, which she has never done.** The fourth book has five headings — attendances, plate, ruling, figure, margin — and a ruled sixth space. She will not write in it herself: a word in a junior clerk's hand at four in the afternoon would be a word a junior clerk chose. **He writes NOT EARNED on a clean sheet and she enters it as received.** She will not copy the first twenty days across, because the moment they sit under that heading they stop being entries and become **a claim**, and it will have to be answered by somebody. **She gives him the Office's own word: settling is what the books call an arrival that has stopped moving, and a settled figure is publishable, and a published figure is the only kind anybody can do anything about — forty days from the last time it MOVED, not from the last time it was spent, a distinction nobody in that building can make because there has never been a figure there that moves when nothing is spent.** Then the sheet goes out at five o'clock and the thin book prints the receiving house: **VEIL-RESEARCH, PERRIN SOWE, RESPONSIBLE.** He knew. He says it in one sentence and she registers the time on the back in the register's hand. **The chapter's landing, in short sentences:** nothing in him had ever come from him, and therefore the only line in the ledger that could never have been relief is the line that reads himself, because relief goes to the person it came from; he had put that line in on the thirteenth of last month with sixty people behind him, and he had not spent anything that afternoon — he had been the mouth. His brother's sentence was not a question about a figure. It was a delivery note.

### Ch 77 — The Forty Days
The 28th, a paper bag on the counter in Ferrier Street, and then Nadia Ferrous with a pencil because she works a timetable. **The arithmetic: forty days from the last time the mass MOVED. The last time it moved was the thirteenth of last month. Seventeen days to the end of that month and twenty-three more into this one. The horizon was the twenty-third and it is five days past.** **He has been counting from the wrong end since the third of this month — from the day he stopped spending instead of the day it stopped moving.** Nadia's correction is the chapter's engine: standing still is not a thing you do to an office, it is what an office does to you, and a duration costs the person it is applied to absolutely nothing to run. **She says the second cost out loud, once: the longer he stands still the more true the map becomes**, and she says she has known it since the fourth and did not say it because she had given his name away and did not want to add a thing to a pile. **And the real answer: he cannot tell whether it has gone flat.** Either it has not, which means the number is wrong, or it has, and a flat figure is describable, and four streets away an office has had a strip of it every Thursday for a month and an Exchange has been building a return form on the flatness of it, and a junior clerk has signed a true document twice that will be read as a drain. **He does have an instrument, and she points at it: a drawer off a corridor with four beds at the end of it, and a date he gave.** Ends at that corridor at twenty past nine, not knocking, because he keeps a date he has given.

### Ch 78 — The Weight She Carries
The 29th, nine in the morning, and he asks for it as he promised. She turns the instrument away from him and takes three readings ninety seconds apart. **The same number three times is the definition of settled, and she has never produced it in this city for a living body.** It is not in grains, it has no interval, it is not a reading and not a score, and any machine in this city will give three clicks and a scrape and a blank. **The shape is the finding and he asks for the shape instead of the number: a settled mass is not slack, it is a room with something in it being held at a constant.** It has not changed in seven days. **And then the chapter's real damage: a settled mass makes a body too well — that was her working theory since March and it fits all two hundred and fifty-eight charts and it does not fit him.** He is not a body that is too well; he is a load-bearing structure, and if it is not a tank then three years of arrivals are not accumulating. She refuses to make a sentence out of four days of a reading and eleven years of charts. **Then the weight she carries:** she is at the end of her rack — twenty-seven on the wall, four walls, no fourth wall, two years of counting — and she tells him the one time in eleven years she put down something she had been holding and the person in the bed went in four days later, with no cause. She wants somewhere to put it, and the only structure available is a line in a day book with his name and a date on the first of every month. He takes the sheet and she takes it back. **And she will tell him on the first what she has not told him yet, because he asked her a question this morning and it would be dishonest to answer it and then go back to being somebody who keeps things.**

### Ch 79 — The Yard Gate Watch
The 30th, six past ten, and Beatrix Halm puts the tram at the stop outside the chop-house and does not say a word about it. **The notebook woman is on the tram: third seat, a season on it, four turns, every working day since the eighteenth, and the guard has checked it four times without being asked.** At the yard gate the checker has his own account ready and unprompted — the three of them attended on the nineteenth, one at a time, the fourth box empty, and he does not know whether it was kindness or a man saving it for somebody. **The checker says the thing the chapter turns on: the dark coat has been at his gate twice and asked the same three questions a man with a pen asked inside four rooms, in an ordinary voice at his own work, and she did not tell him what the answers were for.** The notebook woman says his name, says he did not give it, and gives the whole of her method: nineteen operators, one of whom could feel it, the Vestry lift, and **a signalman at the Yard end who had been at that gate nineteen years and said his name in about eleven seconds because it is a name everybody in a corridor says out loud without knowing they are saying it.** She does not write names down and she keeps them, which she says is worse. She has a season she bought herself and four hundred and eleven counts and no badge, and she says the first question anybody has asked her in two years that has been the right one. **At nine that night the household assembles and works out that there are two collectors in one city and that they are not the same kind of animal: one that reads and one that talks, and the one that talks is the one who is going to get put in a drawer.** The finding is four words: the second has been at his own building for **forty days** and the Office has had her on a list since the seventeenth and has not touched her, and she does not know the other one exists. **And Nadia claims it in the doorway, in front of everyone, three weeks late.**

### Ch 80 — The Paper He Will Not Sign
The 31st, a Thursday, and the fifth strip of a standing. **The schedule is finished** — third, twelfth, twenty-first, all kept, and the hand that renews it writes a column when a standing supersedes it and has not written one since the third. **The standing has five dates and no end date, so he has attended everything anybody has ever written down, and there is nothing after this morning.** Then a prepared, dated, unsigned sheet is put face up on the table: four paragraphs, and the fourth holds an attendee at the desk for the term of the inquiry and cites an account in this office's custody, **one digit long.** It came up from three floors on the **twenty-fourth — two days before he said the number out loud to Ruta, and seven days before it was put in front of him**, and both of them sit with that and Drant says the whole of what it means: the fourth paragraph does not say the man holds an account, it says the account is in custody and the man at the desk holds it, **and this building has been living on the gap between those two sentences for three years.** **The batch's decision.** He does not sign it. He does not refuse it either, because Drant's point is that a refusal is an answer and an answer keeps him there, and Drant will enter a third thing that does not exist on any form. **Instead he tells the Office about the two women.** Both, in full, with the notebook woman's method and the dark coat's forty days. He does it because the second is four streets from being in his own file, and when the Office finds her the third question is *who have you spoken to* — a gate man, a porter in Bellhouse, **and a woman in a lift at the Vestry.** **Ruta writes down where each of them got his name and one of the sources is that lift, and he has to give a woman's name out loud in a room with a clerk in it.** He cannot say it back to her in a room because it is a piece of paper in four minutes. **Nothing is signed in this chapter.** Drant enters attendance kept and the order as prepared, unsigned and unissued, and at the end, to an empty room, says that there is a third thing after all and that he has had it for nine minutes and is not going to say it again in this building this month.

### Ch 81 — The House Behind Him
The 1st of next month. Seven women in the room above the chop-house: Nadia, Beatrix, Tessa, Ines, Marguerite, Ola, and **Sun Meiling, who came in last off the landing and did not sit** — the count now matches the seven names. Nadia's name is on a sheet that left at four on the 31st. He says the sentence back to her and she makes him say it **again**; it is minuted in the house. **Sun seeds her refusal of the household's protection logic** (outline fixes her refusal by Ch 100): the sworn are the ones who get requisitioned, the unsworn are the ones who get described, and standing between her and a list makes her the reason there is a list. Ola writes it down and dates it. **Marguerite carries the change that leaves the room: an instruction has come round the doors of Bellhouse — every caller, parcel and inquiry about a resident entered in a visitors' book with the hour, readable on request from the Exchange.** She refuses the comfort of indecision and makes it stand anyway, with the glass off her own mantel, and says she will read the book herself at the end of every week. Beatrix and Tessa each name a cost they have chosen (the season woman who would move to another tram; twenty-seven on the wall). Change: the household is people the Office can look up, **and a building in Bellhouse has become a register that keeps running whether they are in it or not.** Nobody leaves, nobody enters, he promises to stay findable, and Nadia adds that he must not come *less* often to make it look like there is less to find.

### Ch 82 — The Dot On The Map
The 2nd. Ruta lays the map flat with the ring still round the Review square and draws a second ring round the margin. The nineteenth's three addresses are not a room; the thirty-one marks are where he walks. The Office's attention moves from a room to a man in the instrument: **watch the attendee**. **Register count corrected to five strip lines and a sixth started for the seventh**, which agrees with Ch 80 (fifth strip on the 31st) and Ch 87 (six strips on the 7th); Ch 71/73's inherited "four" is left alone as reviewed-and-repaired. **Two of the three buff squares have already gone out on the 1st and the third goes at four** — three women in three buildings now hold one each, and Ruta refuses to name them, because the not-telling is their only protection left, and she will not do it on the floor. Drant gives the rest of it: the Office's line is in the district line, so two readings of the same sentence are equally available and neither office has reason to prefer his. **Cost named on both of them**: Ruta's map has stopped being a record and become a drawing she must keep from her own division, and Drant's entry is a third kind of line in a book never built to hold it. He asks Ruta what she would do if he offered a second spend; she calls it the worse of two wrong questions and says a reported refusal is a direction. Ends on the cost — a second spend makes a line with his name at both ends.

### Ch 83 — The Quiet Floor
The 3rd. Tessa's mass reads settled, held at a constant, unchanged. The floor is quiet because nothing is arriving. Visible worsening in a person, not a chart: the ward runner sprains her wrist catching a board, is off the stairs a fortnight, charts do not get filed. **The change that leaves the floor: a clerk from the Exchange arrives for an eleven-year summary line, is told there is none, and records in pencil *summary not produced, given by the holder in person on the third, no substitute offered*** — one of three in a column by the third of next month, after which the Vestry stops being a service failing to reply and starts being a service being asked properly and not answering. He reads the weight at eight, nothing is written down, and the runner with the wrist refuses the arrangement in front of both of them until Tessa restates it as one she can actually keep: no boards, file and sort from the desk, call her if a pile falls. He asks the girl's name and is refused, correctly. **Day count corrected to tenth day yesterday, eleventh today** — the grey, counted inclusively from the 23rd, per Ch 77's sixth day on the 28th. He keeps the 29th and the 1st dates and is told to come back on the 4th.

### Ch 84 — The Sister's Stair
The 4th. Ines walks him to the Cordwain intake stair, the Volume 1 location. Ding put her name on a sheet on the 20th of last month; Ines said her sister's name once in a queue. **Ding is not on the page and B5's answer is not opened** — the stair is location and pressure only. She orders the four-sided sheet with RECIPIENT to stay in his coat: neither evidence nor protection while it is a question. **The change that leaves the stair: a lawful production request for everything an agent has touched since the Review that has not gone into a district file, dated the 13th of next month, with a form attached.** She has written the refusal, dated it the 14th, and will post it on the 13th; producing costs her the line's only remaining shelter and refusing costs a review of her ticket. She names the figure before it happens — three offers of private work she has not taken — and says a private agent who does not produce records on request is a woman with a flat and a habit. She also confesses she knew on the 22nd that he was going to file Nadia and watched, which is the second record she has not put on a list.

### Ch 85 — The Locked Office Opens
The 5th. Colm Deveraux unlocks his office, shut since the 9th of the season, and admits he gave the dark coat two answers at his own counter for nothing and answered the Office last week. He opens the room, asks for no forgiveness, and puts his own episode — nine pages, dated — in the drawer, and will not let Lin Hao read it. **The 12th: a sitting on the arithmetic, eight names, his sixth.** He asks for one thing — do not vouch for him, because the moment Lin Hao vouches the finding is about both of them — and Lin Hao gives it. **The change that leaves the room: the corridor.** Two returns clerks look in and one advises leaving the door open all day, not because it helps; nine people now know there is a thing in Colm's life that he kept in there, and there was not. Lin Hao gives him the one fact nobody in that room will have: the dark coat never asked for the arithmetic, she had it by then, and she got the building off a gate. Colm commits to opening the door every morning including the ones after the 12th. Pay toward B1 without resolving it.

### Ch 86 — Marks The Field Leaves
The 6th. Runa Sato's second scene, immovable, gap 74 to 86 twelve. Between an inventory summons and a requisition she chooses in writing: attend to inventory, decline delivery to holding, Field retains file. **Sixth direction — she asks to be kept, to have a line in his keeping on paper, rather than to keep him.** Adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated; four true statements first; no draw spent; violence and sex separate; **the scene itself is on the page** — a Field room with a narrow bed and a jug on an upturned crate, her harness bands and a two-day-old bruise, her weight a stone and a half more than the eye agrees to, and a man who is used to being the instrument asking to be the floor she puts down. She tells him where his hands are throughout; twice she grounds him in the room rather than in her body, which is a field habit for people who stop hearing where they are. She washes him the way they do in a field wash, which is not washing. Irreversible: **he gives his word for her household line and she signs it; her deniability is gone and the Wing can answer her instead of rounding her.** He cannot walk it back.

### Ch 87 — The Finding Number
The 7th, a Thursday, sixth of the standing. Runa's minute entered and read back; Drant notes the household line is hers, not his. Ruta shows ruling 112 joining machine to register across six strips — **3rd, 10th, 17th, 24th, 31st, 7th.** Step 4's instrument is time: forty days from the last movement, **fifteen days past the 23rd** (corrected from fourteen by the volume-close audit), and the finding number is how a settled figure gets published. **The change that leaves the room: the figure is now a *reference*, not a case** — a line in a district table assembled twice a month by a Reading Office clerk and read by people who will never ask him anything. Five people and a tile handle it; not one can be got at, because none of them did anything. Ruta distinguishes tabled (quoted whole with the assembly date) from hand-copied (which is what a district table travelling round a building actually produces). Drant enters the weekly return as information, not as an instrument, which cannot now be unentered by anyone who did not see it entered, and tells him to be so dull in the book that there is nothing to carry.

### Ch 88 — The Wing Stair
The 8th. Drant stops him at the top of the second sub-level stair. The thin dated sheet lies in a cabinet drawer below, prepared, unsigned, never turned round; the drawer has been open every working morning since the 24th. **He does not descend, does not turn it, signs nothing.** Drant went down once, nine years ago, with a junior clerk, and opened nothing. **The change: four unsigned holding requests have been sitting in a tray since the 24th and go back down stamped at four** — a sheet that can take whichever name is put in it first, waiting for a name the Wing can put in itself. A runner comes up from the goods lift looking for initials; Lin Hao asks her how many have gone down unanswered and she says four. Drant enters the time, the floor, the reason and both their names in the thin book — **the book, not the tray, because the book stays on the floor** — and says so out loud, up the stair, where the runner hears it. He chose to stand on the third step, inside the frame of the corridor and the light, rather than go down or stay at the top. Nothing is signed and nothing descends; the option of a drawer with no name on it is gone, and Drant is now in the same book as the man he has been protecting.

### Ch 89 — The Held Room
The 9th. Precedent of Ch 74 extended, not a classification, not the third sub-level. Petra Hall, a tram conductor, holds the fourth quiet room one night because her date is still open — she turned up, the clerk was out. **Nineteen nights since the spring, four of them Ilse Korvin's and one of them Tessa's, and Tessa has been lying about the rooms in the ward returns for five months.** She refuses to make it a debt when Lin Hao tries, and says the floor is four beds short every day. **Petra's own account, and the thread the room turns on: she bought a berth in March in a shed behind a tram office, never used it, and cannot return the card without attending — so it has been the reason for a line about her and six refused questions since March.** Tessa says that is a holding and that she is going to read the card before she builds anything on it, and that the fourth room is a courtesy. The fourth room is the last one; a fifth goes to a man on a blanket in the office. Petra refuses a man outside a gate at eight in the morning. He asks her the thing he actually came to ask and does not finish it in a corridor.

### Ch 90 — The Cost Of Keeping
The 10th. Petra's date closes in eleven minutes with a stamp and she goes home on a tram. A ward clerk, **Fenn**, comes down with a copy of the 31st sheet and a request for the days Nadia worked the tenth and the persons she spoke to; **a man has already telephoned the desk about it and will come back that afternoon.** The lift log exists and the building manager will let him at it, because a manager reads the heading and not the paper, and the log will put forty to sixty names off Nadia's own page into a document they have no business being in. **The beat is rebuilt so it is not a fourth telling of Ch 81:** Nadia names the count herself — *that is the third time you have said it; the first time was the first and I made you say it twice, like a man reading out a docket, and I let you do it twice because I wanted to see whether you would soften it* — and asks for it again with the paper under his hand, because it is different when the paper is in the room and not from memory. He says it a third time with Fenn present, and adds the whole chain. She gives Fenn the line to use with the manager and refuses the line that would help her. Ends on the instrument reaching him: the forwarded strip read in the Vestry corridor, figure unchanged, and a lift log open on a table somewhere below it.


### Ch 91 — The Fourth Summons
The 11th. **The plate hold.** A fourth notice, dated the **9th** and put face up by Drant on the 11th after two days of carrying it back and forth, holds the plate at the place of score from the **15th to the 20th, six days**, with no figure published for the term. Its third paragraph is the Office's own sentence turned into an instrument: **an absent reading is an absence of input; absence of input requires a check-in by an officer of this office; the check-in is not made at the place of score.** Drant says the whole of it in one line — *they are not holding your plate, they are taking your reading off your floor.* **Four costs, given before the decision:** **there is no Thursday at all inside the six days**, so the machine stops on the 15th and the next one is the 21st, and six days of a corridor where ninety people walk past a silent machine is an event they all hear, and then the bell rings a week late and everybody who timed their week by it knows what a week late means; **Drant is the man who stops the machine**, on his own floor, under his own hand; **Ottilie Prebble will have to sign the Exchange's own absence-of-input sentence back with her name at the bottom**; and Faber, who has cleaned the fourth floor himself since the 23rd of last month and told nobody, is about to have a place-of-score correction and a branch-aggregate audit signed with his name on it — **and Drant tells him on the fourteenth and not before, because a man with four days will go and ask somebody.** Change: the Office has found a way to measure him somewhere it cannot be checked, and the batch's horizon arithmetic runs from Ch 87's own figure (the 11th is nineteen days past the 23rd; the 20th will be twenty-eight).

### Ch 92 — The Unfiled Line
The 12th. **Colm's sitting, held on the second floor because the room on the third has been shut since the 26th with nine chairs standing in its corridor.** Eight names, Colm sixth, reading seventeen weeks of hand addition that does not balance by a constant. **Lin Hao keeps the promise from the fifth of the month and does not vouch** — four seconds, counted. He could have said the dark coat got the building off a gate; he gave that away on the fifth and promised in a doorway not to say a word of that shape in a room with Colm in it, and he says nothing, and it costs Colm his arithmetic. **The change: the finding carries a note that a document exists, entered voluntarily on the seventh by an agent of the district, which is not a district record and which the office has not been permitted to read.** Nobody refused. Nobody asked. The four-sided sheet with RECIPIENT printed over an empty line has been sitting in a district file for five weeks being nothing at all. He goes to Lantern Row and Ines says the general production of the 4th becomes, on the 13th, a production **of a document**, with a finding number in the corner; and she has decided and will not be argued with: **she refuses, in her own hand, with a reason that is true and about the document and not about him.** Her refusal goes at half past eight on the 13th and is dated the 14th because she will not sign a thing she has not slept on. She also tells him in two sentences that her sister went on the 9th for a chest, and asks him not to ask the rest of it.

### Ch 93 — The Vestry Bench
The 13th, the stone bench outside the Vestry with a brass plate he has never read. **He comes to tell her there will be no printed figure between the 15th and the 20th, and she was waiting for that sentence for ten days, because it means her reading on the 29th has nothing public to compare itself against.** What she tells him instead: **the rack has stood at twenty-seven with nothing arriving since the 17th of last month** — twenty-seven days, twenty-eight if you count the first — and she has been putting both numbers in the ward returns for five months so that nobody in the building has to have the conversation he is about to have. **The plain sentence after the machinery: the thing that has not arrived at that clinic for twenty-seven days is him, and an officer working backwards from an empty rack will reach him in four minutes.** She refuses to put her instrument in a Wing file and says so before he asks: the moment it is named in a sentence about him it stops being hers and becomes a reference. **The runner with the wrist is at the desk and is not named**, and he asks and is refused, correctly, again. **The third *summary not produced* falls on the 3rd of next month**, and after it the Vestry is a service being asked properly and not answering. He offers to read his arm so she has one honest afternoon, and she reads it twice and writes nothing down, which is the result and not an omission. **Grey: the twenty-second day, counted inclusive from the 23rd of last month, which is the day it began — not from the 3rd.**

### Ch 94 — Bellhouse Tea At Night
The 14th, a Thursday, **the seventh strip of the standing** prints at 8,138 and is forwarded to the inventory room at four because that is where it goes now. At night at Bellhouse, with **Marguerite's visitors' book** on the table and the glass off her own mantel beside it. Sixty-one entries since the 1st; nine of the twenty-two who asked about somebody asked about one of them, and she will not name the nine and says why. **Her cost, said once: the instruction was written by somebody who wanted a building where the pressure could be seen, and she chose to make it happen, and what she has instead is a book that says which of her household was asked on which day at which hour.** **Sun Meiling, in the house for the first time, has been asked inside Veil-Research and has answered truthfully and completely.** Her finding number has just been attached to an unfiled document, and the fourth and final provenance request is a **direction to the house**, not a visit to her door — which Ruta's finding makes the dangerous outcome, not the safe one, because a direction leaves no door to record. **The change: Nadia puts a notebook on the table and proposes that the household record where he is and when, from the 15th to the 20th, because for six days nobody will know whether he is alive.** Sun Meiling names it as the argument she made on the 1st and says the standing makes the household the register, and that he does not get a copy or a vote or the number of his friends who wrote down that he was in a kitchen on the 15th. He asks for the notebook to be closed; she refuses and will not be argued with. **Marguerite's terms stay down in a drawer, unsigned, and are declared off the table.**

### Ch 95 — The Ninth Chair
The 15th, the first silent day of the hold. Nobody noticed at ten and four people in the corridor had noticed by twenty past ten. Drant tells him the shape of it: **on the night of the 12th, two men carried eight chairs up to the second sub-level in a goods lift at eleven at night.** Drant has not gone down and gives his reason — that corridor is not on his floor. **Lin Hao goes to the third step and no further.** Eight chairs against the wall in two rows of four, one chair square to a door four feet out, and through the gap at the hinge a strip of light, the edge of a table, and the grey cloth off the inventory table of the seventh. **He could have gone four more steps and does not**, and what he feels is not fear but the nausea of a thing being made, lawfully, out of his own household's furniture, by people who have done nothing wrong. **The ninth chair stayed.** It is on the third floor of the Reading with a folded card on the seat, and the woman sitting on it is **Sabine Rooke, who has set that room up for eleven years**, named for the first time. She has been asked twice about a Thursday and told the truth both times and says that three is a different animal from two, because two is a woman being thorough and three is a file. She tells him to come and find her first if she is asked a third time, and then immediately tells him he is the worst person in Ostara to say that to and that she is going to do it anyway.

### Ch 96 — The Empty Trolley
The 17th, the third day of the hold, and **not a Thursday — there is none inside the six days; the last one is the 14th and the next is the 21st.** The strip does not come off the machine and **the forwarding envelope must go anyway, because a forwarding that goes late is a forwarding that did not happen.** Drant offers two things: nothing, which has not happened in nineteen years, or a sheet with the figure on it and the reading's date under it, six days old by the time it arrives. **Lin Hao says send nothing**, and the second half of his reasoning is true and the first half is fear: a stale figure copied onward becomes a claim nobody can date. Drant enters it as *figure not produced, reason: no reading taken at the place of score, forwarding made and empty*, and does not ask him to sign. **The trolley goes down the long road at half past three with the rail empty** and a fish woman asks whether the machine has broken. **Tove Marlin, a Wing clerk, conducts the check-in in the Vestry corridor** because the check-in is not made at the place of score, tells him up front that it is not a summons, asks whether he will give an account of the absence, and — the one thing she is not permitted to do — **tells him a paper with his name on it goes to a desk on the 18th, drafted on the 17th, because a man who is told the day before can spend a night doing something about it.** **The change: he chose to be absent rather than stale**, and an envelope that arrives empty is an event rather than a figure.

### Ch 97 — The Auditor's Answer
The 17th, at night, Lantern Row. **Ines Duarte's second scene, gap 86 to 97 eleven.** Her refusal went at half past eight on the 13th, dated the 14th, and four days later nothing has come back, which she says is worse than a refusal. **The revelation, and the volume's: the himself line has been read wrong by every office in the city including its author for four months.** A ledger entry is a pair — a person relieved and a person it came from — and his line has one name on it. The word in it is not *from*. It reads *to*, from a side of the entry that has no name, and the reason is that on the thirteenth of last month he was standing in a room holding something. **She can describe him in one sentence, and the sentence is that he is not a tank, he is a door** — and if that line is filed the Wing has a shape, and a shape is what classification is. **Her decision, made on the 12th and waiting four days to be said: she refuses the production, and refuses the second one too, and will stand in a review and say that being true was not the same as being owed to anybody, and she will not file it in a drawer and sleep.** **The scene, and it is a seventh direction: she asks to be the one who gets him right, and he wants it.** Adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, no draw spent; four true things hers and four his before anything starts; she corrects him out loud, at the wrist, at the forearm, at the ribs, at the date he has been counting the grey from, and his want is to be corrected by the one person in the city who can read him. **The terms are on the page and they are explicit: she is awake and unmedicated and says so, she will ask twice, he may stop her with one word, and she wants him to have said something true and unpleasant about himself.** **The back half of the scene is on the page, not summarised** — the blanket off the couch, her naming where his hand goes before it does, and then the second correction, about the carefulness, which is the expensive one: he has been careful with four women for five weeks because he thought he was going to break something, and she makes him say out loud that he is not careful. Violence and sex stay in separate registers. **No draw is spent and she says so afterwards in the only register she has for it.** Ends on the line staying unfiled and legible to two, and on the paper that is coming within four days.

### Ch 98 — What He Will Not Spend
The 18th. **The lawful, narrow demand**, dated the 17th, put on the counter face up with Drant's hand on it: attend at the hour and place named (the 20th, the hour the plate hold expires), present the plate there, repeat the episode of the 13th of last month under the control of the office, and **name the instrument that will measure before and after**. The fourth paragraph is the price: the office describes the account once, in its own hand, the inquiry closes, the standing goes. **He refuses all four, in person, in words, on purpose, and asks for it to be entered as a refusal rather than as a third thing — the deliberate opposite of Ch 80.** The reason he gives for the third is that there is no episode: there was a room, sixty people and a man at the front who was the mouth of a thing being given away, and a description of a thing that happens once cannot be made by watching it happen again in front of people who came for a hearing. **The fourth he refuses by name, so the refusal is on the record as a refusal of that condition: he will not name an instrument**, because the only one in Ostara belongs to a woman with a rack on her wall and a girl she is keeping off the stairs. **The costs, given by Drant before and after:** the five-date branch standing and the fourth floor are **discharged at ten past eleven**, and with them every Thursday he has; an inquiry without a standing is an inquiry on **dates somebody else sets**; the band is not lost but is **suspended pending description**, which is a different and worse shape; and Anneke Roos will have the refusal in three days and the ruled sixth space in the fourth book is going to get a line in it. **IRREVERSIBLE. The option of remaining undescribed at the price of a calendar is closed forever; what replaces it is an open-ended summons on another building's schedule.**

### Ch 99 — The Classification Chair
The 19th. **Three costs in nine hours, none of them his.** **Tessa**: the runner's wrist mends on the 19th, the boards went up at one the day before, and the desk at four empties — and a third request has come asking not whether the clinic holds an instrument that reads a mass but whether it holds one, period, with a form for her name on it. She answers in four lines: it exists, it is hers, it is clinical, the readings are not produced. **Her cost, in her own words and after carrying the four lines since the 12th and writing them nine times: in order to refuse to describe his mass she has had to describe it.** The word *mass* is now in a Wing file in a clinician's handwriting on the 18th, and 258 charts stop being a clinic and start being evidence about an ordinary person. She refuses him taking it off her. **Runa**: the Field declines to deliver the subject a second time, in writing, against a dated demand — and the Wing answers her paper with **six lines of her own household line**, entered on the sixth at his word, read back to her in a room as *available*. A field operative who disobeys twice is a disciplinary hearing in about two months, and the hearing will list everyone in Ferrier Street. **Sun Meiling**: the fourth provenance request is a direction to the house, and she refuses it in writing inside Veil-Research with her name and number on it, which makes her the only person on that floor who has ever said no and puts her at the top of a list in the best finder in the city. She will not let the sentence about that be written down, because a sentence on a pad is a thing a man can produce in a year. **The classification paper is on Anneke Roos's desk, dated the 19th, five paragraphs and a sixth space, unsigned, and not in a tray.**

### Ch 100 — The Door At Rank One
The 20th to the 29th. **The resolution, and none of it is a win.** The hold expires at four on the 20th; the strip on the 21st is the eighth Thursday running, entered as received and **forwarded nowhere**, because the standing was discharged on the 18th. Anneke Roos puts the classification paper in the tray at four, unsigned, two days after it arrived already decided three floors above her — the third unsigned paper this desk has sent back this month. **She names the sixth space: it is ruled so that a person can be shown where it would go, and the hand that draws it is a hand three floors above her she has never seen.** **On the stair at five he reads the plate on the classification door from the top of the flight for the first time since the hold was put up**, because the drawer is shut and the goods lift is lit, and it says what classification is and that the holder is returned at rank one and nothing else comes back with him, and **there is no handle on that side of it.** He does not go down. **Ruta names the district-table clerk — Saskia Verhoeven — and tells him not to go and find her**: a reference becomes a finding when somebody asks the clerk why the line is there, and the answer will be a person. **The household notebook stays theirs and he is refused the signature he offered; six women write it, in six hands, on the 21st, after six days of not writing it.** The plate is 8,138 against a floor of 8,141, three grains of margin; the band six still sits on the Review instrument and not on his plate;
