the three Review turned stay turned; the corridor board carries his name and the word held. **The horizon was the 23rd and is thirty-six days behind them** (the month Ch 77 is standing in has a thirty-first — see `reviews/volume-02/volume-close.md` §0), the mass has not moved across thirty-one readings, and the account is past everything an office needed.** Tessa writes the one sentence in the day book she was promised in June, in her own hand, on the line with his name on it. **Volume 3's question opens at the end: keeping the account means being looked at on the Office's schedule, and when the measurement comes into the open it will not be a description of him — it will be a description of everyone who ever fed him.**

---

## Volume 1 — Rank Four

### Ch 1 — The Second Basin
Lin Hao's Wednesday plate comes back **3,412 grains with no interval** — a figure with no rate behind it, so no band, so no publication on Thursday. He takes it to the counter clerk, Faber, instead of walking out, and Faber calls it *unsettled*, wraps the plate in oilcloth, and puts him on the Thursday review list, which is how he ends up in a forty-name queue that goes up to the forty-first office. Behind the last counter he finally reads a door he has walked past for four years: **CLASSIFICATION WING — INTAKE AND SUB-LEVEL ACCESS.** Continuity: the counter shuts at eleven and an unpublished plate reads as nothing at all; six washrooms on the second floor and no window anywhere on it; his two-year blank in the record predates this posting. Open: what is on a Thursday list and who reads it.

### Ch 2 — No Interval
He reads the public band table and understands for the first time that **six is the first rank with a verb in it** — sealed filings open — so the want stops being a boy's arithmetic about a door and becomes a number that tells him what to do next. At the Correction Office petition rail he finds Petition 4118: Ding D. Duarte, rank two, auditor, refused, *no instrument of record*, act not scored under any available heading. A messenger gives him the floor phrase — *taken for a reading* — and a counter clerk, whom he deliberately lets see him ask, tells him they keep you until the arithmetic closes and to take a book. At the end of the corridor a rank-two auditor in grey is leaning against the wall where she has plainly been for some time, and she says his name. Continuity: Faber is settled and untouchable; **eleven unsettled readings a year citywide, nine audited by Ines** — the one figure in the batch, quoted identically in Ch 1, 7 and 8. Open: how a woman forty-one offices up has his name.

### Ch 3 — Nineteen Grains
On the Ferrier tram his hand is on a stranger's wrist before he decides to put it there. Something cold and heavy runs out of him into **Halvard Rees**, a wire-splicer from the yards, and a four-year-old pylon injury goes quiet. **In the same instant the cold crosses back** — three seconds of it, up through the wrist and into the old place, visible from the outside in the man's shoulders and breath — and Halvard attributes it to a draught off the junction, as any sane man would. In the concourse under the Reading his wrapped plate reads 3,393 — nineteen grains lower — and the notation arrives.
[Ledger. Three lines.]
Continuity: the draw is relief, not strength; Halvard is finished, not well. **He does not thank him** — he refuses to, out loud, in front of Lin Hao, and warns him against doing it twice. That refusal is the first turned line, made in nine seconds, by accident. Two of the three lines belong to people he has never met. Open: what the other two are.

### Ch 4 — Cold Minutes
Two days of touching nobody cost him the ordinary ability to stand in a corridor with people in it. The Thursday review is nineteen minutes and nine questions with a tired auditor; nothing is wrong with him that is anybody's business, and of the forty names on the list nine go down. On the landing outside, two auditors discuss a woman in a Vestry lift who has been logging cold spells for four months — **the third one is different, because a pattern gets an address.** On the Friday his figure has risen by 69 while he spent nothing: accrual is proximity, not contact. Exhausted and sleepless he takes the Vestry lift and the operator **Nadia Ferrous** catches his wrist as the car lurches; a cold minute goes into her hand and she laughs, and says *that's the third one this week*.
[Ledger. Nine lines. One turned.]
He makes a rule he admits is cowardice, and it lasts seven days. Continuity: **the grey at his left temple starts here**, after two days of not sleeping, and stays. It does not exist before this chapter.

### Ch 5 — The Junior's Return
**Tobias Renn**, junior filing clerk, rank two, four years in the filing room, tells him at length and with pleasure that the draw-down opens Monday the nineteenth, that ninety-one names get re-read and nine go down, that the *files* are what matter, and — reading from the card — that Lin Hao's second line reads **COMMON ORIGIN. SOURCE UNPLACED.** Tobias is a non-feeder: Lin Hao takes hold of the same requisition slip at the same moment, nothing moves, nothing is given, and he is grey at the temple before he reaches the second floor. Supply room three is closed pending audit, the key is with Colm Deveraux, and **Emrys Vaz**, seventeen, carries stock up from the yard at six. His want, for the first time, has a face on it that is not his own. Continuity: **Tobias's file goes in Chapter 23 on his own arithmetic.** Bernt Halse on the fourth floor gets one paragraph here and has no idea what is coming.

### Ch 6 — Supply Room Three
Withdrawn from the rota, Colm Deveraux tells him there is no form that opens a store in a draw-down week and refuses to be moved. **At ten past ten on the Thursday night** Lin Hao puts a hand on his chest to shove a door — and the wrong thing goes in: not relief but **account**. Colm's Exchange figure jumps a thousand and one grains, his rate does not move, and he goes up a back stair and locks his office and will not see him again. **Forty minutes later**, at eleven, the Vestry supper run comes up; he takes four trays off the rail and **Ola Bright** catches his arm on the step, and a cold minute goes out of him, and she shakes her hand out and laughs about it before she has let go. Stock moves at six. Four sheets of penciled diary are found under a saucer on the landing rail — eleven entries from the fourth of June, one word, *again* underlined twice.
[Ledger. Fourteen lines. Two turned.]

### Ch 7 — Lantern Row
He goes to Lantern Row because he cannot think. **Ines Duarte**, rank two, thirty-four, two rooms she cannot afford, four hundred files, will not take her coat off. His file is four pages and a bone-coloured card: four years of employment, a mop, a two-year gap where he does not appear at all, and at the bottom of the gap a cross-reference — **LIN WEI. R.6. SEE ALSO — READING OFFICE TRANSFERS, CLASS.** The want moves from nine to six. The bigger fact is the one she cannot stop thinking about: in three weeks not one instrument has touched that file, and somebody has gone to the trouble of keeping it clean for years. She asks *who helped you*. He does not answer; he tells her what Petition 4118 says about her sister instead. She lets him, and does not leave. Continuity: he has one felt want on the page — the ninety-one — and it is what drives him into the room.

### Ch 8 — Afterward, Ines
He tells her nineteen grains. She assembles the whole thing in under a minute and gives him the rule: rank six petitions a sealed filing. She tells him about Ding and the long stair and the 300 grains she has been carrying for four years. He says *I've got some of that* and tells her the cost before she decides. She takes her coat off and hangs it where anyone coming in will see it, and says **yes, do it, now, before I think about it.** The relief goes into her; she shakes from the heels up and says her sister's name into a pillow.

Then the scene, written at length — explicit, unhurried, and entirely hers, including an instruction she gives him *mid-scene* that the giving and the wanting are two different transactions and she is not going to let him confuse them. Afterwards she says the sentence he has believed without checking for three years: you have never once in your life been the thing that happened to somebody. Then, with her head on his chest, she feels the ledger — nine of the fourteen — reads off nine names she can identify, finds that not one of the nine is on the Thursday list, and writes a tenth in pencil on a clean page. The name is **Ola Bright**: not on his file, not on hers, not in any list in the city.

### Ch 9 — A Cold Minute, Recorded
The four pages go on the table and Ines reads them in the order the cold happened. The argument: he will go alone, she will not let him, and she is right — she is the only person who can find where a person with no reading sleeps. The count is fourteen; he has deliberately touched three; **eleven lines belong to people he has never touched.** At four in the morning she says *somebody has to* has just been proved twice — once by her sister and once by a woman on a bench with a pencil — and refuses to be sent back to her room. They come down into the Vestry arcade at ten to five into a shelter hall of thirty people who have been waiting since six the previous evening, and on the end of the front bench is a woman in a woollen hat with a school exercise book open on her knees.

### Ch 10 — No One Keeps the Diary
Ola Bright is delighted, not frightened, and hands over forty-one pages. Nadia has been telling the whole arcade, including a man from the Church of the Quiet Number. Eleven episodes, never four in a week. Ines explains that the diary is the only record the cold leaves and that the last tenth of the distance to interesting is a room with no window. **Bernt Halse**, 51, fourth-floor stock clerk, went down at ten past ten on the Thursday **in the morning** — the same clock time, twelve hours earlier, as Lin Hao putting a hand on Colm Deveraux, which is the sentence he cannot stop trying to join up — and has not come up; his sister **Ditta** has been on the Vestry benches since six on Friday, twice sleeping there. **Hester Quill** at the Classification Wing's public counter explains, kindly, that nobody is taken for a reading for anything they did, that the person stays until the book closes, and that her job is to make sure the name was on a form. He asks Tessa Okonkwo whether a body can be found in the records that does not match the body in front of her; she says yes, she has a box, and she does not count them. On the clinic rota board: `MON WED FRI — KITCHEN RUN — O. BRIGHT`. He does not see it. Across the road, a rank-five clerk in grey has stood on the step of the Church of the Quiet Number every morning for three years filing the same petition — *count the ones nobody counted* — and asks Ines, the fourth auditor in three years, to stand on the step and read it.

---

## Later batches

- Batch 0002 — Ch 11–20. Cards in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0002.md`; prompt in
  `workspace/volume-01/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`. **Grain spine: 4,012 → 4,412 (Ch 15) → 4,612 (Ch 18) →
  8,141 (Ch 19, aggregate). Ledger 14/2 → 16/3 (Ch 13) → 19/3 (Ch 18–19). System messages Ch 13 and 19.**
- Batch 0003 — Ch 21–30. Cards in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0003.md`; prompt in
  `workspace/volume-01/batch-0003/PROMPT.md`. **Figure unmoved at 8,141 — no climb, no aggregation.
  Ledger 19 lines, 3 turned → 19/5. System messages Ch 25 and 30. Scenes Ch 26 (Tessa) and 29 (Nadia).**

### Ch 11 — Ola Bright
He has the ruled sheet and the pencils for copying her book in his coat and does not use them. She is
delighted to be asked to *work*; he asks to read all forty-one pages sitting down, tells her one true
thing — the third one is different — and watches her write it down. Nadia crosses the corridor twice
loudly. The arcade is the widest room in the Vestry and it is full of doors nobody records. Ola's rota,
flat, flatmate Mord and her refusal to have it explained all come out between entries. He leaves the
book with her, which costs him the copy, and takes the Beatrix Halm name instead — eleven times, four
spelled Bright. Ines tells him admitting you is something he is unexpectedly good at.

### Ch 12 — The Ferrier Line
He pays for a ticket with a plate that has opened a staff gate for four years and stands in the public
queue for twenty minutes. The 980 shortfall is passengers, not staff: strangers helping each other to
the door is the densest thing in Ostara. He gets on with a good day and gets off heavy, has to sit in the
shelter for eleven minutes. Ines rides out with him and audits the doors instead of the people, and makes
him say the thing he wants out loud before she will show him anything. One mis-spelling is a date, and the
date is a roster day when Beatrix drives the arcade car to the yards.

### Ch 13 — Nine Hundred and Eighty
Rank five is 8,000 and he is at 4,012, so 3,988 of his is in no ledger he can see. Tobias Renn explains
it with pleasure. Two porters and a bag of brass fittings go past and one of them attests it in front of
him: forty-one grains, a stamp, and a crowd. He stands in the marked square alone and learns from a clerk
of twenty-nine years that a man may attest his own hands, and that the only part of the machine still
requiring a person is somebody asking somebody else. Two new tram lines take him to 16/3. Ines works out
the nineteen turns at the yards and makes him promise to write to her with exactly what he asked for and
exactly what he got.

### Ch 14 — Two Witnesses
The Board is repainting and Dereck Hain's tile goes to white four rows above his own, and he finds that
wanting a tile and wanting to be looked at are not the same thing. Four refusals: Nadia in a second,
Ines because an auditor attesting a drawing account ends her seal and she says so out loud, Ola because
the one person genuinely willing is the one person the city cannot record. He reads Beatrix's depot
roster from just inside the door, washes the pencil off his hand, walks out through a gate that is not
guarded, and finds his heart going. Nadia gives him a roll for a day that has not happened yet. Ola shows
him four years of somebody else's pencil work and the word headache underlined until the paper went soft.

### Ch 15 — The Interchange at Four
He stands where two streams cross and practises being looked at, and nobody returns it. He cannot stage
anything. The nineteen comes round too fast, he moves before deciding, and for half a second it is not
about the Exchange at all. The clerk stamps 400 grains in front of two hundred people, eleven plates go
up, and she witnesses it herself: permanent, and it does not come off for being sorry. He cannot find the
woman with the basket afterwards. Three feeders feel it at once — Beatrix Halm at the brake with thirty
aboard, Nadia Ferrous at the terminus, Halvard Rees at the Cordwain bench — each named, housed, working,
and none of them thanks him. Sixteen lines, three turned, and the count has not moved.

### Ch 16 — What a Band Costs
The Exchange is marble and a permissions board priced in waiting time. A woman with grey hair carries a
slip she has kept since last winter and is offered March, and takes it. 3,588 still to run, 400 at a
time, nine evenings, twenty-seven cold minutes, one of them a person who keeps a book. Emrys Vaz's rota
line is deleted on the twenty-second and he is on the list at the bottom under a heading for staff who
hold no plate. He takes the day off and spends it on the arcade wall doing nothing — and the figure goes
up anyway. **Stopping is not available as a category.** Ines tells him the difference between him and his
brother is a document, and goes down the stairs without her file. He says out loud that he would do it
again. Tobias has worked it out.

### Ch 17 — The Cab at the End of the Shift
Beatrix states her terms before hello: a use, not a man. The price is agreed out loud before anything
starts. The scene is the scene — cab and roof in the rain, unhurried, and afterwards the argument about
it, which is the actual price: she felt the interchange in the brake and put a man through a wall last
winter for less, and she has a hearing she cannot afford and a column with three ticks in it. She gives
him all four years, including a name she has seen on a rota in his own building. And the triangulation:
nine turned lines is a legible shape, and the dot is not a street, it is a house.

### Ch 18 — Signal
Four hours of laying her counts against his on his own table with a ruler across them, and the arc
appears: yards to free clinics, four times a day, with the shifts. He walks it and counts eleven doors in
two hundred yards. His poverty is a power stat. Ines puts her coat on his table, which she has never
done, and tells him what happens to the one person who is asked for an address, and what happens when
somebody with a seal asks where his figure comes from. Beatrix brings the week's plate herself: two
hundred, not four hundred, because the Exchange prices the deed and not the man, and says the ninth one
is her in September. Nineteen lines, three turned, all located.

### Ch 19 — Field Order
The ninety-one are on a board by the second-floor door, his thirty-eighth down, Emrys Vaz last under a
heading for staff without a plate. The clerk reads his plate twice and calls Vole, who reads it a third
time. Every plate in the branch carries two figures — the man's and the floor's — and the floor's is
released in slices only to somebody with a witnessed deed. **His 3,529 of doors and trays and washrooms
was held for four years and releases automatically.** 4,612 + 3,529 = 8,141. White tile, rank five. He is
grinning in a corridor for ninety seconds and then goes out to the rail and sees his own tile is still
black, because the Board publishes once a week. The same plate carries a Field observation request.
Tobias tells him at the turn of the stair, to the wall, that eighty-two of the ninety-one settle, nine do not, Lin Hao is number four on Tobias's sheet and Tobias did not put him there, and that he has about six weeks before it matters.

### Ch 20 — A Woman With a File
Rhee's office has a door that closes and a chair with a broken caster. He wants Lin Hao employed, not
punished — plate reissued in the branch name, four times a week for two hours in that room, on the record,
credited to the Branch and not to him, and he says out loud that the day Lin Hao wants out is the day a
man in an office asks him a question. He neither takes nor refuses. He asks for the audit; Rhee grants
fourteen days because nine would bring him back on the ninth. On the stair, Runa Sato says the order is
not Rhee's, is not Tobias's, was written before the twentieth, and that she has read four other files this
month of people who fit the same shape.

### Ch 21 — Ninety-One Names
Runa's observation room is a store cupboard she furnished herself, and she states what observation costs the observer: every word she writes is a thing she did, and a Field operative who reports a supply is the instrument that took him. She asks what Rhee offered and he tells her all of it, term for term, which is the lever he spends. Then he gives her the other thing out loud: the account is **geography, not intention** — one arc between the yards and the free clinics, four times a day with the shifts — and it moves even when he stands still. She writes `Runa Sato, Field. Observation only. No requisition recommended at this time` and signs it, and tells him it is worth exactly a week. Rhee is in the concourse at ten in the morning in a coat four hours early and closes the offer without heat: a man who has put what I offered on a public record cannot then sit in my private room twice a week on the record. **The last friendly door in his working life closes with a courtesy and he did it himself.** Ends on the formal deferred schedule: ninety-one names, a time against each, his at 11.20, and the small print — *attendance compulsory; those not attending are deemed unsettled.*

### Ch 22 — The Draw-down
The Branch cleans itself at seven and nobody comes in early. The clipboard copy of the ninety-one left on the second-floor rail gives up two names he already knows the shape of: **Emrys Vaz** and **Ottoline Barret**, forty-second down, eleven years holding the fourth-floor stair door. Nineteen lines, nineteen people in four years, ninety-one names in one building — the two lists meet, and the draw-down has landed on the invisible. He stops riding the Ferrier line the long way: the figure keeps moving and the faces stop coming. **Spending on somebody listed marks them**, because relief is invisible and a cold minute is not. Then Lantern Row, and the debt paid: what he asked Beatrix for, what he was given, and — the uncomfortable part — that he paid for it with himself, in a cab, at her insistence, before anything started. Ines's real anger is not the book. It is that she could have asked first and could not have moved a page of it, and he could. She will not take the rota name for a month and signs a paper saying why. Ends on the half-landing with Tobias Renn holding out a folder and asking, on the record, **what he is** — and Lin Hao saying "I don't know," because every true sentence available contains the word he cannot afford.

### Ch 23 — Two Kinds of Clean
The witness book has a left-hand column for automatic releases and a narrow column for attestations, and the narrow column is no longer empty: the 27th's release of the 3,529 carries *Attestation entered to satisfy reconciliation. T. RENN.* A true release with a false attestation, entered on a junior clerk's own initiative to protect the Branch's reconciliation on a draw-down week. A retraction before four o'clock is a correction and nobody writes it up. He has seven hours and forty-nine minutes and takes it nowhere, because every route — Vole, Ines, Rhee — ends in a document with LIN HAO on it. Tobias works it out, states all four lines against himself in the front of his own file, offers Lin Hao the witness line, and is refused. The cost is named: off the filing register, not suspended; twenty-nine months of correct filings re-opened by hand; then the boxes in an annexe. Ends on the empty chair and a name written out for the first time — **EMRYS VAZ**, the third tick in Beatrix's column, the boy he promised in a cab not to spend.

### Ch 24 — What the Clinic Files
The Vestry keeps paper because a headcount file cannot hold a temperature at four in the morning and nobody funds asking. Tessa shows him intake charts that are all correct and a rack of ten clipboards in the cold store that are not, one of them a woman on a cart at arrival who walked out carrying her coat four hours later with two identical entries in one hand. He has been walking forty-one minutes instead of riding eleven, and the count stopped when he did. He tells her about Colm Deveraux's 1,001 and asks to **aim it**: put the figure in an account on purpose, on a consenting patient. She refuses the ward and allows the cold store, after hours, because it is the one room where nothing belongs to anybody else. Consent is hers to obtain and she has not asked yet. Ends with permission and a time: **9.40, and Wenna Ost**, a laundress refused a hearing four years running who wants it written down if nothing happens.

### Ch 25 — The Wrong Direction
Three people in a cold store at ten past ten: Wenna Ost, who consented; Teodor Mace, who agreed at eight to an intake test because he cannot sleep; and Tessa with a clipboard. He has been awake two nights, has walked eighty-two minutes that day, and his hands are not steady. There is no stranger here and no deed, which is why the first deliberate act of his life has nothing to react to. He takes a wrist and aims, and four hundred grains go sideways. Teodor Mace's plate reads 22,640 with an unchanged rate and an unchanged chest, and Wenna Ost's reads what it always has read, because he has drained the woman who agreed to it and given the man who did not. Records stop matching: 2,640 was a man's figure in May, on a clipboard on Tessa's rack, dated the second of May. **The account was running before he knew what it was**, and he ends on the wrong chart.
[Ledger. Nineteen lines. Four turned.]
He keeps back from her, for the first time, that the count has not moved since the 29th.

### Ch 26 — Cold Store
Tessa takes the unreconcilable error out of Teodor Mace and into her own frame, where nobody checks it, and tells him this cold is not one to four seconds and is not in the hand. That is her gift and her inventory, eleven years of it, grey-black stippling from wrist to mid-forearm. She turns: nine of his colds since June on other people, unexplained, called being tired. She also tells him there is somebody else in this city doing what he did in May, and that she has a date. Then the scene — no file, no note, adults, awake, unrestrained, and the first of them who will not let him treat it as a transaction; she makes him ask. Afterwards she takes her position out loud: she is going to be in it, not as one of his but as the person who holds it, and she gives him the count — nineteen, four turned, two going into a room on Thursday, one of them a boy who carries stock up from a yard at six — and tells him a man who cleans washrooms cannot do what comes next in a corridor on the second floor. **He has to do it where they can see him.**

### Ch 27 — The Thursday Line
He finds his own white tile in the third row from the top and nothing happens: two thousand people look at nine hundred names and none of them look at his, because a man going from four to five is ordinary in a city where people move every week. That is the frightening part — the machine has read him in public and has not made a fuss. On the branch floor the queue runs from seven and nobody has been told anything; that is the design. Four names unsettle: **Ottoline Barret, Emrys Vaz, Tobias Renn** and a fourth at eleven o'clock. Emrys was handed a plate that morning at the north gate for the first time in his life and it reads 441. Tobias's reading fails two hours after he has destroyed his own career. Slips print at four, a man collects them at half past four, and he gets ninety seconds a person. Runa tells him the standing and the cost: a referral is not a decision, it is a thing that has already happened. Dread, with a use in it: **one sentence**.

### Ch 28 — Public Record
"Lin Hao. Eight thousand one hundred and forty-one grains. Interval — unsettled." He walks out of the line instead of back down it and tells ninety-one colleagues, a clerk, a Field operative and a floor of witnesses that the unsettled reading is his, is on that board in that hand every Thursday, that anybody may come and look at the plate, and that he will stand in the Exchange box at nine every Thursday and read it out loud until it settles. Irreversible. Emrys follows him with his eyes and understands all of it. Ottoline Barret says one sentence — eleven years on that door, a different man held it before — and goes. A man who has worked the second column for twenty-two years says he has counted the words: eleven thousand a day. Runa puts the notepad away and tells him it is going in, word for word, filed on the 6th, and that he has not yet found out what a document about him does to the next four years. **Rhee closes the offer permanently through two inches of door** and buys a fortnight, and says it is the last one he will put his name to. The new board prints `UNSETTLED AT READING — CARRIED AND DECLARED`; five names struck through. The slips print at four. There were none.

### Ch 29 — Between Floors
He sits on the Vestry bench doing nothing for two hours, which is new, because there is nowhere else to go. Ola puts the forty-one thousand of the quarter on him. At twenty past nine Nadia Ferrous stops her own car between the arcade and the clinic and makes him get in. She has told sixty-one people by four o'clock and the whole Vestry by the weekend, and she knows exactly what she is, and she came to say one thing badly and says it: everything she feels is a floor, she has never once gone up, and on Thursday morning she felt him go up from the arcade with her hand on the brake. She spends **four seconds** on it, in a lift, in lift operator's boots, in the voice she uses for announcements, and she is not quiet once. Afterwards she carries the car **up one floor** — as far as she goes, at a price — so that it cannot be a thing that happened to her, and asks for her choosing of every part of it to be on the record. Then the doors open on the arcade and she tells nine people at the top of her lungs. She has read the notice: the re-read is quarterly, and a man who says a thing in a corridor is worth nothing, and a man who *keeps* saying it is worth something.

### Ch 30 — A Fifth Figure
Seven days later the Branch has opinions about him and no one has said any of them out loud; forty people watched him wait two hours, a dozen changed their good morning, and a woman from the second column leaves tea outside the washroom. The ninety-one are off the wall in a single line — *no further action this quarter* — and Emrys Vaz has the yard at six again. His tile is white with a black five, four figures under the name, and a **fifth figure that is not a rank**: 8,141, his exact grains, printed on a public tile for the first time in the Board's history, because a declared unsettled reading cannot be banded and the machine fell back on the raw number. Eight thousand one hundred and forty-one grains, and nineteen people inside it. Beside the Board, a Classification Wing sheet: accounts under continuing observation, register opened **nine years ago, thirty-one entries, entries are accounts and not persons**, listed by plate number with no names at all. Runa has never seen it. The oldest entry predates the printed column — opened by hand, in pencil — and is the only one of the thirty-one still running. Five years of nobody's book before anyone's book.
[Ledger. Nineteen lines. Five turned. The count has not moved since the 29th.]

### Ch 31 — Provisionally Six
The Exchange counter at nine on the 11th, and Garrick Neve, a Delta clerk, works out how to make a
declared unsettled reading official without a band existing behind it: a **provisional band struck by
hand from a figure**, which prints on the Board and leaves the figure where it is. He may not have six —
his figure is in the five band and the clerk has no authority to band him above the number in his hand.
The price is two things: the counter puts a question to him every week in public with the queue
listening, and the quarterly re-read gets a **printed date he does not control**. He takes it. The band
line prints **PROVISIONAL, STRUCK FROM FIGURE, FLOOR 8,141** with **TOLERANCE THREE GRAINS**, and he
works out in the rain that he can lose four grains and lose a rank. **Marguerite Vale** of the Vant
arrives in the Fourth Branch corridor at ten past one with a card, a printed address and a time, having
waited twenty-one days for the conversation.

### Ch 32 — The Vant's Price
Bellhouse, above a chandler's. Marguerite Vale is direct and enjoying herself. The offer: take the
**figure off the Board's arithmetic entirely** into a sealed line held in her personally, untouchable by
court, at one grain in four off the top for five years. She states the defect herself — a line held by a
person can be compelled out of the person — and says the third sheet names other citizens' balances, so
it is a road and he is to be the road. The favour: she wants to be the broker who found it before her
chair turns over. **Solveig Rask's real books** are on the table, unwritten spine, and a column of
**thirty-one plate numbers over nine years, his own at the bottom, entered on the 3rd**, and a valuation
that requires knowing the rate since the 1st of June — known to two people, one of them him. He asks
for the **date, the room and the time of the offer in writing** instead of the terms, and says he will
ask his question at the counter on Thursday.

### Ch 33 — Solveig's Arithmetic
He refuses standing up, and says why: nineteen hands and a number attached to each, a band that is the
only instrument that has ever been on his side, and a cupboard where the moment after he stops being able
to reach anybody he will have to choose between a figure and a person. The refusal is written down with
REFUSED and initialled. He reads Rask's four lines under the valuation — movement continuous, source
count not established, settlement risk nil assumed, **first entry standing alone — 400** — which is
accurate about a woman at an interchange putting a plate down and picking it up, four months before he
aimed anything. Runa Sato at the rail: the Field file served on the 6th has **no answer and no sponsor**,
and an unanswered file is read as agreement and gets a **mandate** — execution on the officer's own hand,
no countersignature. Ends with a man with a clipboard writing **SUPPLY — UNSETTLED — SUBJECT PRESENT**
in pencil in the Fourth Branch's own corridor, four words, and Emrys Vaz reading it as he passes.

### Ch 34 — Cordwain
He walks the arc on purpose on the 15th and takes **twenty-two new faces in one day**, because he has
nineteen people and no way to get a twentieth. The figure keeps moving. The faces come back. **Forty-one.**
Nell Ardery, a machinist on the landing of number 14, has eleven weeks of figures and a band that has not
moved; she names **Cutler's Row**, thirty-one dwellings, **2,140 grains over eleven weeks**, taken by a
district return to reconcile a shortfall of about 300 a week against 9,000 people, initials **ADJ-DT**.
On the 17th the requisition comes off the board: **thirty-one dwellings, mandate, no countersignature,
execution the 19th at six, two names — SATO, R. FIELD, and LIN HAO — SUBJECT, ACCOUNT CARRIED, PRESENT
BY ATTESTATION 3RD.** Runa says she looked for the ground and did not write it, and that a requisitioned
address is not adjusted. He goes down at four. The back stair fire door has been **forced twice with a
bar**, by somebody on the 11th and by the warders at three that afternoon, and a woman in the row has
chained it twice and told nobody.

### Ch 35 — A Clean Report
A Wing clerk brings the quarterly re-read **eleven days early**, the 18th, and the re-read is deferred to
the 29th by the provisional band with a receipt. Runa's Cordwain report goes in at two and comes back
stamped and **entirely accurate and not about him**; in the margin she records that the premises were
executed against because an address on the observed arc required a subject, and that this is a defect in
the method. A supervisor countersigned it **noted**. She tells him the margin is her, buying room. The
order is served on the Fourth Branch at twenty past six for the employer attestation, and the attestation
box is signed by **Tobias Renn**, who is off the register and was found because he knows where the 3rd is.
He wants it written that it is not a favour, in eight seconds, with a courier's receipt downstairs with his
name in it.

### Ch 36 — Requisitioned Rooms
**Scene.** The execution happens at ten past. Eleven warders, eleven minutes, a district clerk reading a
number that a tenant says is not the address. The back stair goes at twenty past and Runa Sato is under
it; she carries weight and the stair did not move. He had his forearm in to the wrist and had Halvard
Rees by the collar; the warders' tally has him out of the passage at six with no time back in. In the
flat at number 6 she tells him what the falsification costs her and writes it anyway — **failure
unforecast, officer alone in the passage, her hand twice on the form**, false in four places including him.
The scene is adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, one arm each, and four true statements first; she
puts her weight on him and says so, and the chapter says the weight is the only part of herself she has
handed to anybody, and that the injury constrains them without causing anything. The whole day is at
**number 6** — she is moved up one floor at four o'clock when the warders want the ground floor back, and
the report is written there at nine. He decides, alone on a landing at seven in the morning, that there is
not another side: he writes a four-page statement putting his own name under the stair, the forced door,
the book, and every dwelling that comes off that row.

### Ch 37 — The Provisional Band
He writes and files the statement at twenty to six on the 19th. At the Exchange counter Garrick Neve puts
the **first of the two question families** — *where does the movement come from* — and he answers in front
of forty people: *a man who cleans washrooms is standing in a place where strangers hold doors for each
other four times a day. I did not do any of it. I stood in it.* The Board prints **PROVISIONAL SIX.
STRUCK ON A REQUISITION OF PROPERTY. CORDWAIN. 19TH.** with floor 8,141 and tolerance three. **He was not
six because he earned six** — the machine found a rank-six act and attached it to the only account in
Ostara with a declared reading on it. Rank six carries warrants, requisition of property and **compelled
testimony**, and the correct use of it would be against him. He tries to petition Lin Wei's file and
cannot: four thousand one hundred and twelve folios, and three grains.
[Provisional band struck. Six, pending quarterly re-read. Floor 8,141, tolerance three grains.]

### Ch 38 — Five Cold Minutes
He goes and finds all five turned lines in one night, and finds Halvard Rees first because he is the
control — the row emptied at ten past six and he is sitting on a wall in twenty degrees, and the climb was
done forty times and **not one of them came off him**. **Nadia** will not be warned: *do not spend me, I am
the one thing in this quarter that goes up and I am not going to be turned into a room.* **Ola** has left
twenty-two new entries and one underlined line — *the Vestry, week beginning the 18th. Nobody has asked me
to do this* — and he has made a clerk. **Teodor** will not come out of the counting house. **Tessa** is in
the cold store with the stippling above her left elbow and pale blue where it crossed, six episodes since
the third, none of them hers, a **twelfth clipboard** dated today, and one request: once a month, say the
number out loud to her. She will not thank him and says so first. **Emrys** has 441 and can only go down
and has told nobody. The cold minute goes out across an open door to **Ines**, and it is the third since
June, which is the line at which the Bureau stops believing coincidence. Her month on the rota name is up.
She puts it on the table: **Emrys Vaz, yard rota, six in the morning**, and she will write it tonight if he
says the word, and she will not say it first.

### Ch 39 — What the Board Does Not Say
Ines counts what the register is not. Nine years, about eleven unsettled readings a year, nine of the
eleven to her — **a hundred against thirty-one**, so the register is a catch and not a census, and it
records what this city *catches*: the ones entered are the ones somebody looked at, and the 31st is his and
is dated the 3rd, the day he stood up. In Runa's cupboard: the Field and the Wing do not share systems, so
the register was never withheld from anyone. Runa wants the declaration **written down and dated** with the
five names on it — a cheap weapon, and she cannot write the clean version herself. Ines blocks it and turns
on Lin Hao, and there is the four seconds where the negotiation belongs to him. He refuses Runa's document
and dictates his own: five names struck by declaration and not by evidence, **Emrys Vaz is not settled, his
plate reads 441, I knew it on the day and I said nothing.** It goes into the front of Runa's file. It puts
five names back on the branch board and the division will ask for the audit, and they will be right to.
Ines's price is not a speech — she carries a name up a corridor because a drawer is not a place to keep a
person.

### Ch 40 — The Winter Queue
The branch board has five names on it again with a gap where the reason should be, and **Tobias Renn** is
the third one down. The Exchange publishes the **Winter Review candidates: rank six**, twenty-two names,
ordered by published standing, and he is the last, and the word **PROVISIONAL** on his line is the only
reason he is on the sheet. Two numbers, and they do not mean the same thing: **three grains is how close
he is to losing it; 11,859 is how far he is from having it** (band six opens at 20,000, published standing
8,141, a witnessed deed scores 400, so twenty-nine deeds in six weeks, and he has nineteen people and no
twentieth). The sheet's small print, up since the 24th: **a provisional band is not carried into review.**
Runa: the document he filed on the 23rd is why a Reading Office query is open on his standing, and a query
wants an instrument he has not got. The clerk who entered his reading on the 3rd says she cannot invent him
one. Ends on the corridor board: **EMRYS VAZ, four hundred and forty-one, the next reading the 29th.**
[Winter Review queue published. Provisional band is not carried into review. Band six opens at 20,000. Published standing 8,141.]

### Ch 41 — Zero
26th. Published queue last of twenty-two, provisional the only reason he is on it. Yard gate shows no twentieth person; Vestry shows the five turned are the only named hands. Zero to spend. Ends on the Board repainting Thursday with the only instrument he holds being his own declaration.

### Ch 42 — What a Sealed File Asks
**Thursday the 22nd, four days behind Ch 41.** Box, first standing. Weekly question from the index: what
instrument. Answer: requisition of the 19th and declaration of the 23rd, no other. Third question new: what
a sealed file asks — rank held, deposit, form with folio count. Neve: the cheaper price is standing here,
the dearer one is the date at the top of the band line, seven days. Query owned by the clerk of the 3rd,
who cannot invent one; Runa makes him swear to stand the box every Thursday until the Review. Ends wanting
one true paper with another hand on it.

### Ch 43 — The Longest Walk
28th. Walks Branch to Cordwain and back looking for a twentieth face. Crossing full of the forty-one. Nell
Ardery confirms no new face on the arc; Halvard confirms not one of forty came off him. List written:
forty-one lines, five turned. Ends with grief for the trap: the only people he can relieve are the people
relief would mark.

### Ch 44 — An Empty Account
29th, Thursday. Box second standing, then re-read: 8,140, one down, one inside tolerance, band holds,
two grains of margin left. Query separate and still open; the Review will ask for an instrument in the
sixth week. Beatrix: account empty in the one respect that matters. Ines: paper discharged, rota name said,
word ungiven, May clipboard still unasked. Nadia: do not spend me. Ends with three stamped slips, none an
instrument.

### Ch 45 — Nine Seats
First Thursday after the re-read. Box, third standing: 8,139, two down, band holds. Marguerite terms
return: same line, one in four for five years, no road, plus **a date a week ahead**, a room in Bellhouse,
and nine seats with the house in the public seats. Runa: not an instrument. Ines: an evening is not an
instrument. He goes for the worse reason — leverage, not money.

### Ch 46 — The Best Rooms in Ostara
Scene. Bellhouse top rooms. Terms read aloud. No road. Evening asked for herself, answered yes before
anything starts. Scene on the page, unhurried, mutual, his tender arm respected. Afterwards he refuses the
line again for appetite rather than principle. She keeps the terms down, signed or unsigned, and will sit
the Review.

### Ch 47 — The Review
Thursday in the sixth week. Box fourth standing first: **8,138, three down, three inside tolerance**, band
holds through the day on the edge of it. Review room nine chairs, twenty-two called nine by nine; four
grains down lapses a man who held five for six years, which is the proof of the three-grain tolerance.
Query puts him in the room — Runa says so plainly: *without the query you would not be in it at all.*
Panel: provisional not carried, standing is a five. Ines puts the May clipboard on the table in the
corridor ("I have the date and the figure and I have not asked") and **neither of them touches it**.
Open-question slot offered — one question put on the floor, public, no notice. He takes it.

### Ch 48 — One Grain of Itself
Takes the slot. Question: what is the figure made of. **He spends the account on himself, once, first time
in the book**, and the answer is true: doors, trays, basket 400, door 200, aggregate 3,529, shifts wherever
he stands — *I did none of it, I stood in it.* The machine reads the figure against the **band line**
instead of the floor. Panel: **a candidate is seated on published standing unless the band was struck on an
instrument** — the requisition of the 19th is on the table, and a true answer under the open-question rule is
a finding, and a finding is an instrument. **Rank six, held, not provisional, seated on an instrument of that
room. No grain was scored and the plate still reads 8,138.** Cost, all four on the page: a line reading
himself, three new turned (Nell Ardery, Garrick Neve, yard checker), the cab promise to Beatrix broken and
unmendable, and the option of being invisible closed forever. **Ninety-one lines, eight turned.**
[Ledger. Ninety-one lines. Eight turned. One line reads himself.]
Ola reads the finding aloud to twenty people from the landing rail. Tessa charts all three new colds herself
and sits looking at her own arms. Ines stands in the back row with her face in her hands.

### Ch 49 — Four Thousand One Hundred and Twelve
Rank six held; the corridor board's five names gone **by instrument instead of by declaration**. Finding
carried to the Reading Office window. **Query closed — a witnessed answer in a room is an instrument.**
Deposits the form with the folio count (4,112) as the deposit for a sealed file. Index promises a trolley
by ten. Beatrix names the broken cab promise without heat and says the price of it is his to carry, not
hers to forgive in a speech. Nadia says do not spend her and rings the bell up one floor. **Ines: "I am not
filing it. I cannot decide whether to, which is not the same as deciding not to."** The rota word remains
ungiven by decision. Ends wanting one folio by ten.

### Ch 50 — Held Account One
Side room ten o'clock. Trolley, guard, seals checked twice, docket opened folio by folio, copy made at the
side press and logged in the docket book with the date and his band number. **4,112 folios, 4,111 sealed,
one open at the back by Wing order, never called.** Open folio: transfer receipt in Lin Wei's hand,
214,000 grains on the eleventh three years ago into an account the index does not contain, signed Lin Wei;
under it in Wing print: the file of the receiver is Held Account One. **There is no Held Account Zero** —
the clerk asked the index that morning and got the answer the same day. He reads both lines three times
standing up with his hands flat on either side of the paper because if he touched it he would crease it.
Copy slip pocketed with four counterfoils. The clerk who entered his reading on the 3rd closed her query
on a finding rather than a lapse.
Ines in the corridor, **an auditor may stand in a corridor and no further**, the whole hour, folder closed.
She puts it in one sentence: *he said the number was wrong in public and what he did was give it away where
no ledger could follow it. And you are the address.* Then the fact that no Bureau has a shape for: *I do not
know what a Bureau does with a line it has never had to read. Nobody does.*
Final image: he walks the long road home, forty-one minutes, and cleans the last row of basins with the
filed petition drying beside the soap, **turning it face down halfway through because a washroom counter is
no place to leave a brother's hand uncovered.** Runa closes the Field file. Marguerite writes: the terms
stay down. The Board is nine streets away with his name on a tile for the first time since he was a child
and he does not go and look at it. **Three people felt something cold that night and none of them knew
why** — Nell Ardery, Garrick Neve, the checker. Ola copies the finding under the re-read; Beatrix stands in
front of the rail without speaking; Tessa at seventeen boards, eighteen before nightfall; Nadia holds her
car at the terminus past midnight with nobody telling her to.
**Nobody has thanked him.**

---

## Volume 1 closed

Audited against the page on the volume close. **Full working, with commands and chapter-and-line citations
for every claim, is in `reviews/volume-01/volume-close.md`.** The short form, for a writer picking this
file up cold:

- **He holds rank six and he is 11,862 grains under his own band.** 20,000 − 8,138. The band came off the
  requisition of the 19th plus the open-question finding, **not off merit**, and nothing the account did was
  converted into a grain. **B26 is open and is the volume's live consequence.**
- **The mechanism changed mid-flight and the change was correct.** Thirty witnessed deeds in six weeks
  needs thirty witnesses; he has nineteen people and no twentieth, because the only people he can reach are
  the people relief would mark. He was seated rather than promoted, and that is why the gap did not close.
- **Nothing was closed by the volume ending.** Four threads paid (B9, B19, B24, B25), one superseded, five
  advanced, **twenty-six live.**
- **T1 advanced by exactly one folio and must advance no further.**
- **Ten residual ages are announced across nine chapters of Volume 1, and five of them are Lin Hao or a
  core partner stating a canon-correct age.** One instance, `chapter-0020.md:67`, was missed by every gate
  until the `volume-plan` review, and `chapter-0002.md:21` is contested rather than correct. Registered in
  `state/open-threads.md`.
- **Chapters 42–49 all close on the same construction** and it is the volume's outstanding prose debt. Test
  for it with a *contains* check on `He wanted, concretely` **with no trailing comma** — with the comma it
  matches seven and drops Ch 42. See `state/current.md` item 35.
- **Volume 1's six scenes ran in six different emotional directions.** Volume 2's five must not repeat a
  shape Volume 1 spent.

## Volume 3 — The Reading

### Ch 101 — The Notice On The Board
The 1st of the new month. A shift with no questions asked, which is worse than hostility. Drant's month-end return, dated the thirty-first of last month and a day late because the desk was discharged on the 18th. A Correction Office hearing notice, served personally: one numbered paragraph asking whether an account may remain undescribed. Fifty-nine days to the 29th of next month — fifty-seven from the 3rd, fifty-eight from the 2nd. He asks whether the date is a Thursday and is told nobody prints the year on a notice. Published on the district board on the 3rd; cannot be withdrawn by the person it names.

### Ch 102 — A Reason To Be Measured
The 2nd, Lantern Row. Ines reads the notice in ninety seconds: the answer is legally no, because an undescribed account cannot be administered. The notice publishes on the 3rd and the only door shuts with it. A member's form is coming that requires a reason in his own words, and every possible answer is a description. She is kind and completely unhelpful: everything that happens next happens in front of people. He has been invisible for a fortnight — the standing and the floor were discharged on the 18th of last month.

### Ch 103 — The Man From Cobb Street
The 3rd, morning, the Cobb Street hall. Yusuf Adeyemi, eleven years running hearings, honest about being hated for it: a hearing is not a trial, the member may speak and bring one witness, the Office must put its case in full and may bring an instrument. As a courtesy he hands over the trap without appearing to know it: the instrument has never worked in an empty room, the Office expects a full hall, a hearing is public, and he has no power to keep anyone out and would not if he had. Fifty-seven days of notice, and the notice was drafted on the 19th of last month, the better part of a fortnight before service, for a reason that was not him. Lin Hao will walk in to refuse it and will bring no witness.

### Ch 104 — The Form Requires A Reason
The 3rd, afternoon, the Vestry. The member's form arrives: one paragraph, in his own hand, giving the reason the account should remain undescribed. He reads it twice and understands the trap is the form. On the blank strip he writes that a reason will be given in the hall, in person, on the day, and not on paper. Tessa's cost, told to him second-hand: the clinic's summary line went back empty a second time, a lapse and not a refusal, and a service failing to reply twice is about to become a procedure with a man in it.

### Ch 105 — What The Hall Is For
The 4th, Thursday, the Board repaints at nine. His tile is gone — a pale rectangle the next repaint, on the 11th, will cover — and the strip stopped on the 28th, six days before the notice went up on the frame, and this is the tenth Thursday the figure stood still. Four months of public mismeasurement ends in public unmeasurement, and the cost is that nobody has to care about him. The only road back onto the wall is the thing he refused twice: a public measurement. In three days a paper goes to every Correction desk, and one desk must decide what goes on the roll.

### Ch 106 — The Best Use Of A Fact
The 5th, Bellhouse. Marguerite asks leave to speak for him in three rooms using the house's name: a man about to be described in public cannot be seized by a court, and she wants to price that. Refused at the table, and she will do it anyway. Explicit scene, adults, four terms stated, no draw spent, her terms down and unsigned and untouched. Afterwards the visitors' book: an entry dated the 3rd, no name, four words — *Ask him what it is.*

### Ch 107 — The Chairs They Put Out
The 6th, the Reading's third floor. Eight chairs in two rows and a ninth square to a door, which Sabine Rooke moved there herself; the room shut since the 26th, eleven days. Sabine moved the ninth chair square to the door on the 1st and squared the eight on the 3rd, the day after eight men carried them up the outside stair. It is not a hearing room but the room the parties wait in while the hall is cleared, and the Office has decided to put the exhibit roll in it and him with it. The roll has nine items. A queue of eleven people in forty minutes goes into his book with the cause named.

### Ch 108 — The List Without Names
The 7th. Tove Marlin, who produced the roll, reads it exactly as written and apologises for the room. Nine documents, no names: the Field minute with Runa's household line, the visitors' book, the clinic's four lines, the household record described and not named, the lift log's forty-one names, a district table with no clerk's name, the discharged standing, his own refusal, and a ruled empty sixth space. Five days to acknowledge or object: the twelfth is the last day a registrar will take anything, and the roll goes down to him on the morning of the 9th and comes back stamped the same day.

### Ch 109 — What He Signs Instead
The 8th, six women and the notebook with nineteen days of lines. Nadia wants outright refusal; the roll goes in either way. He signs the acknowledgement with a written objection attached: item four, the household record, excluded from being read aloud, and lodged three days before the deadline — a record that is not his, never signed by him, described in his own hand to a registrar. Ines refuses the second document that would reconcile it. Tove reads the objection back exactly as written. It worked, and six women let him do it.

### Ch 110 — What Comes Back Signed
The 9th. The objection went down to a registrar at half past six that morning and the registrar's four lines came back stamped and did not decide anything: item four excluded, and described — six lines, one line per woman per day, from the 21st, signature refused. The roll itself is not lodged until ten past three that afternoon; lodging and answering are two different things. He traded a name for a description and got the worse half. The classification paper is still unsigned. The roll closes, the hearing stands for the printed day, and the Office sends what it intends to put before the hall. The first item is not a document about him. It is a description.

### Ch 111 — The Roll They Are Building
The 11th, Thursday, the Board repaints and paints over his rectangle with a stranger's tile. On the Reading's third floor the proved roll sits in nine grey boxes on the eight-foot table, the fourth shut and wearing his objection on a string. Proving means what he signed on the 8th made of the other eight: every one will be read aloud on the 29th. Forty-nine days.

### Ch 112 — The First Exhibit
The 12th, the Field yard. He helps Runa carry a press bed to the far store before a four o'clock inspection — iron, brick, no draw, nothing owed that a hand could carry. In the clerk's room she attests the hall's copy of the minute as true in three words and leaves the cover note unsigned; the cover comes back as a dated demand and her hearing firms. Her cost advances and does not resolve. Forty-eight days.

### Ch 113 — What The Visitors' Book Shows
The 13th, Bellhouse. Marguerite's weekly reading: five more entries since the 7th, ninety-four in all, thirty-one asking about a person and fourteen about the household — the Office's sheet still says twenty-nine and thirteen and will say it on the 29th. One entry in the porter's hand names Nell Ardery as holding one buff square — the first square named, by somebody else's hand, on the page. Six women argue in their own words; nobody leaves. On the landing Sun states her refusal in her own words on her own ground: the house protects by writing down together, and she will not be kept. The logic takes the hit without breaking. Forty-seven days.

### Ch 114 — Down And Unsigned
The 14th. Two papers stay down: the classification paper, unsigned, carried up three floors and back, and Marguerite's terms, unsigned, out of force, which she refuses to take up even when he asks and Nadia offers her own name instead. An office argues about objects; nothing happens. He learns nobody's hand is coming down for him. Forty-six days.

### Ch 115 — The Counter-Forensics Desk
The 15th, the instrument certification desk. Adeyemi's standing rule: an instrument offered in evidence must be shown capable of producing what its proponent says it produces. The paper does not exist yet; it wants the desk's run and the builder's word. The reporting officer is named: Ivo Sarn, nine years at the desk, the hand three floors above Anneke Roos. He decides whatever the tool does happens in the hall or not at all. Forty-five days.

### Ch 116 — A Tool With Two Columns
The 16th. The certification request goes by hand, and he reads it at ten: two paragraphs, a slip describing the tool flatly as two columns on paper output, subject and counterparty. The second paragraph names the builder for the first time: Wren Kallo, outside the standing roll, seven years on the second bench. He tells Marguerite alone; she will hold the name till morning. Forty-four days.

### Ch 117 — The Officer Outside The Roll
The 17th. The confirmation is back: she built it, in her hand, matching the slip — and the envelope carries three extra initials, because she told the runner, the plate clerk, and Cressida before ten. A bench order dated yesterday moves her to paper review effective the 19th. He sends a note with no envelope: an hour in the morning, at the second bench. Forty-three days.

### Ch 118 — What She Cannot Keep
The 18th, Thursday. Wren Kallo's scene, immovable, written rather than summarised (3,476 words). She cannot keep a secret; she asks to be made a party to the proceeding she is losing her post over, as his one witness. He decides out loud, in the room, knowing she will repeat it, and she repeats it back word for word — **once**. After Cressida's knock she will not repeat it again; she writes it on a bench paper, hands it across, and tells him to keep it until the twenty-ninth. Adults, awake, door locked by her own hand, no draw spent, nothing moved. Cressida knocks at eleven and Wren tells her with him still there. Forty-two days, six weeks.

### Ch 119 — Six Names In A Minute
The 19th. The hall's copy came back with a dated demand: attest as a whole by the 26th, complete to the household's true number, or answer for the gap. **One 26th, one demand** — the cover note at 112 printed no date, and signing it means the seventh name. The roll's arithmetic already shows it: six names where the file says more, and the seventh line is Sun. He refuses in Tove's day book to complete another officer's minute; the demand goes to Runa instead, and her hearing firms. Sun stays off paper. Forty-one days.

### Ch 120 — The Hearing In Writing
The 20th. The Office's written case goes on the table, opening with a description, and his one witness stands named on the hearing officer's form. The certification paper says the tool meets its specification. In the register room Anneke Roos enters Sarn's paragraph into the ruled sixth space as received, not agreed: a settled mass in the possession of a member, accumulating by the member's own condition, no source identified, no measure obtainable. The record is complete; what the instrument can actually do is still unanswered. Forty days.

### Ch 121 — What Adeyemi Is For
The 21st. The hearing officer comes to the Fourth Branch and stands at the top of the stair serving the discharged fourth floor for nine minutes, and Drant enters the visit in the thin book and then declines to write a reason. Adeyemi states what certification means for the hall: the Office must put its case in full; an instrument is not evidence until a person says in the room, in words, what it does; **the person who must say it is the proponent, Ivo Sarn, and if he cannot the instrument does not go on the table.** His own rule requires the desk to show capability on paper before the day, and the desk's answer is whatever the desk has. He gives the deadline — the 25th — and he tells him plainly that he does not enjoy the work and will not stop it. He wants to make Sarn say it; he has been handed a door inside the room. Thirty-nine days.

### Ch 122 — The Chair She Never Took
The 22nd. A woman with a card asks Sabine Rooke a third time, and the third question is not about a Thursday but about whether the room was always arranged for nine, and the answer makes a file. She comes and finds him first, as she said she would, and she asks him for the one thing she has never asked for: the ninth chair, in that room, on the morning. He cannot arrange it — a porter's book on a floor with no standing on it would carry his name — so he carries the chair up four flights himself at eight in the morning and a porter writes it in. She signs for nine chairs at seven. Her price is named by her before she asks: her name on a sheet about him, which makes the drawer of eleven years of her own paper a different question. B99 advanced. Thirty-eight days.

### Ch 123 — The Corridor With No Handle
The 23rd, half past four, because that is the hour the trays come down. He goes down the Wing stair for the first time past the third step. **The corridor is empty of chairs**: bare board, the marks of eight legs in two rows of four, and four feet out from the wall the single mark of the chair that stood square to a door. The eight left on the 2nd, the ninth on the 27th of last month, and the list of nine has had one unfilled line since the 26th, so a room with a gap in its count is an unfinished room and he has been treating it as an occupied one for six weeks. The drawer that was open on the eighth has been re-fitted so its front lines up with the others. Four blank plates and a door with no handle on this side, and a filled rectangle of paint at handle height where one was taken off and painted over years ago. The door is not locked and swings on his hand. He stands in the doorway and does not go in. Thirty-seven days.

### Ch 124 — The Cloth On The Table
He goes in. Eleven feet by nine, warm, swept since the weekend. Under the grey cloth: about fifty blank forms, six headings ruled, a sixth space ruled and empty on every sheet — the Wing's own paperwork, in the warm, in his own building, with no name on any of them. On nine of them, evenly spaced down the stack, a sixth space full of pencil scoring rubbed out with an eraser: nine sentences somebody started and decided were wrong, over years. The chair is pulled out at the angle of a man who stood up in the middle of something. He puts the cloth back the way he found it and runs the stair as four trays come up. Drant writes nothing. He does not tell the household. Thirty-six days.

### Ch 125 — The Old Run In The File
The 25th, the batch's Thursday; the Board repaints and he is not in the square. The Certification desk's answer is its own best work: a certified run of the ninth, four years back, on an unnamed subject. Subject column, a plate number with the name line burnished over. Counterparty column, six names, printed flat. **The run cannot be repeated**, because the premises closed and the subject is not available, and that is what makes it paper. The clerk admits it has been carried into four quarterlies under the same six names. Lin Hao sees a city that has spent four years certifying a column and never once certifying a person. Tove arrives at the end with the file he did not ask for. Thirty-five days.

### Ch 126 — Two Columns, One Head
The 26th. The subject's case file of the same date: **twenty-one recorded movements**, under the same heading as the run's six. Fifteen. The margin on the certified run reads *instrument out of tolerance*, dated the ninth, and it has been carried forward four times — a district summary, two quarterlies, and a closing index card — and never reconciled, because each office was waiting for the next reading and not for the last one's arithmetic. Tove gives him his one written question and a deadline of the 27th. Then the Field yard, and **Runa's refusal**: she will attest to six lines and refuses to attest the minute as a whole, in her own hand, for her own reasons, because the seventh line is a woman on a landing who has never been asked. A second written disobedience: her hearing firms from a month into a date. Thirty-four days.

### Ch 127 — What The Instrument Cannot See
The 27th, ten o'clock, a room four feet by nine off the certification floor with a clerk in it. **The rule is stated once, from the tool's own specification, clause nine**: the counterparty column is filled at the moment of contact between the observed subject and a person, in the room, while the tool is running, and the tool reads nothing else; clauses four to eight say it stores nothing between runs. Six on one sheet, twenty-one on the other, fifteen. **Then the irreversible decision (option A): he asks for the run, the file and the margin to be entered against his own file**, so that he can never again call the instrument unreliable, knowing it goes to the hall and cannot be withdrawn — and the cost is six strangers' names, one of them Marta Vey four streets from Ferrier Street, and Wren Kallo must stand behind a run she did not make. Thirty-three days.

### Ch 128 — Nine Weeks To The Hall
The 28th. The threat's new species, carried in the body: he takes his hand back from four inches of a woman's plate on the second-floor sill and says the sill is wet. A slip by hand from Wren Kallo — the Office asked the builder to answer for the run; she has; and she cannot keep count of the copies. At the table, **Nadia Ferrous works out that clause nine does not say who starts it**: the Office need only leave a man who has touched people for four years in a room with a hundred and forty strangers for an hour. Beatrix Halm's condition — spend on people, not on himself — is dead, and she says so. In the rain at the gate, a woman says good evening to a man with his hands in his pockets. Thirty-two days.

### Ch 129 — The Debt She Cannot Hand Back
The 29th, the Vestry, a runner sent to a washroom attendant for the first time in four months. **She asks to be released from the clinical debt** and he cannot release her, because a debt is paid and the only thing he has to pay with is the account, and paying her would move on the only person in the city whose body cannot be examined for it. She named the cost before she asked. Then she says the other half out loud, and what happens is not the debt: adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, nothing spent, and afterwards both of them know out loud that she is the one person owed who cannot be paid. The first of next month is in three days. Thirty-one days.

### Ch 130 — The Only Movement Left
The 30th. The blank sheet: what the tool prints on a person who touches nobody is the head, two empty columns, a ruled line for a finding, and a signature space. The instrument is withdrawn; the roll is read anyway; the notice does not close. **An empty reading closes nothing, and the Office knows that without knowing why** — a procedure that comes out with nothing in it has to be done again. The only movement left in the room is himself, and he will not spend it and will not find out why by practising on anybody. The five days after the thirtieth go by with nothing in his hands. At the table he manages four minutes of silence, and Marguerite stops him before the price is quoted. Thirty days.

### Ch 131 — What The Women Object To
The 5th. He says it at the table, badly, three times, and the argument is about him and not about the Office. His plan is to refuse ninety chances and a reaching hand is not a decision. **Nadia Ferrous refuses being the reason:** she has been refused things for four months and will not be the last one. **Beatrix Halm says what replaces her dead condition** — she will come to the rail, she will not be asked, and being at the rail would make him reachable through her. **Ines Duarte states the thing the argument has been circling:** a procedure is not a decision, and a decision is that somebody else holds the hours. Sun advances from the landing and does not come down. At the branch, a clerk asks him for a date and he says twenty-four days off — the first time he has had an answer ready for anybody. Twenty-four days.

### Ch 132 — The Household Arithmetic
The 6th. **Marguerite Vale prices the household**, by her own method, and the price is negative: seven women in that room is a negative value, six is worse, and a house that stays at home is worth what it has always been worth. A book cannot keep anybody. **Ines Duarte corrects her own method** — the true version is seven women who have every reason to reach for him standing in a room where reaching is the only thing provable against him — **and states the batch's structural decision: nobody is at the rail on the 29th.** The price is not presented until the 14th. The visitors' book is read aloud: a hundred and six live, thirty-eight asking about a person, sixteen about the household, against the Office's eighty-nine, twenty-nine and thirteen. Sun's line from the landing: the house is worth what the hall can make of it and not one person at that table gets to decide. Twenty-three days.

### Ch 133 — A List Of Acts
The 7th, from half past four in the morning. **He begins a second book, at the table and not in the drawer, and writes four true acts before seven o'clock** — a tap, a man's box, a woman on a step, a boy who worked out the socket. Then a fifth at a sink: the hand taken back from four inches of a plate, and the sill was wet, it was wet every morning. **The finding is structural: he can produce a hundred and twelve faces and cannot tell anyone what any of them is for.** Ines separates the four on the page — two witnessed and scored into an aggregate, the tap nothing at all, **and the woman on the step the only one never in any document in this city, whose name he does not know.** Ola copies it. The sheet goes on a shelf above the sink and stays there for the rest of the batch. Twenty-two days.

### Ch 134 — The Witness Problem
The 8th, a Thursday, the Board repaints and he does not go to the square. **Wren Kallo has been on paper review since the 19th of last month and the machine is still on her bench.** The problem is that the certified run of four years back is on his file at his own request, so on the 29th she must speak to a run she did not make, and she has three answers and two are fatal. **He tells her his intention. She repeats it back once, then writes it on bench paper, dates it, signs it and puts it through Cressida into the file** — because the alternative is a rumour, and a rumour about a member on the 29th costs somebody their post. **A clerk writes four lines against it: a member who states an intention in advance has had the opportunity to prepare the room. It is a lie, it is in the file with a number, and it cannot be withdrawn.** She refuses to sign a correction and gives him the refusal in her own hand instead. Twenty-one days.

### Ch 135 — What Ola Keeps
The 9th. **Ola Bright has been copying everything for six months: three hundred and eleven sentences, eleven of them hers, and not one person has ever asked her for a page.** He reads it back and understands that his household has been running an unasked record of itself inside a house he believed kept no registers. **He asks her to hold the four hours. She says yes in about four seconds, writes them in her own hand, and does not give the page back** — it comes back on the 14th or not at all, and she makes him stand in front of the six and tell them. She has also copied the four acts onto the inside cover of the soft-backed book, dated separately, behind two hundred pages of nothing. **It is the first record of him he has ever let another person hold, and the reason is the same reason as Wren Kallo's, and the effect is the opposite: Wren cannot keep a secret and Ola cannot keep a silence.** Twenty days.

### Ch 136 — The Lift Log Comes Up Again
The 10th. The dry reading of the roll in the parties' room with a clerk in it. **Tove Marlin reads the forty-one names out loud — seven minutes, four hundred and twenty seconds — apologises for the room first, and then reads them anyway**, and she says why: an apology afterwards is about the names, an apology first is about the room. **The four district services are bracketed and the Office's letter takes eleven days.** At the sill at two o'clock, Nadia Ferrous tells him the consequence has arrived: the service was asked to identify the operator, she was handed the letter at a change of shift with nine other people in the cab, and she answered in her own hand in front of all of them rather than let the only copy live in a clerk's drawer in Cobb Street. **She says the price she was right about a month ago has come due, and that he is not to make it better at the branch.** Nineteen days.

### Ch 137 — What Runa Is Not Allowed
The 11th. Item one read in the Cobb Street hall with a clerk in it, and the hall is empty. **Runa Sato is in the corridor and has been since twenty to nine, because a serving officer present during the reading of her own minute would be a party conversing with the clerk.** She is not allowed three things: to be in the room, to object, and to be told — **and the third of them is him, and she says she has noticed that he has not.** Her hearing is the fortnight after his and the date is not printed. Tove Marlin says she is sorry about the room and then reads six lines at a quarter speed and stands in four seconds of silence where a seventh name would have been, and apologises for nothing. **He learns the price of six:** on the 29th the room will hear the gap, nobody will know why, and he will know why and cannot say a word, because saying it discloses a Field demand that is not his. Eighteen days.

### Ch 138 — September Is Still Beatrix's
The 12th, half past eleven at night, in the rain, at the yards. **She tells him what the ninth of September is** — a request made twice a year for a year of her handwriting on a map — **and then gives him the whole book, five years of it, with no condition on it**, because the condition died on the 28th of last month and a thing with no condition is a thing he has to ask for. **The price to her: on the ninth she will have to say it is gone, to a man with a form, in a way that makes her look careless instead of principled.** The broken price from the square in the winter is named out loud for the first time in four months: unpaid, uncollected, unforgiven, and she was in that square and did not open her mouth. She tells him he will use it as a book of names, because that is what he is frightened of. Seventeen days.

### Ch 139 — The Price Of A Public Good Deed
The 13th. He calculated on the Sunday: a man who intends to announce four hours of not touching anybody has to be a man who has been seen not touching anybody. **He carries a bundle up a flight of stairs for a woman outside the Fourth Branch in front of eleven people, on purpose.** Anneke Roos writes it in the branch register without asking why. **A witnessed deed is a recorded deed and nothing came: the grey is at his left temple a second and a half later and he does not sleep.** By that evening there are nine new faces at that counter. **At the table he names the price himself, because he worked it out on the Sunday and will not let anybody else say it first — and it is Nadia Ferrous's forty-one names, read to two hundred and eleven people instead of thirty, with no line in any document saying she chose it.** Ines says the reason he did it is the worst possible reason and also the first true sentence anybody has said about the plan, and reminds him that Marguerite priced the house on the sixth and told him on the fourth that the price would come on the fourteenth. Sixteen days, and the plate printed once: 8,138 against 8,141, three grains, 11,862 short.

### Ch 140 — The Woman Who Will Read It
The 14th. **Tove Marlin reads the whole roll in the Cobb Street hall to nineteen people, and every one of them is there because she asked them**, on the grounds that the member is entitled to be present and so is anybody the reading is about. She begins to apologise for the room and stops four words in. Marguerite hears eighty-nine and twenty-nine and thirteen with a hundred and six in the book in Bellhouse and says nothing. **The forty-one names go in a third time and Tove gives Nadia a notice instead of an apology, because an apology is about the clerk.** **THEN THE IRREVERSIBLE DECISION: he asks Tove to enter the four hours in her own day book, in her own hand, as a clerk's record of a member's statement. She tells him both halves — that it is lawful and that a document in advance is a thing the other side can prepare for — and writes it anyway, because a thing announced into an empty hall protects nobody.** Then she asks who is going to hold it. He names three women and her. **It cannot be withdrawn by the person it names; the right to change his mind at ten past ten is gone; and in fifteen days four people will each already have agreed to a sentence.** Fifteen days.

### Ch 141 — What She Will Not Read
