The 15th, Thursday, and the Board repaints at nine. **Ola Bright's page of four lines comes back at four o'clock on the fourteenth**, under the salt on the hall table, and she makes him stand in front of the six and say out loud that she held it five days and gave it back on the day she said she would. At half past seven, Lantern Row, **Ines asks to be appointed reader of item four — the household record his own objection of the eighth shut out of the hearing.** The argument: a shut box on a proved roll is a shape, and the shape is six women and a building above a chop-house in Ferrier Street, and she is the only person who can stand at a rail and read six women's days aloud and refuse, six times, to let one of them become a description of anybody. **She prices it before he may ask: a reader is on the room's own list, the list goes on the proceeding's file, her review is the second week of next month, and it acquires a date on the twenty-ninth.** She names what she will not read and will not describe — one paper she has read and has not filed, described by neither of them — and says she files nothing. **She asks him for a sentence in his own hand on the back sheet of the roll**, because a sentence in a mouth is a speech and a sentence in a hand is a document. Four terms; the true sentence is that he has never minded being looked through and has been proud of it; the scene; nothing moved, and she can tell. **She closes: fourteen days, and he will want somebody in that room who can end it and there will not be one.** Fourteen days.

**REPAIR PASS, after external review.** The scene was summarised in the first pass — about three hundred words of periphrasis standing in for an hour, in past tense inside present-tense prose — and **is now written on the page**, about 1,250 words of body. **Nothing in the summary above changed.** The staging was also separated from Ch 149 and from Ch 129: the floor and a low cabinet with the lamp moved to light the files and nothing else, his name said once with the rank on it, **and he has to say out loud where the fourth rib is before she will put her thumb on it.** Ch 141 now runs 4,913 words, over Ch 8 at 4,761 and under Ch 55 at 5,065.

### Ch 142 — What He Tells Them First
The 16th. **He tells the household first, before the clerk and before Wren, and it is two things.** One: Ines has volunteered to read the household record aloud in the hall and he is going to let her. Tessa prices it — six handwritings in a transcript sold at a counter; Beatrix names it as the same trade as the five years of cold minutes she gave away on the twelfth with no condition on it. Two: **there is one thing in that room which might move, it is him, and he is going to find out in front of two hundred and eleven people rather than on anybody in this city first.** He will not practise on anybody. **THE CHANGE: Ola Bright declines to write it down.** A fact recorded before an event is an instrument; a thing said before it happens is a thing somebody else can produce. Marguerite sides with her for the first time since the first. **Ola puts three hundred and eleven sentences under the table where no hand in that house can reach them, and does not burn them.** A woman who took her coat off a rail and queued in her shirtsleeves on the second floor goes into the drawer with a street and an hour on it. **That was a hundred and thirteen.** Thirteen days.

### Ch 143 — The Hall Fills
The 22nd, Thursday. The 17th to the 21st went by as a gap. **The Board repaints at nine and the Office publishes its sitting notice on the same morning at the same hour, and the hall is booked to its rating: ninety more chairs on a handcart, two hundred and thirty.** Adeyemi has read the file overnight and **he learns the instrument's rule, the fourth and final permitted use of Volume 3's reversal** — six counterparties on the certified run against twenty-one movements in the same subject's file, fifteen unaccounted, a margin in an older hand reading INSTRUMENT OUT OF TOLERANCE carried forward four times and never reconciled, and clause nine of the tool's own specification. **The 103 courtesy pays, exactly once**: on the third he told him as a courtesy that the instrument has never once worked in an empty room, and he did not know why he said it, and he knows now. **He rules the instrument runs after the roll and for the whole sitting** (an instrument is not run on a person who has not yet been heard) and **rules that the member may speak at any point and has refused a slot four times.** He keeps what he knows because a rule announced to a room becomes an instruction. Wren Kallo is told, dates it, signs it, and gives the number: four people in this building know and three outside it. Seven days.

### Ch 144 — Nine Minutes Before
The 28th. The 23rd to the 27th went by as a gap. **A party may view the hall on the afternoon before the sitting and the caretaker gives him nine minutes, counted, one to the minute.** The doors eighty-one feet from the table. The table eight feet by four with the machine's chalk rectangle two feet left of centre, facing the bench. The rail four feet off the table's edge and a three-foot gap at the end. The bench four inches high and nine feet from the table; the builder's chair with nothing in front of it; the ninety indistinguishable. **In the sixth minute the cleaner comes round the wrong side with a pail and her wrist passes within four inches of his hand and he keeps the hand where it is.** In the seventh minute he asks for the chair to be struck and a caretaker writes him a card. **In the eighth minute he understands the only thing on a sheet of paper in this city that is his will not be in the morning's record.** The ninth minute the doors are locked and he walks the eighty-one feet counting it wrong. One day.

### Ch 145 — He Gives It Away In Public
The 29th, Thursday, ten until two. Six floors cleaned first, which takes the ledger to **a hundred and fourteen**. **Tove Marlin reads the room's list on the step: one hundred and thirty-nine of one hundred and forty seats, seventy-two standing, two hundred and eleven; the plate by the door says two hundred and thirty and there are nineteen places a person can stand and not be counted.** The roll in full: the Field minute and its four-second gap, the visitors' book at eighty-nine and twenty-nine and thirteen, the clinic's four lines, **item four read aloud by a reader who was appointed and who is not a party**, the forty-one names at four hundred and twenty seconds, his own refusal. Adeyemi puts the Office's paragraph and says he does not believe it and that his not believing it is not evidence. Sarn submits the instrument; Wren Kallo says what clause nine is and that it has never been asked for once in seven years. **He speaks into the microphone before the instrument is run, says the column will print nothing, and says in advance that it does move, on him, three times, at the table.** The machine runs from twenty past ten. **Three movements into the fourth rib on his left, at about eleven, about half past twelve and two minutes to two, one to four seconds each, in the open.** The counterparty column stays empty. Wren certifies it. Adeyemi closes the instrument because the Office cannot use it and says the notice does not close. Runa Sato stands in the second row and sits back down.

### Ch 146 — The Ninth Is Himself
The 29th, late afternoon, the parties' room on the third floor. **The only system panel in Volume 3: the ledger reports a hundred and fourteen lines and nine turned.** Wren Kallo saw two of the three movements and works out the arithmetic with him: **one felt in the winter on a tram, three in a hall, four, and four is where a person becomes a line — a line that can be led to a source by a doctor, a tram operator, an accountant or a relative.** The ninth turned line in a season is his and it points at him. Tove Marlin will write the three movements into the transcript as times and will not round them off. **Wren gives him the number: four people deep, three of them told — a plate clerk, Cressida, and a sister who asked how their mother's knee was.** He can feel one coming, with a second and a half of warning, and a single sample cannot be triangulated, and his only cover is the promise he made before the act rather than after it.

### Ch 147 — What Goes In The Sixth Space
The 29th, quarter past five, the branch counter. **Tove Marlin reads the hearing officer's finding at the counter because the finding has to be entered the day it was made: the account is not such a mass as the roll asserts, and the description is superseded in the room by the member's own statement.** **The band comes off the suspension and goes back on the instrument it was always on — the requisition paper of the nineteenth of last month and a finding dated today — and not on the plate, which cannot carry it: 8,138 against 8,141, three grains, 11,862 short.** Anneke Roos has the fourth book open. **She strikes Sarn's paragraph through in her own hand and enters his own sentence under it, copied word for word from the transcript face, as superseded in the room, and signs it — the first entry in the sixth space she has originated rather than received.** She says out loud that it is going to cost her something she has not been told about, and works out herself what it is: a clerk who has entered a thing and struck it and entered another over the top has a position on the content of it. The classification paper dated the nineteenth went up at four and came back down unsigned.

### Ch 148 — A Description In His Own Hand
The 29th, quarter to nine, the parties' room. A clerk with a chair and a sheet and a pen: **a statement into a microphone is not a description, and the record needs one in the member's own hand, going into the transcript, the first sheet of the exhibit roll, the certification desk's file, and a book three floors above a counter he cleans.** He writes it in the first person — four hours, no person touched, three movements on his own body, nothing left him, nothing in the column, an account he cannot measure — and then the last line, which he says out loud in the record before he writes it: **he is unable to say what moved, he is not going to attempt to say it again, and he told the hearing officer in advance and not after.** The clerk makes him say the second sentence again and fixes it in about ninety words, because a clerk records what is in front of the clerk. **Ines Duarte in the stairwell says the two halves: it is the only true document about him in the city and it cannot be got out of a clerk's hands in eleven years; and it has put an address on his only turned line.** Her review is the twenty-second of next month and she will answer it, and being the same woman in front of it is the defence.

### Ch 149 — The Woman Who Is Not On The List
The 30th. The transcript is on a Cobb Street counter for a shilling and the Reading Square's repaint sheet from Thursday carries the only two lines in Ostara that are not bands. Drant says nothing at the counter for the first time in four years. **At four o'clock, on the landing, twenty-nine days in a coat, Sun Meiling asks to be the person the description does not name.** A name on a document in this city is a handle, and a research wing cannot be written down without the wing being written down. **She asks for it and says out loud that it is not modesty and not a favour: she would like the thing, and a research wing is the only room in the city with a door a warrant cannot get through.** She closes the refusal herself. **A household that does not stop him is not a shield, it is an audience** — said in her mouth, on a landing, and she says the six women have known it since the sixth and have been arguing about a price. She gives him the depth of the leak: four people, three told, no number, and it goes through the shop and the yard and the counter rather than through the people he told. Four terms. She wants the man who is not careful. Nothing moved, and she can tell. **She goes down the stairs and says she will sit at that table for the rest of her life and will not take a pen.**

**REPAIR PASS, after external review.** The scene was summarised in the first pass and had re-used Ch 141's staging almost beat for beat — same hand between the shoulder blades, same *"She was exact and she was not gentle"*, same turn and thumb onto the rib. **It is now written on the page, about 900 words of body, and staged in a place no other scene in the book uses: standing, then on the landing boards with his back to the stair rail.** **She never touches the fourth rib** — she holds a hand flat on his side and makes him say the number three times — and she corrects him on the first half of her name. **Nothing in the summary above changed**, including that she closes the refusal here and nowhere earlier. Ch 149 now runs 4,467 words.

### Ch 150 — The Board Repaints
The 31st. **The Board does not repaint, and that is what earns the title: the only weekly publication in the city paints bands, and not one band had changed in the month in which a hearing was held, so the finding went underneath a line about plates coming down on the repaint sheet by the rail, in the open air.** The tile in his rectangle is the same stranger's name it has carried since the eleventh. At the branch a clerk asks whether a form wants a date and is told to put the day it was fixed. **A hundred and fifteen.** Plate 8,138, floor 8,141, three grains, 11,862, unchanged all month. **At the table he asks to be refused a third time in front of all six, and gets five reasons, and none of them is about a signature.** Then **Ola Bright takes the pen out of the drawer she put it in on the sixteenth and writes one line in the seventh space under the sixth line of the thirty-first — the only line in that book that is not a woman's day, and his, and a sentence rather than a signature.** He wanted nine and has not moved a grain. **Seven women, none of them on the roll, and that is the only thing he has built in three months.**

---

## Volume 4 — Six

**Chapters 151–200. ALL FIVE BATCHES WRITTEN. BATCH 0004 (181–190) and BATCH 0005 (191–200) closed;
BATCH 0005 ends Volume 4 with the certificate, the indemnity and the roll.** **VOLUME 4 CLOSED IN PROSE.**
**> In this volume, Lin Hao must build a household that can hold rank six, or he stands at six alone and is taken apart for it.**
**Planned in `outline/volume-04.md`. Scenes at 157, 169, 179, 189, 197.**

### Ch 151 — The Repaint Sheet
The first of April, the one day a month the Fourth Branch is shut and the cleaners have the building to themselves. **The card by the time clock prints the month's instrument days — the fifth, the twelfth, the nineteenth, the twenty-sixth, and then the third of next month — which is the anchor fixing April at thirty days and is the only reason a later writer may subtract against it without assuming it.** On the fourth floor, the certification room off the landing has a bench cloth over the machine and an empty slot in a rack of eight nameplates. **Ivo Sarn is gone.** On the Reading Square the tile in Lin Hao's own rectangle carries **IVO SARN, BAND SEVEN** — put up on the twenty-second of March, **seven days before Anneke Roos struck his paragraph out at a quarter past five on the twenty-ninth, and three more days before a bench cloth.** Ten days from a tile to a cloth, and for the whole of the first seven of them a man was walking into a building every morning and his name was on a wall. **The finding: a name on a tile is a public statement that there is a person, and it comes down because the band changed and not because anything happened to the man — and the machine that would find anyone in this building takes about nine minutes, so the only reason seven women have been safe for four months is that they have all declined to be written down.** He decides to ask somebody to be written down.

### Ch 152 — The Tray On The Second Floor
The second. **The address trays are on the second floor under the north windows, eleven drawers, alphabetical by street, and a stranger's name to a written address takes eight minutes and forty seconds without anybody doing anything clever.** He watches it happen once and then works out, with his own card eleven drawers along in the F, that a skill he spent four years perfecting has never once been hard. He signs a request for Sarn's card and the answer comes back at ten past four: **a tray holds plates, not men, and the drawer was emptied at the end of the week for the period.** **He could have gone and looked for the card that would erase a man and does not, and is careful to be honest that the reason was curiosity and not virtue.** **Piet Ossick**, a band-three gear-runner at the Cordwain coal wharf, sits on the second step of the stair for forty minutes and says the steps are colder than the yard has any right to be; that is **line 116**, and the batch's one System panel reports it. At the table, Beatrix Halm reads out the visitors' book at **a hundred and eleven — forty-one asking about a person, seventeen about the household** — and Sun Meiling comes to the top of the stair without coming down and says the thing that sets the volume running: *there is one person who can put your name on a piece of paper and make it a fact about a body instead of a fact about a man, do not do it gently.*

### Ch 153 — A Legal Person
The third, the Vestry clinic, the dispensary hatch, a queue of people who got up in the dark. **He asks her straight, with no preamble, and she says no in four seconds and then gives him the thing he actually came for.** *Mass* is in a Wing file in her handwriting and she wrote nine hundred wrong words on purpose, because a description that is wrong is a description somebody can check. **A name is a thing you have to be right about; a description is a thing you have to be able to fit, and the Office issues findings.** Every entitlement a member has is a permission, permissions are bought at a counter with grains, and an account in this city has a classification — **so band six buys him nothing, and a counter may not print a blank in a schedule.** **And a requisition of property does not need a person, it needs a place: rank six may requisition property, property in this city is anything a person is using, and the Office can write *the household of the member described in the finding* and have it delivered.** The protection is not rank; it is being in a file, a service or a roll, and the only structure a paper cannot get into is six women who have declined to be written down. She refuses him a card and says the day his name is on a chart in that building is the day the building stops being a place people come who are not on a plate.

### Ch 154 — Wren Kallo
The fourth, the paper-review room on the ground floor of the Reading: a desk, a chair, a window that does not open, and a clerk who opens the door from outside and stays there. **The leak is five deep and four told.** Wren's fourth name is **Hessa Lund**, a dispensary clerk at the Vestry who does the clinic's requisitions, and the question she was asked in a corridor on the twenty-ninth of March was not about a man — **it was whether a clinic that had never been entered in a member's file could be entered in one later, which is a question about a building.** Wren asks to be put at the head of the chain so that it ends at her. **He refuses and gives the real reason, which is not a better one: if she is at the end, the chain starts in her room, and the only door between the thing and the corridor becomes a woman who cannot keep a secret.** She says she has been sitting for nine days turning the shape of the question over. The clerk in the doorway is a document that has not been written yet.

### Ch 155 — The Permission Circular
The fifth, a Thursday, and the Board repaints. **The turn of the batch.** The Exchange posts its monthly circular on the same rail because it has no other way of reaching a person who is not at a counter: every band one to nine against what it buys, with a ruled margin headed ACCOUNT — CLASSIFICATION ENTERED carrying **NO ENTITLEMENT ESTABLISHED** against any account that does not carry one. **He is band five on a plate and his entitlement is blank, and in this city the band is what people read and the entitlement is what they act on, and only one of the two is ever printed on a wall.** Four months of believing his difficulty was a moral question; **it is a blank space in the margin of a printed sheet that has been returning the same line since the start of the winter.** The only door out of it is the classification — a held account, a room with no window. A clerk at the second-floor counter refuses him a plate hold in ninety seconds and tells him what she would have had to write, and offers him the sentence on a slip. **The second door is a standing household, which shares one plate and is read against the highest standing in it, and it requires a name at the top of it.** Eleven thousand eight hundred and sixty-two grains between a plate that says five and a band that says six, and not one of them can be earned by anything he does in public. He decides on the second most frightening thing available to him and goes to ask five women to do it with him.

### Ch 156 — The Composition Return
The sixth. **A composition return is how this city says who lives at an address and under whose standing. It is not served; it is posted, and then it comes back.** He watches a clerk put one in an envelope at the second-floor counter and asks to have it stopped: it cannot be, and it cannot be answered in advance either, because a return is completed at the premises by a person of reading age at the premises, and a return completed at a counter goes back empty and earns a second form with a shorter clock on it. **It carries no name — a street, a number, a floor, and *the household of the member described in the finding of the twenty-ninth*.** The Office has had the address since the eleventh of March because a visitors' book with a hundred and six live entries went on the exhibit roll that day, and he read all nine items of that roll aloud in a hall on the fourteenth and said nothing. **Twenty-one days, due the twenty-seventh, printed with its subtraction.** At the yards, **Nadia Ferrous** has already read it over a clerk's shoulder and tells him: nobody in that hall can be asked first, and there is no version of him entitled to stand at a door on Ferrier Street. He says the price out loud, which is that he is proposing to put his name at the top of six other people's paperwork, and she asks for the names, and he asks for a woman who can put a name on paper and make it a fact about a body.

### Ch 157 — On The Scale
The seventh, the scene, in the sixth-floor washroom at about eleven at night with one bulb over the sinks and water off on three floors. **Tessa Okonkwo carries a balance up the back stairs herself** and sets it on the window-cleaning shelf. **Four conditions in plain words before anything starts: nothing spent by either of them; the ribs are not touched; he asks her out loud every time; not one person is told.** Adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, no impaired state, **no draw spent on anybody**, violence and sex in separate registers. Unhurried, narrated in a clinician's register, and the staging is new against all ten scenes of the grid: **she never touches the fourth rib and gives the reason — the ribs are the only part of it on his body, and a clinician who has put her hand on it can never again write a chart on him with her own hand out of it, and she says out loud that this is the same refusal he made in a hall.** **He does not use her name until the last line of a chart, because a chart does not take a name until the end.** Afterwards she weighs him: **seven stone and two pounds over, nothing in it that a clinician would call fluid, swelling or tumour, two ribs recorded and nothing else.** She signs the chart, dates it, times it at five to midnight, **and gives it to the dispensary day book at the Vestry.** It is the first document in four years with both their names on it. **It cost the clinic the one ground the Classification Wing tolerates it on, which is that it is unfunded and has never been entered in anybody's file — and she did it because a woman who will not be written down cannot be defended, and not for him.**

### Ch 158 — Ferrier Street
The eighth, and the return is on the table by four. **Ola Bright brings it up and has not opened it and says so on the stairs.** Six women at the table and a real argument, and the findings are each one of the six's own. **Ines Duarte:** a return is answered at the premises by a person of reading age there, and a household is read against the highest standing in it, and she says out loud that she raised the objection in his favour first and then had to argue against it anyway. **A member with a disciplinary process open is entered on a return with the process attached**, off the schedule, and Runa Sato says it is correct, says she will be the reason the paper works anyway, and tells him to stop asking whether she is sure about anything for the rest of the month. **Beatrix Halm:** forty-one people asked about a person and seventeen about the household, and the difference between not being frightened of that book and being frightened of it is that six of them are now frightened of the same thing at the same time. **Marguerite Vale prices it once and refuses to price it twice:** the right to be asked before you are touched, for a building, for a year, is the entire value of the sheet, it is not protection, it is a chair at a counter where somebody has to say your name — and every one of them has been arguing about it from the wrong end. **Tessa says she has already done it.** **Sun Meiling says the question is not whether they answer it but who does, that she could answer it, and that she will not, again, in front of a form.** They agree on one thing: one return, six hands, and he does not see it and does not sign it and is not in the room. Runa Sato puts the visitors' book on the form and says the first thing the room does without him is read the book out from the beginning and answer what it can.

### Ch 159 — Beneath The Table
The ninth. **Ola Bright tells him before he has his coat off: four of them wrote back, over the weekend, on the actual form, without telling him, and she has copied all four into the book under the table where it has been since the sixteenth of March — and she tells him what that has cost, which is that four women putting a thing in writing about themselves and a record that they did it before anybody asked was the one thing she could not sit upstairs with.** Four answers, each unalike: **Ines's is the best piece of paper the household has produced and it is a filing, and she attaches a clause that is not on the form about the band of a fifth person belonging to an employer.** Beatrix wrote NOT MINE and would fill the rest in at the yards. Runa wrote a Field address as a joke that is also true. **Marguerite wrote the standing as an invoice.** All four answered the first question perfectly and separately and none of them put a name in the box. **He turns the form over: at the foot, four inches wide, headed PREMISES HELD UNDER THE STANDING OF, and every one of the four answers stops an inch above it.** Nobody is going in it — and so the return goes to a counter with the box empty and a counter completes it itself out of the exhibit roll, which contains item four. **Beatrix says the true thing: they wrote those four answers because they could not stand in that room and be asked, and a table with him at it is a table where they agree with him.** He asks one person to do the one thing in that house she has never refused to do.

### Ch 160 — Six Carbon Copies
The tenth, and the batch's irreversible decision. **A hundred and seventeen**, a man on the second floor with a daughter off school since the sixth with a hand that would not close. At the kitchen table Ola Bright tells him what the request will cost before she does it: **since the sixteenth of March the copybook has been the only thing in Ostara nobody asked for, and from tonight nothing written in it is unasked, which is the last thing in the city that was hers and that she did not have to explain.** **He asks her to copy one sentence six times in six hands and hand him the page.** It is a standing instruction: if a paper comes for this house, answer it in writing in your own hand and do not consult each other, and if any one of them cannot answer a question on it, write the word no and her name and post that as well. **It removes him from the chain of command of the only structure he has ever wanted to be inside of.** Five copies are handed out in the room, each woman refusing a different thing for a different reason: **Nadia** is not the reason and never will be, and he tells her he has taken the thing she would have said yes to off her; **Beatrix** reads it on the step and goes out into the rain; **Runa Sato** signs it face down and says he has just made her the reason the paper works and to stop asking whether she is sure; **Marguerite** says no person she has worked for in thirty years ever gave six people a standing instruction in writing and then walked out of the room. **He does not give Sun Meiling one, because she has said no and he has stopped asking her.** He writes the sentence in pencil on the wall of the landing, undated, with no name near it, at her instruction, so that she can come up a stair and read what she said while it is still true. **The sixth copy is Ines Duarte's and she refuses it**, because a woman who files nothing is a woman who cannot be got at, and she writes four lines on the back of it that are not for him and not for the file and says she will not say what is in them. **The first blank in the house that none of them put there on purpose.**

### Ch 161 — Runa Sato
The eleventh. **The visitors' book is read aloud from the beginning, as Runa Sato decided on the eighth, and Beatrix Halm admits she has begun to hear the same surnames before she gets to them — which is Sun Meiling telling her to go and count them, and that instruction pays at 167.** Runa comes in wet at half past five, empties the room, sits on the bottom step, and says three things: he is not to be in her hearing room, she will not be able to object, and **when she comes out at two o'clock he is not to tell her what was said in it — because she would rather walk out of that building with a lie than with a man who spent four hours on a bench thinking about how to be honest with her.** She tells him the question in advance so that he can decide in advance, and quotes the Bureau's own muster-room rule about it **about a division officer in Kestrel Hill**, in the wrong tense. He says no. **Beatrix writes the refusal and the date in the seventh space.** **Ledger 118**, in prose, a man in the Cordwain yard moving a stone out of a path four hundred people had walked around for years. **He stays at the yard gate until six and counts every body that comes out of it, in the way he counts washrooms, and understands standing there in the dark that this is the whole of what a division clerk will ask anybody for — not an admission and not a feeling, a figure, and a man who can produce one can be asked for it twice. That is what he carries to the hearing the next morning.** Ends on the fuse in the corridor at Cordwain that has been out since the summer.

### Ch 162 — The Muster Room
The twelfth, a Thursday because the reading is. **The hearing happens in front of the reader and does not resolve.** Runa Sato is in a fourth-floor room from ten until two with four people in it. He is not admitted; he takes a candle to the ledge outside the door, and at half past nine a clerk takes his name, his address, his trade — *washrooms at the Fourth Branch* — and how long he has known the member. **At the other end of the landing the muster room is open and the roster is on the wall, ruled into four columns, the fourth with no heading, and painted above it in older paint a sentence he reads four times in an hour.** At twenty past one she comes out and says *they have twenty minutes*, and says why: a member may not be present for an item concerning another person. **At twenty to two he is brought in alone. The count is about him — how many times did something move out of a person and into you, in the hall, in the open, in front of two hundred and eleven people. He says three. The officer says out loud that a number answered by another person cannot go on a record of the member's own conduct, so the count stands as refused and is entered against her and not against him.** In the corridor she asks him the question she named on the previous evening and he lies. In the muster room her name is in the roster with one word in the fourth column, and the duty clerk explains the second half of the wall: an unanswered count cannot be reconciled by an office, and an office that cannot reconcile one has one lawful way left to find out how many people are in a building, and it is an assessor. **And he says the half of it that matters, which is that a member entered as refusing is still on this roster, still on the strength, still paid and still in the division, and that the fourth column took nothing off her.** And the standing she entered on the return on the eighth is now a false line on a form. **He tells her he has lied. She says *right* and goes up the stair.** The frame comes off the wall at the end, and the wall behind it is a different white because a wall remembers having something hung on it for eleven years — and above where the frame was there is a band about a foot high, a shade off the white around it, painted back by a man with the wrong brush, which is what Chapter 163 finds and Chapter 176 is named for.

### Ch 163 — Off The Roster
The thirteenth, and **the batch's irreversible decision.** At half past seven in Cordwain the frame is back on the wall, the rule has been painted back in white and slightly out of true, R. SATO has a rule straight through it, and a card under the rail says STRENGTH RECONCILED, SEVENTH — eight on that wall at ten yesterday morning, seven now. **And the duty clerk says the thing that makes it survivable: the fourth column took nothing off her, she is on the roster and paid and in the division and her name goes out on Thursday morning with the rest of them, and he ruled that line off the wall himself, yesterday, at about four o'clock, because he has owed that building a strength count since the fifteenth of February and there was one name too many on it when he came to do it.** At ten past ten a young man with a satchel knocks twice at the door of Ferrier Street with a notice of premises standing, dated the twelfth and effective from the fifteenth, and carries in capitals: from the fifteenth of this month, any office in this city may serve a paper at these premises, no warrant required, no reason required. It is served on premises by a person of reading age at the premises, and the stub wants a name in a hand and the hour. **Sun Meiling comes down four stairs and signs it, and writes the hour when he tells her the stub wants the hour, and the whole thing takes about ninety seconds.** And she names the arithmetic afterwards: five women in that house can be reached somewhere else and she cannot be reached anywhere, and a paper about a building does not have to find her, it only has to find the building, and the person who answers the door is always the one who has not got anywhere else to be. She refuses to have the notice copied, refuses to let the room take a position on it, and tells the room that no other woman in it could have had this happen to her. Then she writes a second standing instruction in the seventh space: **if a paper comes for this house it comes to me, and I answer it, and I do not ask anybody else in this house what the answer is.** She goes up and shuts her door for the rest of the day.

### Ch 164 — Nadia Ferrous
The fourteenth. Nadia comes off the cars at ten past two in front of nine operators, as she does once a week for four months, and goes to Cobb Street instead of home and **buys the exhibit roll for a shilling with no name taken on it, and finds that it is set up fresh each morning, which is the only thing about it worth a shilling.** She reads it on a low wall in the arcade. Item four is the six lines a day in six hands. Underneath it, because the household said its own arithmetic out loud in a hall on the fourteenth of March, **a hundred and eleven visitors, forty-one asking after a person, seventeen asking after the household.** And on the back, set out as a date and a line from the twenty-ninth of March downward: **the twelfth, a member entered as refusing by the member, count incomplete; the thirteenth, premises above the chop-house in Ferrier Street, notice of standing served, received by S. Meiling at half past ten.** Ninety seconds yesterday, in type this morning, for a shilling, and the man who set the type has never heard of her. She buys a second copy, puts one face up on the kitchen table, and then says the thing she has been four months from finishing: **that she has wanted him since the second of March, that he has never once let her finish it, that the only one of the six of them who can go anywhere and come back has never been sent for anything, and that there is not one line on that sheet about either of them and that is correct and it is also the whole of what either of them has.** Ends with her at the table asking for one sentence about what they are going to do about the fact that it exists.

### Ch 165 — Ines Duarte
The fifteenth, in the corridor at the back of the kitchen. **She tells him her review is on the morning of the twenty-second, a week away, and names no weekday, and the date is not explained in this volume.** Then she gives the rule: **the only thing which defends a person against an audit is being the same woman all the way back** — and an audit does not ask what you did, it asks who you were on a date, who signed for you on it, and whether the person answering is the person the second question is about; it cannot tell a person who changed from a record that changed; after nine years of that, eleven hands have described one life and not one of them can say whether the first and the last ever met. **Everything the household has done since the first of December is a defence against an exposure and would fail an audit in about nine minutes**, because six people who changed four hundred times in four months is a confession in a machine's language. And Sun Meiling's stub on the thirteenth is the first document this house has produced with a continuous person in it. **She tells the story of a tram inspector of seventy-one whose column was touched by nine years of improving hands**, and then stops, and says she is not going to say what she thinks is going to happen to her. She asks him not to tell the table, and gives the reason: memory is a thing you lose an inch of every year, and she has been paying for eleven years of being the same woman with no document anywhere that says so. He agrees. **She has done the one thing she has refused twice in writing to do — told one person a fact about herself in a room — and she says so on the way out.** Ends on the visitors' book: a hundred and eleven entries in one hand over four months, the only document in Ostara in which six women are demonstrably the same six women all the way back, and it is for sale for a shilling.

### Ch 166 — Form 4-C Returned
The sixteenth. **The second issue of Form 4-C arrives in the post with eleven days on it, and the sixteenth plus eleven is the twenty-seventh, which is the same day the first issue is due.** It carries the four questions, the box, and — under the box, in smaller type — **a fifth question the Office already knows the answer to**: has any person of reading age at these premises been entered as refusing an answer to a count since the first issue was posted, with a line under it reading *where the answer is not given it is taken from what the office holds.* And it prints **a fifth permitted answer to the fourth question** in the margin, and the schedule it comes from: **item eleven. Associations holding no plate and no audit. Persons entered in this quarter, eleven thousand and some hundred. Membership attested. Attestation not audited. No composition return has been filed by any of them at any time.** Sun Meiling does the arithmetic and calls it a shape rather than a group, and Ines Duarte says the real thing: **for four months the blank at the foot of that box was the only thing holding, and it was holding because not one clerk in Ostara could fill it in — and now an office can, with something that is not a person.** Six women answer in six hands between two and six, unconsulted, as the instruction says. **Sun Meiling writes yes. Runa Sato writes the word that is true.** The form goes to a counter with two different answers about the same afternoon. Ines Duarte: *that is not an answer, that is this house.*

### Ch 167 — Beatrix Halm
The seventeenth, and the batch's reckless act. She counts the forty-one surnames on the back of a handbill: **eleven surnames account for thirty-four of the forty-one, every one of them came to that gate more than once, and not one of the eleven ever asked whether there was a house.** She tells nobody she is taking the visitors' book out of the house, walks into a room off Ash Row with forty people in it, puts a shilling on the table, and **reads all a hundred and eleven entries out loud from a table, standing, because a document can be bought and thrown away and forty people in a room have to hear a voice.** Seven of the eleven react. The thirty-eighth is a man who puts his hand flat on the table and says *that is my sister.* On the step he tells her there are eleven of them and that he has been buying the paper since the twelfth of March. She comes in at ten past one to a room where Runa Sato is asleep sitting up, which she has not done before, and says the whole of it, including what it cost: **the only document in Ostara in which six women are the same six women all the way back now has her mouth on it, and a timetable printer in Bellhouse knows the name of every cabin on her line.** The room works out what she worked out and cannot fix: there is nothing in this city they can serve him with, because he is not the Bureau and he has not broken a rule. Runa Sato says the true sentence about it — a man who is refused at a gate comes back — and Beatrix claims the gate.

### Ch 168 — The Shilling Version
The eighteenth, and the batch's escalation. **Ossian Kerr, a band-four jobbing printer, four doors up Ferrier Street for nineteen years**, is at the gate at a quarter past two with a folded sheet and has been there since half past one. He is not an officer and not a villain and not a fool. He has bought the exhibit roll and written **six questions on the back of it in pencil**, the sixth being *why, in nineteen years, not one person at these premises has ever been on the Board.* Beatrix takes it in. **The paper comes to Sun Meiling, and she distributes it without a word, and six women write between two and six in one afternoon in one room without speaking to each other about it** — Ines a filing with a footnote and a sentence about nobody being kept, Runa the second and fourth and sixth, Beatrix hers standing up in a signal cabin, Tessa hers sent in an envelope off the dispensary that names a building for the first time, Marguerite a price and a refusal, and Sun six and then the number seven underneath. He reads it on the pavement in six minutes and says three things. **He has what he came for.** He was wrong about one thing and names it: six answers in six hands written under one roof without anyone speaking is the answer to his sixth question, and he did not have to pay a shilling for it. And there is no protection in answering him, because there is no office to complain to, and the next one will not knock twice. He walks **four doors up, and Beatrix watches him go in and now knows where that man sleeps.**

### Ch 169 — Agnete Lindqvist
The nineteenth. At the hatch, Tessa Okonkwo says one thing and calls the next person: a woman came to this quarter on the night before last, walked to the end of a corridor in a building off the Vestry market, and asked one question at a door — *what is your name* — and the woman on the other side opened it and said it. **A laundry off the market, a stair at the back, four floors, a corridor on the second floor with a runner worn thin down the middle, four doors, a window that does not open, and a brass plate four inches by two with nothing on it.** She knocks twice with a two-minute wait between. **Agnete Lindqvist gives four terms in a former clerk's register, and the fourth is the cost: this is a room in which a person cannot be compelled to testify, which means she cannot compel him, he cannot compel her, nothing said in it can be taken out of it by anybody — and therefore there is no protection in here at all, for either of them, for the whole of an hour.** Then she names it: **the Church of the Quiet Number**, eleven thousand people in this quarter, no plates, no returns, twenty years of organising to say that what you do when there is no witness to it is the only thing that counts, perfectly correct the whole time and perfectly illegal the whole time, and *your eleven thousand is not a number on a form about our house.* **THE SCENE, WRITTEN OUT IN FULL — 4,719 words, and this is the repaired version.** About an hour, on the runner in the corridor, in a room of one chair and a window that does not open; nothing spent by anybody, no impaired state, adults, awake, unrestrained, the ribs never mentioned by either of them. **She puts her cardigan over the one chair because she is a woman who has kept a room for eleven years, and the zip of the skirt is mended with a staple, and there is a vest and a stocking with a darn and no shoes, and he learns that the shoes are under the bed. She tells him he is allowed to look and that she has not decided what she would want said about it. Then she tells him in advance that the next four minutes are the part where she is bad at it. Her feet are cold and she says so out loud and laughs at herself for a second and a half, which is the first thing either of them says in that corridor that has not been arranged in advance. He takes her shirt off badly and she swears at him in the flat register she uses for figures. She tells him where to put his hands twice over in different words; his right one goes to the back of her neck and is told not to pull; she makes a noise and puts her forearm across herself and will not be looked at while she does.** She works at him with the patience of a woman who has read about this for eleven years and done it once, and it is not good, and she keeps at it because she said she would. She wants it said out loud, twice, and then slower, and then in single words, and then there are none and he is taking directions from her hands. **About ten minutes in the body stops being bad at it: she gets him off the wall onto the floor of the runner with her on top of him and her hands on his chest, watching him the way a woman watches a form she has to sign, and puts her knees on either side of the worn path so that both of them are on the hard edges, which she worked out four seconds before she did it. She lays a hand flat against him and does not move it, and tells him why before he gets there: if she stops touching him she will stop knowing where he is, and she is not losing this.** She says he is not frightened of her and that this is a little insulting, tells him he should try to like her, and when he does she says nothing about having been asked. **Then she gives him all of her weight at once instead of holding herself off on an arm.** His name is not said by either of them; the name-exchange is that he has not said hers and she has not said his and she has been not saying it on purpose for four hours. **She stops talking and it is not tender: fast, expert, and at one point she laughs out loud once in the middle of it at nothing at all and then stops as though caught and holds on to him. In the last four minutes she holds his head with both hands so that she does not have to watch his face, and he would have given her anything and she took none of it and said so out loud while it was happening.** She came first, and he let her, and he has done that once before on the sixth floor of a branch office with a balance on a window-cleaning shelf. Afterwards she walks to the door at the far end — the one onto the stair, not hers — and opens it, and **the market and the cold stone and the rain come in and stop at the edge of the runner, and she leaves the door standing open behind her and does not look back at it once.** She sits on the floor of the corridor in two feet of light and says that she has been reading a description of a man in a file that went in three weeks ago, and that somebody has been reading something aloud in a kitchen in the same voice for months and she has heard it through a wall and never once gone to the door. **His name is used once, at the end, as a word inside a sentence and not as an address.**

### Ch 170 — The Vestry Steps
The twentieth, and the day is named by nobody. **He finishes the sixth floor at ten past eleven and does not go home, and Agnete Lindqvist is on the bottom step of the building off the Vestry market and has been there since about seven, because she could not go back in** — she got to the fourth step at ten past seven and sat down on it and has been outside for the first time in eleven years and cannot get back into a building. He walks back to Ferrier Street and brings six women out on foot. **Sun Meiling stands at the bottom of five steps and does not go up, and says that if anybody says the household has got itself a woman out of a building, she wants it written down that she went and got her.** Tessa Okonkwo goes up first with a bag from the clinic; Agnete Lindqvist says she has been kind to her for eleven years and has never seen her in the room. **Ines Duarte states the problem: the first question asks how many persons of reading age keep an address at the premises, and there are now eight if Agnete agrees to be there, and Ines cannot write her in, because the Office holds a record of her and of every person in her association and there is not one ruled space on any form in this city where she goes — and she has spent eleven years trying to get people off forms and has never had to try to keep one on.** And the fifth permitted answer in the margin of the second issue is the standing the Office can now put in the box, and it is eleven thousand people, and one of them is standing on the top step. Beatrix argues the form is the best thing that has ever happened and then refuses to say so, because of Ossian Kerr. **Agnete Lindqvist says she would tell a private citizen nothing, not out of caution, because the moment the Church is named in an answer in a street in the Slough then eleven thousand people become a thing that has been named, and they have survived twenty years on being a shape and not a fact.** **Runa Sato asks to stand at the bottom of that stair**, because she cannot be found by any office at all and wants to be there at two in the morning on the nights when there is nobody in the street. **And she states the distinction the whole batch turns on, in her own words and in the street, where six women can hear it: she was entered as refusing on the twelfth at ten past two, and that is a word in a column and it cost her the premises and she would pay it again — and there is a second thing on that wall that is not the column, a duty clerk she has never spoken to ruling her line off himself the previous afternoon over a strength count the office had owed since February. Two pieces of paper on her and one of them was hers.** Ends with the number turning into people — about forty of them going past that market before eight in the morning, none of them on a plate, none of them going to be counted — and with **seven days to the twenty-seventh**, four names and two blanks, and a woman who could go in the box if the city had a column for her and it has not got one.

### Ch 171 — Household In Law
The twenty-first, and the household does a sum nobody says out loud. **Agnete Lindqvist is at the kitchen table for the first time** and stops on the second step and the fourth on the way up, and she has been reading the second issue since the fourteenth — seven days — and she is the only person alive who has read four hundred composition returns from the receiving end, having been a clerk for eleven years and four of them in composition. **She states what the instrument is for: a composition return is an instrument of acquisition, it is how the city acquires the right to serve a household, and it has three states — complete, and it goes to a district; late, and it goes on four shelves on the third floor of the Reading and is read by a woman doing a period bundle; and incomplete, which is not a return at all but a set of answers to a form.** She says the last of it in the yard with Sun Meiling, because Sun will not have it said in front of a room. **The box at the foot wants a head of household: a person of reading age who may be required to answer for the premises and may be answered for — and a standing cannot be answered for, so if the box is filled with item eleven the premises has no head and is described rather than held.** Ines Duarte tells him where incomplete returns go: the third floor, the drawing-board room, the third shelf, and that she has never been into it because she does not work there. **Seven refusals go round the table in about eleven minutes, one from each of the six and one from Agnete Lindqvist, and he says nothing during any of them and is the only person in the room who has not spoken.** Sun Meiling states the arithmetic in one sentence — the name goes in it because there is nobody else, and that is a shortage and not a reason — and goes up. **The batch's clock: six days, 21 + 6 = the twenty-seventh, agreed with the two figures already on the table.** He does not go up to the third floor that afternoon and knows exactly why: the third floor's public stair is open until four, he is not at work until seven, and he could have gone in his coat and come back with a shilling for a cup of tea, **and he does not go because he would have had to ask, and a question about a return would be written down in a book by a stranger.** Ends on him at the junction with a plate he has read in his head and forty feet of corridor he has never walked.

### Ch 172 — The Waiting Room
The twenty-second, and nothing at all happens in it, and the day is not dated and its weekday is not named. **He goes up the public stair of the Reading for the first time in four years and Sabine Rooke lets him sit in the parties' room because he is on no roll and is not a party and is not an observer, so he is a member of the public, and a member of the public is the only person who can sit there without anybody having to decide anything.** The room: a long table, six chairs a side, eleven chairs with arms against the wall, a jug and two cups, a card on the door frame. **A man called Ferren has been in the far corner since a quarter to nine and has a coat-shaped hollow in the dust behind him**, and at five to ten a clerk comes to the door and says his name is not on the sheet and to come back at the next sitting, which is the cruellest thing that happens in that building because it costs the Office nothing. Ferren's count came back wrong at the start of March, one thousand nine hundred against four thousand for years, and his daughter's place at a school in Bell Row runs on his band. **Lin Hao fills the jug and puts a cup in front of him and says I am sorry about the sheet, and cannot help him, and says so out loud: a hand moves nothing on a stranger, it costs grey at one temple and a grey night and a night of sleep like being woken on the hour, and he promised in a hall on Cobb Street on the twenty-ninth of March that he would not move it on himself again — so the hand is on the table and it is nailed shut and he did that to it himself in front of everybody.** Ferren's answer is the chapter: *you are the first person who has said the word sorry at me in this room and I would rather you had not, because now I know that you can see it, and there is nothing at all you can do about that.* Four hours of counting — eleven chairs, two cupboards, four windows, a rail of pegs with one coat on it, a clock a second fast. **At ten to two the door at the end of the corridor opens and four people come out, and Ines Duarte looks at him, and he has about nine seconds, and she says do not ask me and goes down the outside stair.** He counts the four doors on that landing on the way out because he counts things: **the fourth is shut, has a grille across the bottom third of its window, a brass plate reading DRAWING BOARD. LATE RETURNS AND PERIOD BUNDLES. IN BY THE PUBLIC STAIR, and a bench outside it, and nothing went in and nothing came out of it between half past nine and ten past two.** Forty feet from where he spent the day. He does not go back up.

### Ch 173 — Sun Meiling
The twenty-third, and **the notice of premises standing signed on the thirteenth does its work.** The knock is two and a wait and two, and the young man with the satchel is the same one, and what he carries is a schedule of the persons of reading age keeping an address at the premises, required under that notice, with a month from the date of service on it. **Sun Meiling comes down four stairs before anybody speaks, because it comes to her.** She gives the room nine seconds and one sentence each and then says the reason she will not write six names: a service file, a posting and a yard roster, a process open in Cordwain and a rule through a line, a guild, a clinic that is unfunded, and a finding of the twenty-ninth of March with a number on it — **what is in those two inches is not six names, it is six doors.** Beatrix Halm says an unserved schedule goes in front of an assessor. **Sun Meiling writes the number six, and then writes item eleven underneath it in full, with eleven thousand and some hundred persons, in her own hand, in a room in Ferrier Street, and signs it and dates it and puts the time on it because her hands do that.** It is the one thing Agnete Lindqvist said in the yard on the twentieth must never be done, and it is done inside four days. **Agnete's price is four sentences and a document: go and get the sheet back and on the back of it, in the margin, write the date, write that no office asked for it, and sign your name twice, once as the person who gave the answer and once as the keeper of the premises — because in eleven years the only thing that will exist about today is that sheet, and a woman who has never asked to be on one will come across it and not know whether it happened to us.** Sun Meiling goes and gets it and writes it. **And she says one thing to him, in front of six people: somebody in that house is going to go and read what is on that shelf, and it is not going to be her.** Ends on the standing she just wrote: a body is not a person and a body cannot be answered for, and a household in law needs somebody who can be.

### Ch 174 — Sabine Rooke
The twenty-fourth, and **THE REVERSAL, proved on paper in five minutes by a man going up a floor he has never cleaned.** He goes in his coat at ten past one and is in the third-floor corridor at four minutes past one, forty feet from a door he stood in front of on the twenty-second having read the plate and gone down. **The drawing-board room: four shelves, the third labelled LATE RETURNS AND PERIOD BUNDLES, a bar across the ajar door, and a woman at a bench with a bundle who tells him it is not open to the staff, that he is not staff, that if he is here about a late return they are on the third shelf and in the files by street A to Z and she will send him to the counter, which sends him back to this floor.** He goes in. Files by street, eleven along in the F, the identical machine to the address trays on the second floor of his own building, **which he learned on the second of this month, twenty-two days ago, by watching a stranger's name become a written address in eight minutes and forty seconds.** **Eighty or ninety cards in fourteen inches. A card marked LATE, PERIOD CLOSED, dated THE TWENTY-SECOND OF MARCH.** One address and six names under it, each with a printed standing and a printed band, and he knows the first three before his hand has stopped moving. **The box: four inches wide, ruled, and empty — not crossed out, not marked, not held open, empty in the way a thing is empty when the person who wrote the rest of it got to it and had nothing to put in and went on to the line underneath.** The clerk's line: S. ROOKE, about half past four in the afternoon on the twenty-second of March. He puts it back at the same angle with the stub facing out. **The arithmetic: seven days before the hall, ten days before the first of April.** Every protection the household believed in for four months had been a description of a piece of paper. It was the price of a ninth chair and he had agreed to it in a corridor in eleven seconds because he believed he was the only thing on the sheet. **Nothing was hidden: it was on a shelf because it had missed its period, and a filing system does not conceal late returns, it is the place late returns go.** He could have had it in ninety seconds. **The cost: the woman fills in her bundle card at about five o'clock and the number against persons off the public stair is two, and she did not write what he looked at, and he can do it once.** First person, alone, in the square, and it is a loss with a use and he does not resolve it. Ends on the two lines and a hundred yards of street.

### Ch 175 — The Head Of Household
The twenty-fifth, and **the batch's irreversible decision, and it is his.** Ines Duarte comes at ten with a coat on and he does not ask her about the twenty-second. **He tells her there is a composition return on the third shelf, dated the twenty-second of March, six names and an empty box and a clerk's line, about ninety seconds with the file, and he does not tell her who signed it, and she lets him not tell her and says why in four seconds.** Then the work, in four points: a return is not filed incomplete; the Office has never held a household at that address because there has never been one; a return with no head is completed by the Office out of the schedule at item eleven and the premises are then described; **and a return is completed by a head of household, and a head of household does not have to keep an address at those premises, which she has known for eleven years and never wanted.** **She refuses it herself and says why: six women answerable to an auditor is the only arrangement she could get.** **She is the one who makes him say it at the table, because he will make a bad job of it.** Sun Meiling connects the schedule she answered on the twenty-third to the margin of the second issue and refuses to be put in the box, and makes Tessa Okonkwo refuse to be offered it. **Seven refusals and he said nothing; Ines reads out the six liabilities one at a time — Runa's count declined and the rule through her line, Tessa's mass in a Wing file in her own hand, Nadia's forty-one names and a service file, Beatrix's broken price and the ninth of September and a year of her handwriting, Ines's two refusals and a review she will not discuss, Marguerite's terms down, unsigned and out of force — and he says put my name in it before she has finished.** He says it aloud in a kitchen with six women in it and it cannot be taken back and not one of them thanked him, which is correct, and which he had not expected, because it was not a gift. **Sun Meiling refuses to be thanked for the other thing: she is going to spend the rest of her life being the one woman in that house who is not on a piece of paper, and she will not be talked out of it by somebody who has just volunteered to be on one for her.** **Ines's last line is the price: the return goes in complete on the twenty-seventh, being entered is a form, and a form is completed in a room in front of a clerk, so he is going to have to go back into that building and this time somebody will write down that he came.**

### Ch 176 — The Pale Rectangle
The twenty-sixth, **a Thursday, and the Board repaints at nine because the Board repaints at nine.** He goes at half past eight with a broom and has never once been there at nine, and the old man with the white beard in the middle hands him a ladder and says he has been thinking about it since February. **A Board is a list of bands and not a list of people, and where the week's list is empty the men leave the slot bare, because a tile nobody has claimed is a claim.** At ten past ten he comes down the ladder with a tile in his hands and puts it face down in a crate: **IVO SARN. BAND SEVEN**, off the wall after four weeks because he has not been on the list since the first and a hole in the wall is not a list. And under his feet is a pale rectangle of bare biscuit about four inches by two, which he has looked at four times a month for four years and never known was there. Anneke Roos, on the third step: **that will be bare until somebody takes the slot, it is not held for anybody, it is not reserved, it is ceramic.** **Wren Kallo is there because the Wing certifies the week's list, and she is one of three people alive he may tell, and she is given the finding in eleven seconds at a rail in the middle of a square:** an incomplete filing is not a document, it is producible by nobody and citable by nobody; a complete filing is indexed and produced to anybody who asks, once, and the copy goes to the person who asked. **So the empty box has been the only reason that card has never reached anybody, and it stops being the reason the moment a name goes into a box.** She gives him until tomorrow afternoon. **Seven of them come up the street and the loose group comes apart about forty feet out: Sun Meiling in daylight for the first time since December, Runa Sato standing twenty feet off the rail where the square goes past and the rail does not, and Agnete Lindqvist on the step of the stair because she cannot get back into a building and can read a wall four hundred feet away.** Nobody comes over. Ends with **tomorrow being the twenty-seventh, 26 + 1**, five days since the twenty-first, and a wall printing a blank for a band that has not moved.

### Ch 177 — The Counting Room
The twenty-seventh, the deadline, and **nine seconds at a counter on the second floor of the building he cleans.** He goes past the counter at ten past eight in his overalls, having worked in the building four years and never been behind it. **COUNTING** on a glass door, a room twelve feet deep, pigeonholes to the ceiling, and **Wray**, the clerk who refused him a plate hold in ninety seconds on the fifth. He has not thought about the first question — how many persons of reading age keep an address — and Beatrix Halm says seven, and one of them is asleep in a chair and one of them cannot go back into a building. **Wray counts for four minutes with a tally frame and reads six answers in six hands and says unconsulted is not a thing that exists on a form, and writes seven because it is arithmetic and not agreement, and says so out loud so that nobody goes home thinking she believed them.** Then the box. **It comes to him, Marguerite and Runa and Tessa say his name in the same four seconds, and Sun Meiling says nothing, and his hand is out before he has decided to.** He writes it in nine seconds at the foot of the fourth question, Wray initials under it and dates it and says **that is a return.** **A complete return goes to the district inside a week and opens a certification of sixty days from the date of filing, and the certificate, not the filing, makes it a household.** He writes it a second time on the older sheet, which is the one the district uses, and it is much harder. Then Wray stops: **Runa Sato's second line carries a Field division address against a standing printed in the Office's own type, and a small book in the tray holds an entry made at about seven that morning and dated the twelfth, and the return is going down complete with a false line in it and the Office will check it against a wall and it will match**, and in about eleven days there will be a line in a district file saying a member of this household gave an answer that was true on the eighth and is not true now. Runa Sato says do it and Wray says she has looked, which is her doing it. **Out on the pavement he tells Ines Duarte, and she works out in eleven seconds that a complete return reconciles every other return on that address and the one he read on the twenty-fourth is off the shelf within the hour — and then she says the thing he has not worked out: he found a paper he was never supposed to know about and instead of burning it he went and made it true, and four of the six people in that building are going to find out that he knew for three days and did not say, and every one of them will be right.** He told her and not one other person. **One hundred and eighteen lines, nine turned, the ninth still him, nothing spent on anybody, the promise intact.** Ends on a strip of card filled in at about eleven minutes past five the previous afternoon, and a figure against a column headed persons off the public stair.

### Ch 178 — A Patient File
The twenty-eighth, at the Vestry. **A notice comes up on the wire and it has no name on it:** unfunded establishments recorded in connection with the file of a member of the Bureau will be brought within the ordinary schedule from the first day of next month, and are advised to prepare and submit a schedule of persons treated. **About eleven unfunded establishments in this quarter, every one of them has to work out whether it is about them, and not one of them can find out, and that is how this city says I know where you are and I am not going to say it out loud yet.** Tessa Okonkwo closes the hatch for twenty minutes and reads it as what it is: **not money and not the file, but headcount.** The second that there is a schedule of persons treated in that building, the Wing has a list, and a list means the hatch stops being a place people come who are not on a plate. **She asks him how many people have read it, and answers the count herself from her own day book — nine of the hundred and eleven come to that counter, nine have come since December, six keep an address in Ferrier Street and hold no plate.** Then the leak: **it was asked on the twenty-sixth of March, in a corridor, by a dispensary clerk sent to find something out, and what was asked was whether a clinic that had never been entered in a member's file could be entered in one later, and it was about this building and not about either of them.** Four people deep and three told and nobody broke anything; the answer came out of a chart she signed herself at five to midnight in a washroom on the seventh. **Wren Kallo has been waiting two days to be ruined — since the morning the Board came down and he told her at the rail that she was one of three who could be told — and is not, and cannot be told why, and does not know how to have been spared by a building.** **Tessa Okonkwo signs a second chart at ten past ten at the back of her own building** — the same form, her name and his and the date, and four lines under the date saying the establishment is unfunded, that no schedule of persons treated exists, that any schedule submitted will be incomplete and the incompleteness is the patients and not the clinician, that it has been entered in a member's file once by her on the seventh in a room deliberately, and that the notice is answered there and not at a counter. **The clinic's nine years of being unfunded and unentered are over and a person with a warrant can stand at that hatch from the first of next month and ask a name**, and she says on the way out that the six of them have spent four months not being written down and she has spent nine years making this building a place where people who have not been written down are treated, and those are the same project, and she has known since the fourth of April and went on opening the hatch every morning.

### Ch 179 — The Hostel Room
The twenty-ninth, **the scene, and it is Runa Sato's, and it is immovable.** Her own room in the Field hostel at Cordwain: nine feet by eleven, a bunk, a desk, a chair, a footlocker, a window four feet from the next building. **Her kit is packed and has been since the thirteenth, sixteen days, and it has not gone anywhere**, because a Field operative packs a kit the way a person puts a coat on a hook. **The muster-room roster is on the desk face up, with a rule through R. SATO and nothing under it, because she carried it down off the wall herself.** The ask, in a field register and before anything starts: **she is asking to be answerable for all of them, because a household with a head can be asked to produce a member, the person the request comes to is the head, and if the head cannot produce them it goes back and goes on a list.** She has been produced once already and knows what it costs. **What it costs this time: a Field operative is not a person who works, it is an instrument that can be sent for, and being on the list has been the only thing in the city that could find her, and a thing that is sent for and a thing that answers come down the same wire — so to be answerable she must ask in writing to be struck as available, three months, a signature, and it does not come back, and it is the last document she will sign that says a person who spent nine years in a division has told the division to stop having her.** Four terms in plain statements: awake, sober, untired, nothing given to either of them and nothing to be given; **nothing spent, not on her and not on him and not on anybody in that building; the four ribs named once, not touched, because a person who carries things does not go near a thing that is a permanent instrument; and she will do most of it with her own body because that is what she is for, told in advance so that a man who has been looked after by four other people in four months does not find it funny.** **Fifty minutes are written out, in the room's own furniture and not one image borrowed from Ch 169:** boots under the desk, the tunic over the chair, the body underneath it built in bands by nine years of lifting other people, and what nine years of being carried looks like on a person. She takes the load before she takes anything else, she narrates the whole thing in a low flat register because a person being carried cannot see and must not be startled, she stops him once with *not yet* because she told him in the first four minutes that she was going to come first, the bunk complains once and she does not stop, she is faster than she said she would be, and she comes first and holds on afterwards with about as much weight as she has been doing the whole time. **His name is not used once, by either of them, because a roster does not take a name.** Afterwards she asks him to read her the four lines painted above the muster-room roster eleven years ago and tells him he is the only person who has ever read it aloud to her, and that this sentence is about something she is not going to explain. **She refuses to be thanked, in terms: if he thanks her it becomes something she did for him, and a thing that can be unpaid, and she is going to need it to be paid every time for the rest of her life.** Then she puts her hand flat on the roster, looks at it for about ten seconds the way a woman looks at a thing she has been told to put down, says do not thank me, and turns it face down. She tells him to take the stair, the yard gate is shut from the inside until six, and not to wait at the gate, because a man waiting at a gate is a man anybody can ask about.

### Ch 180 — The Month Ends
The thirtieth, the last day of April. **The standing roll comes up on the wire at the Vestry market and a boy carries it sixty-one steps, and it has to go on a wall** — kept at the premises, displayed, and produced to any person who calls and asks, for the two printed reasons that a person who cannot be found cannot be asked and a person who cannot be asked cannot be produced. **Seven ruled rows. Six have a name and a standing and a band on them and every one of those six wrote an answer in her own hand between two and six on the sixteenth. The seventh row is empty and it is numbered seven and there is nothing in the other three columns.** Above them, in a heavier line, THE HEAD OF THE HOUSEHOLD, and under it his name in his own hand. **Sun Meiling puts her hand flat on the board: that is mine, and the Office wrote my row out for me because I am the seventh person of reading age in that building, and it is going to be on a wall.** She decides it goes on the landing at eye height, where anybody who comes up sixty-one steps can be shown it — not in a drawer, not in the notebook, not under a table — **because a household that cannot be reached is a household that can be lost in instalments by people who never have to say they took anything, and she signed a stub on a doorstep on the thirteenth and that was the cheapest door in this city.** It goes up over the pencil: the sentence from the tenth of April is still on that wall four feet below the roll. **Ola Bright writes six lines and a seventh into the copybook, the seventh being him, and it is the first time his name has ever been in that book in any position, and his signature had been refused three times.** At two o'clock he presses his plate in the socket on the ground floor of the Fourth Branch: **eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight, three grains above a floor that has not moved, eleven thousand eight hundred and sixty-two under a band that is sitting on paper in a room he has not been in since the seventh of April.** **The roll has six names and a space, and the light at half past five stops about two inches above the seventh row, and it will be there for sixty days.** Runa Sato puts her kit down under it, which is the first time in four months anything has been put down in that house rather than carried. **Agnete Lindqvist comes up the sixty-one steps and reads the whole thing and says there is nothing for her on it, and that this is correct and she has been awake about it since the twenty-third.** Wren Kallo comes at half past eight because she comes on the last day of a month: **the leak stopped on the twenty-eighth and no confidence was broken and she has spent four days waiting to be the reason for something and was not**, and she will not put it in Ola Bright's book.
