Ends with the certificate at the end of sixty days coming to that address because his name is the first thing on it — not a rank, not a band, not on the Board on any Thursday, but a piece of paper that says six women live at an address in Ferrier Street under a head of household, findable by anybody in one line, for the rest of his life — and the plate at eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight, and the light two inches above the seventh row.

### Ch 181 — The Re-Issued Form
The first of May, and **THE CARD IS PRINTED AND M+5'S LENGTH IS FIXED FOR EVER.** The card by the time clock on the ground floor of the Fourth Branch reads in full: **MAY. THIRD. TENTH. SEVENTEENTH. TWENTY-FOURTH. AND THE SEVENTH OF NEXT MONTH.** The twenty-fourth plus seven is the thirty-first and there is no thirty-first in this month, and the men who paint the wall every Thursday would not print a day that does not exist, so **May has thirty days** — proved by the card exactly as April was proved by Chapter 151's. And it checks itself against a fact it did not produce: April's card ended *and the third of next month*, and the men took Ivo Sarn's tile off the Reading wall in a crate on the twenty-sixth of April, and seven days from the twenty-sixth is the third. So the third is a Thursday and the first is two days before one, and **the word Tuesday is never written**. Then the second paper up the sixty-one steps in a month, and **it is not a question**: the district holds two returns on one address, the earlier cannot be acted on, and it is re-issued so that one line in it can be corrected — **in one hand, with the name of the person giving it at the foot, on or before the seventh, and it does not say which member.** Ines Duarte reads it out twice: the second time is the one that matters, because the second reading is where you learn that a form which wants a signature is the only instrument in this city that makes a household produce one of its own members. **Six days, printed twice.** Runa Sato works out in nine seconds which line it is, because the standing she entered on the eighth was true on the eighth and stopped being true on the twelfth, and says she will write it at a counter in her own clothes and will not tell anybody what it says first, because that is the instruction on her own doorframe. **And Ines Duarte points out the thing nobody else has said: the standing is not the only thing on that roll that has not been told about.** Ends on two days to the third and a rectangle on the Board that has been bare for five days.

### Ch 182 — To Be Withdrawn
The second, and **the first time in this book that a person has asked to become unfindable on purpose.** Beatrix Halm comes off the line at half past six, goes straight up the sixty-one steps instead of down to her own room, and says *take me off it* on the eighth step. **Her reason is her own and it is a good one.** The roll is the only thing in this city that pairs an address with a person in nine minutes, and it has been on that wall two days with a man's name in his own hand above six names, **and she is the only one of the six who writes things down** — eleven months of it, since the June before last, the line and the hour and the section of line, in a hand that gets steadier when it is bad — and on the seventeenth of April she read a hundred and eleven names out loud in a room off Ash Row and there is a printer in Bellhouse who knows the name of every cabin on the Ferrier line. **The kitchen argues and loses, in order.** Nadia Ferrous makes the best argument in the book and Beatrix agrees it is the best one anybody has made at her in four months and that it is in the wrong direction: coming off is how you get to be a woman who chose once, and Beatrix would rather be unfindable and writing than findable and not. Ines Duarte names what he has done to himself in four volumes in one sentence and Beatrix says she is not asking to be like him. **At about eleven that night Ines Duarte strikes one rule from the left margin of the sixth row to the right edge, dates it, initials it, and writes WITHDRAWN BY THE MEMBER**, with the door open behind her so that anybody coming up can watch, which was not required. **It costs her the only protection the roll gives her: from that evening anybody may serve a requisition at that address and there is nothing above her but a row with a rule through it.** Ends on the light stopping two inches above the seventh row and the ruled line being the lowest legible thing on the board.

### Ch 183 — The Bench Order
The third, **a Thursday, and the Board repaints**, and the pale rectangle is bare for seven days. The white-bearded man hands him a bucket without being asked and says a tile that has not been claimed is a claim. Anneke Roos is on the third step for the fourth week running and says *ceramic*. **The bench order comes down off the bench on the wire at a quarter to eleven, one side of a sheet, and it is about this household:** a roll kept at premises and produced to a person who calls is to be produced, and where it is produced the person, the hour and the number of names and spaces upon it are to be entered on the district sheet and sent in. **A roll not produced is not an offence. A roll produced and not entered is.** Wren Kallo's reading: it does not make the roll findable, it makes the roll *used*, and a caller needs no warrant and no reason and no standing of any kind — a caller only has to be a person who calls. **Then she says she has a name, and that she is not going to tell him what it is.** A chart has a read history, the Wing keeps one, she is in that office, and on the morning of the second she saw four entries on the chart of the seventh of April and the fourth of them is a woman of band four in the establishment section who opened it twenty minutes after it was filed and whose name has been in Wren Kallo's head since. **It takes the fourth link out of four chains that ended with somebody losing a place, and she is not going to spend it.** She has read four records this morning and written down not one line, in a building where that is the only thing anybody is measured on, and she has started counting things that have no shape, and **she says brightly that it is killing her and that this is not a figure of speech**, and she says the pale rectangle will still be there on the tenth, and he does the subtraction before she finishes the sentence and she tells him that is the only arithmetic either of them wanted to do that morning.

### Ch 184 — The Long Form
The fourth, and **the batch's irreversible decision, and it is his.** Ten days after he stood in a corridor on the third floor of the Reading, he goes in his coat at twenty past nine and asks at a counter for a form. **The certification schedule is four pages and has to be done in a room in front of a clerk**, in a room ten feet by fourteen with a clock a minute fast and a clerk called Doyle who has been doing it for about thirty years. **Page one asks how many persons of reading age keep an address at the premises.** He writes six; Doyle points out the roll says five and that the premises answer is not the roll, and lets him have the stroke of the pen twice, and writes *one person is not on the roll* in the margin as a permitted answer, and says he is the first person in four years to use it. **Then the standing, and Doyle tells him the figure is optional and that about one in four of the heads who comes through that room leaves it out and gets it filled in by the office out of what the office holds, which is his plate.** So he writes **eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight** into a legal document for the first time in five years, and Doyle reads it back with his thumb on a line and says, once, the whole of what it is worth: everyone at that address is standing behind that figure, and about one in three of the heads who come through that room has never understood that it is the figure. Page two is the members, five of them, written from what he has heard them say about themselves in rooms and not what any of them would have written, **because a sentence in pencil on a landing wall on the tenth of April says answer it in your own hand and do not consult each other, and he did not ask one of them anything.** The last of the five is a Field division at Cordwain, which is false, and he knows it is false, and he signs it, and Doyle says *not that one*, initials the old date, and then says the thing that matters: **when the correction comes on the seventh both dates will be on the sheet and anybody who reads it in nine years can see that there were two, and that is the only protection this office offers anybody and it is offered by accident.** Fifty minutes in the room, forty-one minutes of work. At the door Doyle writes down that he attended, and says he would like to say that at the door and not in the room. **Ledger 118, nine turned, nothing spent on anybody, the promise intact.** Ends on the fact that there is now a piece of paper, in a clerk's hand, saying he came, ten days after a figure of two against a column headed persons off the public stair that had not been him.

### Ch 185 — The Service File
The fifth, **a Thursday, and it repaints**, and the pale rectangle is still bare two days after Wren Kallo said it would be. **The batch's midpoint, and it is Nadia Ferrous's.** Page nine of a district lift service file has a box at the bottom of it headed INCIDENT AND CIRCUMSTANCE IN THE COURSE OF DUTY that has been empty for four months, and a row of about two hundred entries going back four years that nobody has ever asked her about, and **page nine is the only place in this city where four years of a woman being unable to leave is written down in a hand.** Her forty-one names are in four documents and **not one line in any of them says she chose them**, and there is no instrument in the city for it. A service file is the only document she has ever had that goes anywhere on its own — it has gone to a counter in Cobb Street once a week since the tenth of March, eight weeks, without a single person asking. **So she writes an address and a head and her own name against the premises into page nine, in ninety seconds, standing up in a car at the change of shift at ten past two, in front of nine operators, and reads it out loud to them.** She says it in the kitchen first, four attempts at the reason, **and says out loud that he is not the reason and not the occasion: if he were not alive she would have written it in March, in the same words, handed to that counter with the same eleven seconds in front of it.** Ines Duarte says the best idea anybody has put at that table since the tenth of April and then says the one sentence that is in her head — a page in a district book does not put her choice anywhere, it puts a household where the Exchange is entitled to read it once a week for ever — and cannot find a better argument. **Nadia does it in the car because in this room it would have been the house's decision and she wanted the one where it was hers and it was ugly and there were nine witnesses and none of them asked her a question.** Ends on a receipt with a number on it and a woman asking for something that is not about the price.

### Ch 186 — Absent With Reason
The sixth, and **the most expensive emotional movement in the volume so far, and it is Sun Meiling's.** A four-line sheet up the sixty-one steps: the schedule of the fourth records six persons of reading age at the premises and five members, and the sixth may be entered as a member **or** as absent with reason, the reason to be given by the person and in that person's own hand. **The household offers her the first door and she refuses it in front of five women and sends the man out of the room before she goes.** She goes to the Reading alone, at half past eleven, and Doyle takes it while she is standing there, because she has never handed a piece of paper to a clerk in this city and is not going to start by leaving it on a tray. Four hundred words, dated, timed and signed, ending on the distinction that cannot be argued with: **being the person at whom a paper is served is not being one of the people it is served on; I chose the first on the thirteenth and I am not going to be moved off it by being offered the second as if it were a reward.** She does not use item eleven and says the word *no* when Doyle looks at the margin. **She is now in the record, in her own hand, with her name at the top of it, and the seventh row of a board on a landing is still empty, and she has made certain of that in a clerk's queue.** Marguerite Vale prices it and Sun refuses to let her; a shape comes back for a twelfth time in the queue at ten to twelve and she reports that it came back and refuses to say what she thinks it means. **He waits an hour and fifty minutes in a building he cleans every working morning and is not allowed as far as the stair.** Ends on nine days of the sixty gone and fifty-one left, which is the one countdown in the batch that was always right and is the convention the other three are now measured against.

### Ch 187 — No Entitlement Established
The seventh, and **the answer to everything the household thought it had bought, in seven days.** Two things come up the sixty-one steps. The first is a slip saying the schedule of the fourth **cannot be certified** — not refused, not returned, simply not certified, while the sixty days run from the twenty-seventh of April whether it is certified or not. The second is the rail at the junction, where the Exchange's monthly permission circular carries a line it did not carry on the fourth: **the entitlement of a standing household is the entitlement of its head, and where the head carries none the household carries none, and no permission may be sold to a household on the account of any member of it** — printed above **NO ENTITLEMENT ESTABLISHED**, which has been printed against him since December. **Runa Sato goes to the counter alone with her correction and signs it in four sentences: the division, the refusal on the twelfth, the rule on the strength wall on the thirteenth, the household from the twenty-seventh, not on a roster, and a document she has not yet signed.** She comes back and says the thing nobody has said: **nothing any of them has done has bought a single thing at any counter in this city, and that includes the page Nadia wrote into on the fifth and the rule Ines drew through a row on the second.** Then Marguerite Vale says the fourth thing, at a kitchen table, and prices it: **a standing household is bound by the highest standing in it, and the highest standing in that household is not his and has never been his, and it is hers.** Nobody asks her for it. He says so out loud in Chapter 190. Ends with three days to the second Thursday on the card and the certificate fifty days off, being ten of the sixty gone.

### Ch 188 — The Unfunded Clinic
The eighth, and **Tessa Okonkwo's decision, and it is a clinician's and not a speech.** The hatch opens at eight and there are twenty-one people in the queue and one of them has a warrant, and **he waits his turn for two hours and forty minutes** and does not go to the front and does not ask. At half past ten he puts a one-sided sheet on the counter, dated the seventh of this month: **an establishment unfunded, in connection with the file of a member, in which that member is entered as having been treated — a schedule of persons treated is required.** No name, no address. **She reads it in eleven seconds and works out that it has narrowed eleven establishments to one and that the Office did not have to know which one it was.** And that she cannot refuse it, because a thing that is true and not answered gets decided by somebody who has never been to her building. **So she answers it, at the counter, in the queue, in four minutes: between two hundred and three hundred persons treated on an ordinary day, a day book produced to a person holding a warrant on the production of her sheet, and a refusal — no person treated will be named to any person who has not come through that hatch, no person treated will be named in any document that leaves the building — and then the fourth line, which puts a band-five member of the Bureau and that building and her in one sentence in a district file for the first time, and she chose to be aware of it at eleven o'clock in the morning instead of at four in the afternoon.** A woman who has come to that counter every morning for nine years reads the card and asks whether she now has to give her name, and is told no. Ends on the strongest thing the building has ever had: **a card on the counter with her name and her hours and the rule that she will answer for the building and not for the people in it**, and it was got by answering a notice with no name on it in a queue.

### Ch 189 — The Signal Cabin
The ninth, **the scene, and it is Beatrix Halm's, and it is immovable.** A cabin at the far end of the Ferrier line: eight feet by eleven, a window on three sides, a stove in the corner, thirty-one levers along the north wall, the block instrument ticking on a shelf, and a road clear for the last two miles because there is nothing on it. **A records clerk of twenty-six years' service, who has known about the second book for two years and has never once asked, comes out at four with a folder she does not open and says one sentence that is hers and not the office's: an office cannot protect a record it has never been told exists.** The instruction itself is short and does not say by whom. **Seven days after she struck herself off the roll in a hand with a date on it, there is nothing between her and a request from her own service that is not even an order, and it is put to her kindly by a frightened woman in a company coat, and that is what a correct decision looks like seven days later.** She tells him the true reason she has not been able to write in the book since the seventeenth of April and not the one she has been giving the kitchen: **she cannot write it, and the only date she gives for it is the seventeenth of April, and she gives four seconds of the first page to a man who has not asked her one question about it since the seventeenth and burns it.** Five terms in plain statements and not numbered; the ribs are not mentioned at all; nothing is given to either of them; nothing spent. **Explicit, unhurried once it begins, about two thousand words, in the cabin's own furniture** — her hand flat on the bolted frame above his head, the four seconds when she puts her hand over his eyes, the floor and the stove nine inches from his foot, the lamp swinging twice in the hour, the four deliberate seconds. **She narrates it nine seconds ahead of the movement because that is what she does to a person being carried and it is not a kindness**, she says his side is not like his right side and does not want to know what is on it, and **neither of them uses his name once, because she talks to the line and not to him.** Afterwards she refuses to be thanked in terms, says it is the only sentence about her she has ever wanted to say in a room, and gives him the ninth of September again and refuses to say what it is, on the grounds that a woman who has just thrown that away has no business holding anything else she cannot say the price of. **Her last act is eleven months in a stove**, spine last, about four minutes, and then a woman with her hands in her pockets goes down sixty-one steps on the outside of a building and does not say one word on the way down.

### Ch 190 — The Household Roll
The tenth, a Thursday, and it repaints, and the pale rectangle is bare at fourteen days and the painter has stopped saying anything about it. The sheet goes up on the wire with the week's figures and he does not read it, because he has not read one since the third of April. **And at ten past eleven a woman called Nunn comes up the sixty-one steps past the chop-house with a card and asks for the roll, which is exactly what the printed instruction at the top of it says happens, and exactly what the bench order of the third says is to be entered when it does.** Sun Meiling comes down four stairs and tells her she does not have to come up, and the woman says she does, because it is produced where it is kept. She reads the board for four minutes without touching it. **Five names. Two spaces.** One of them has a rule through it with the second of this month on it in a hand that is not on the schedule, and the word WITHDRAWN BY THE MEMBER. Nunn enters the hour, the person, the number of names and the number of spaces in the ordinary way and asks nothing. **And then she says the thing that is not hers, to the whole landing at once, because all of them are standing there and it is better it is said in a room than found out in a fortnight: the roll on this wall is not the instrument, and there is no instrument in this city by which a member can come off a certified schedule between its filing and the issue of the certificate, and the only way is a fresh return, which does not amend the first and stands beside it and opens its own sixty days.** So the woman who asked to be unfindable on purpose is unfindable on a board and entirely findable in law, and it was done to her by a clerk doing her job accurately on a Thursday morning. Ines Duarte says the sentence anyway and Beatrix Halm says *do not*. **Then the certification's arithmetic, said out loud on a landing because he promised himself on the first of this month that he would: sixty days from the twenty-seventh of April, three days to the end of April, thirty days in May because the card by the time clock says there is no thirty-first in it, and twenty-seven days in June. Three, and thirty, and twenty-seven. The certificate is dated the twenty-seventh of June.** Sun Meiling says her row is hers and is empty and that there is now a piece of paper in a district file with her name at the top of it and both of those are true at once and she made sure of it in a clerk's queue in her own hand. Ends on forty-seven days, the certificate dated the twenty-seventh of June going into a post while nobody in that house does a single thing, and the only instrument that could have changed a figure on the schedule being a fresh return that would be dated the tenth and run to the tenth of July, change nothing, and be a second filing on that address — the thing that put the re-issue through the door on the first — and that nobody asked for, and that a woman had told the rest of them three days earlier she could provide.

### Ch 191 — The Certification Rule
The eleventh. **A second caller comes up the sixty-one steps with a folded sheet** — the bench order of the third made the household's own roll into something a man with no office and no standing can come and read, and he reads it from the top and counts **six names and one space** because he reads a rule through a line as though the line were not there, and Sun Meiling makes him write the hour on his own sheet in her hand and sign it himself, and the address has been given to a stranger. He does the arithmetic at a sink on the fourth floor: **fourteen of the sixty gone, forty-six left; ten days to the end of the month and twenty-seven in the next is thirty-seven days to a certificate that cannot arrive.** Then he asks the right question in the wrong order, and Doyle tells him the true thing. **A certification is not granted on a filing; it is granted on a reading** — four pages, a room, the head asked member by member out loud what each member is and where it is evidenced, a clerk writing down what he heard. Where it does not agree the schedule is **held, not refused**, and held is alive and every day held is a day off the end of the sixty. **A standing is only enterable against a document**, and he has five on page two and Doyle can find the source of four that afternoon and does not say the fifth. **The office will not read a member before the head has signed that he assumes the house**, and the instrument is one side of a sheet that has been on the wall behind Doyle for thirty years and is on nothing the Office sends out: ASSUMPTION OF MEMBERS, six ruled lines, and at the foot printed in the Office's own type **THE HEAD OF A HOUSEHOLD ANSWERS IN ITS OWN NAME FOR EVERY MEMBER OF IT.** **The pad is numbered and an unused pad is destroyed at the close of a month, so the twentieth is the last day, and it is the eleventh.** Doyle refuses the pleasant date and gives the real one, says the certificate is the twenty-seventh of June and the schedule goes up the working day before, **and the reason is institutional and not calendrical: the Office does not sit on the twenty-seventh of a month and there is nothing about it on a card** (under the derivation fixed at Ch 201 the twenty-seventh is a Tuesday, so the weekday cannot be the reason and is not used as one anywhere), and then he says the unwelcome thing: **a head who signs is made askable, and the privilege for being asked is a band five, and a paper can now reach him by writing to him first.** Nine hundred households have come through that room; about a hundred and forty have a head's answer; he keeps the numbers in a drawer and will not be asked about them in a corridor by a man who cleans washrooms.

### Ch 192 — On His Own Account
The twelfth. **A requisition of property — a table and two chairs — served at the premises on the head and on no member, on his own account**, with a box in the corner for the hour a person might be asked before they were touched. Ines Duarte reads the trap in the second line: **the paper is not about the house at all, it is about him**, and a man with a plate cannot be served at premises, so it follows him to the Fourth Branch, where forty cleaners will hear it read out. Runa Sato says it plainly: it was served on six women through his name because his name is the only one it could find. Sun Meiling says he is one number on two pieces of paper and neither is a certificate. Beatrix Halm: **you cannot take a plate off a band five in nine days, so they will not try — they will serve it on the man, at his work, in front of the other cleaners, and watch what he does.** The decision is to leave it on the table, and he goes in at seven, does his floors, **and reads the requisition out loud in the entrance hall** — his name, the form number, the date, the nine days, the premises, the words *on his own account* twice, practised at a sink that afternoon — and then answers four questions at the counter at the end of the day, one of them from the mail clerk, who tells him the difference plainly: the ones with a member on them get taken quietly; **the ones with a head on them get a room and a question and a document with a name at the top of it, and everybody in that room has to write down what they think, and the cleaners hear about it in the hall for nine days.** On the landing at ten: **fifteen of the sixty gone, forty-five left; eight days to the twentieth and nine days to the twenty-first, and they are not the same date and only two women in that room know it.**

### Ch 193 — One More Refusal
The thirteenth. **The loss, and it lands on a woman who is not one of the seven.** A third sheet with no name on it, printed on the eleventh, in force from eight, requires the register of attendance within two hours, and the register is nine years of an hour and a count with no names in it, because that is Tessa Okonkwo's defence and it is a real one. She produces it in the room, standing, with the queue behind her, and the line for the seventh of April reads: **MEMBER PRESENT AT THE HATCH AT CLOSING. NO ATTENDANCE.** It is the only line in nine years with a member's word in it and it is in her hand. The man reads the hours off the card she put on that counter on the eighth — the hours are on it because she wrote them there — and works out that **a quarter to eight, every morning, for nine years, in a building with one door, is a person**, and says so to her, and she refuses to name her. **Nobody stopped the woman.** The refusal is correct and changes nothing. She will not stop her coming and will not move the hours, because the hours are what two hundred and three hundred people a day plan their day around, and because a sentence with a building in it and no name will get the name filled in on an ordinary working morning by somebody who will not feel it. **She priced her own name against that building on the eighth and did not price this one, because in nine years she had never once looked down the queue and counted who came at the same minute.**

### Ch 194 — The Fourth Rib
The fourteenth, the clinic, and the hardest chapter in the batch. **Tessa Okonkwo puts her hand on the fourth rib.** She states the reason out loud first — if she touches it and something moves, she is a clinician who cannot un-know it and will spend her life unable to write a chart on him with her own hand out of it — **and the reason has not changed in the five weeks since the washroom and she says so before setting it aside.** The reason she sets it aside is professional and true: **in five days she is going to answer for a building in a room, and she will not do that with a hand that has never been put on the thing that brought the man to her hatch.** Ines made him say the location out loud before she would put a thumb on it and Sun Meiling never touched it and made him say the number three times, so she makes him say it three times too, and **the third time is the one that counts and she does not tell him why.** Then she does it the way she would do a knee, counting along from the angle with a finger, and under the hand nothing arrives. She writes the date and the hour, and the frame, and the heart, and the mass that has not moved an ounce since the seventh of April. **She writes the fourth rib and writes that she does not know what moved in it.** She signs it, tears it off, does not give it to him: **it is hers and it goes in the day book and nowhere else**, and in about nine years a woman in an office will read it and will not know whether she did it or whether it happened to her. She is not going to tell him what she thinks it is — she has a chart, and the chart says the fourth rib. **This is a clinic scene and not a seduction: no sex, no draw spent on anybody, adults awake and unrestrained, and the ribs are not the point of it, the record is.**

### Ch 195 — Nothing Said At All
The fifteenth, Ines Duarte's, and it does not contain what it is named for. **Five member forms go round the kitchen table** — Doyle's rule from the eleventh made real — each wanting what a member is and where it is evidenced, in her own hand. Marguerite Vale prices the problem before anyone else can: **every one of them has written their own name into a document they chose in the last ten days, and none of those five documents evidences a standing; they evidence a decision, and there are five days between now and the twentieth to work out which is which.** Runa can answer it, Tessa can answer it from a chart that has already put a member of the Bureau and a building in one sentence, Marguerite can answer it from the register of brokers. **Nadia Ferrous cannot: a service file records where she works and not that she chose it, and she says so in a flat voice, and she is the one who made herself findable on purpose.** **Ines leaves hers blank with her name on the corner, face down, squared with the edge of her hand, and says the reason is not modesty and not a position and not a price and that she is not going to explain it to him either, and the schedule holds on her line as well as on Nadia's and the certificate does not issue.** Sun Meiling refuses her signature on Marguerite's behalf and is right and it does not matter. Marguerite says the price out loud anyway and says she is not going to be thanked. **And at the end of the day, on the fourth stair, she tells him why he did not ask her — that he made a rule of it once on the twenty-second of April and kept it for twenty-four days, and that he is therefore building a house around the space where she does not say it — and she has read every page of it without looking at the review once, and she knows what that is worth.** Her review is not resolved. Her two refusals stand. She files nothing.

### Ch 196 — The Exchange Floor
The sixteenth, and **nobody in this book has ever been inside the Exchange.** A street of clerks' offices, a name cut in the lintel, a stair that smells of oil, and a sales floor that is not a counter with a man behind it: forty desks, a rail, shelves to the ceiling, forty lamps, and **one window at the far side with its shutters up and the light coming through in a low bar across the boards.** A clerk prices him before Marguerite Vale has finished introducing him and then reads the true answer from the margin of the circular: **a permission is a question you are allowed to ask, and to be allowed to ask you have to be described, and the account of a household is the account of its head, and where the head carries none the household carries none, and no permission may be sold to a household on the account of any member of it.** It is not his conduct. It has never been his conduct. It is a blank in a schedule and there is nothing on the third floor that will sell to him. And then the part Marguerite has not said out loud: **she is rank two, a member is inside the household, and the standing of a standing household is the standing of its highest member — which is hers — and nobody in that house asked her for it.** The clerk tells them both the shape of the thing: a broker whose standing is on a household's face is named on the annual examination and after about four years the examination belongs to whoever holds the book, **and the question is not whether you sign, it is whether you have looked at who is standing in the room when you do.** She decides nothing in a room with a ledger in it. **Nineteen of the sixty gone, forty-one left.**

### Ch 197 — Marguerite Vale
The seventeenth, **a Thursday, and the Board repaints**, and the pale rectangle is three weeks bare and the painter has stopped saying anything about it. The card by the time clock printed the seventeenth and the seventeenth is the third Thursday on it. The Exchange shuts at six; the sales floor empties backwards off the rail; and at twenty past six there is one lamp on the rail and one bar of light through the shutters. **The scene, in an aisle two feet wide between the desk and the rail.** The terms first, all of them, and the reason is thirty years old: **she is looking for the one asset in Ostara a court cannot seize, and a house is not seizable, and the examination is what anchors it** — bought with her own entitlement, which is the only thing that can be spent, entered against her name, and after four years it goes to a woman called Rask who has never met any of them. **Sun Meiling said no on the fifteenth at that table and was right, and it does not matter, because she is not going to be asked.** Her terms of the fourteenth of March stay down, unsigned and out of force, said once in the third person, and not put back. Then the scene in her own discipline — she prices and she does not count seconds: a chair turned with two fingers, three asks and a fourth she asks for, unhurried and at a distance, one moment of stillness in the middle where she says *that is the one I have been waiting for, do not tell me what it is*, and afterwards she stops before he does and says the half she is keeping is worth more than the half he can have whenever he wants it. His name once, third person, priced, as an item in an account, at a figure she will not say because then the figure would be his and the figure is not his. **Her last act: she writes one line in the Vant's real ledger with the pen still chained to the desk, tears the page out along the perforation, folds it in four and buttons it into her pocket. HOUSEHOLD, FERRIER STREET. The first line in that book in nine years entered against herself.** She leaves the lamp on the rail, because eleven years of leaving it off in a room she has stood in is a habit and not a principle.

### Ch 198 — The Invoice
The eighteenth. **A single sheet, no form number, in a better weight of paper, and that is the first thing that tells Ines Duarte to read it twice.** Dated the sixteenth, headed EXAMINATION OF STANDING HOUSEHOLD — PROFESSIONAL FEE, and the value column does not contain money. **It contains a rank, and the rank is two.** The description is six members and one absent, and only one person in the city could have written it, in a ledger with the pen chained to the desk, and he was in the room. It is not a bill and not a rank purchase: **she bought the examination, which is entered once a year against a standing, and after about four years the examination belongs to whoever holds the broker's book, and there is a woman called Solveig Rask who holds four guilds' books and has never met any of them.** She says the reason out loud and it is not the Vant: **a house is not seizable, a house survives an argument, and she has been looking for nineteen years for one thing that cannot be taken off her in a court.** And when Ines reads the bottom half — which she does in silence, the way a person reads a document they intend to be cited from — there is a second line: **on the death, resignation or replacement of the broker of record, the examination passes to the holder.** It frightens her, and she did not see it until a woman read it out in her own kitchen, and it cannot be un-entered. **It belongs to her, not to him, and she says so, and she asks not to be forgiven, because a person who has to be forgiven has not made a trade.** Twenty-one of the sixty gone, thirty-nine left.

### Ch 199 — His Own Indemnity
The nineteenth, **the irreversible act of the volume, and it is a signature.** The front door at twenty past nine, in daylight, in the same coat, on the same walk as the fourth. The same counter. **A second chair on the wrong side of the table that was not there on the fourth.** Four pages of the schedule squared beside one side of cream-weight paper: ASSUMPTION OF MEMBERS, **three spaces** — a head, a date, an hour — six ruled lines, and at the foot in the Office's type, printed and not written, **THE HEAD OF A HOUSEHOLD ANSWERS IN HIS OWN NAME FOR EVERY MEMBER OF IT.** He gets one paragraph of wanting — he has wanted this since the first of April — and Doyle's finger goes on the line. Four questions, all answered out loud, because a head who signs in silence has not read it. Six names, read from the schedule, the sixth entered by Doyle in his own hand as accounted for because she sent him out of the room and was not going to have her name said and signed for her in front of a clerk. **He writes his own name, small and even and not shaking, and the date, and the hour: twenty-five past nine in the morning.** Then Doyle reads out what he has bought and what it has cost: **six people get one question, and the question is worth three grains of margin, and he has made himself the easiest person in Ostara to find and he was the least findable for four years, and a paper can now reach him by writing to him first, and he is bound by the highest standing in it, which is not his, and he was told that in a kitchen on the seventh and did not ask a single question, and the number on the certificate went up by one this morning and he keeps the numbers in a drawer.** At the door, as a man and not a clerk, Doyle asks him one thing that is not in the tray: **do you know what you signed.** He had known since the seventh, at ten past one, in ninety seconds, priced out loud by a woman who was not thanked for it. He says yes and it is not resolved and it is not going to be. **Twenty-two of the sixty gone, thirty-eight left.** And in the inside pocket, on the good-weight paper, two names: his at the top, and hers at the foot, dated the seventeenth, which he had not known was on it.

### Ch 200 — A Household Of Seven
The twentieth, **the volume's last day.** The roll on the landing: **seven ruled rows, six names, one space**, a head's name above them in his own hand dated the twelfth, and a sixth row with a rule through it and the second of May and an initial and four words in Ines Duarte's hand. **The roll is not the instrument and the instrument is four pages in a tray with his name at the top of page one and six members on page two and, since yesterday morning, an assumption clipped to the front of it.** Marguerite Vale says it is six names and one space and that in thirty-seven days the seventh row will be a number in a column and a man in an office will see it empty and will not ask why, **because there is a box on the schedule of the sixth of this month that says ABSENT WITH REASON in four hundred words in her own hand, and that is why — a preference is not evidence and a statement at a counter is evidence.** Ines counts the household out loud on the landing and says which list is which: **seven women, six of whom keep the copybook under the table, Sun Meiling who keeps nothing and is on no list, and Agnete Lindqvist who sleeps in this building and is on no list of any kind in this city and has not been on one since she came out of a room she had not been outside in eleven years — and there is still no place for her, and she is the auditor and she has read every instrument and not one of them will hold her, and she says so once, on a landing, because not one of them asked her.** Then **the roll goes to the counter** — carried down by Sun Meiling to the ground-floor door that the rent comes to — and comes back an hour later with a card she has ruled herself: **the date, the hour, the street, six names, one space, the space numbered and not filled, and a head who signed an assumption at a counter in the Reading on the nineteenth, written and dated and timed and signed at the foot in her own hand.** She says the reason she is not on that roll is now **a fact about a legal document and not only a choice**, that it will be read by a stranger in a building she has never been inside, that she has no way of taking it back, and that it costs her something to hold and she is not going to be thanked, **because a thing done for a door does not get thanked, it gets logged, and there is now a card and she has written it and it is hers.** Runa says the plate is still at eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight and has not moved a grain in five years, and there is a margin of three, and there is a warrant, and there is a name at the top of a page, and there are five names on a wall and a space that is a woman's, and a man who has no rank at all. The lamp goes off; the light stops two inches above the seventh row, as it does every night since the thirtieth of April. **The certificate would be dated the twenty-seventh of June and would go into a post by somebody who had never met one of them, and not one person in that house would have to do a single thing for that to happen.**

---

## Volume 5 — The Auditor's Price

### Ch 201 — A Paper With His Name On It
**The twenty-first, and the volume's anchor, and the batch's spine.** The card by the time clock still says MAY, because cards turn over on the first, and he reads the fifth line for the fourth time in twenty days and works out what June is from it: **four of the card's five entries are days in this month, the fourth is the twenty-fourth, the card does not print a day that does not exist, and this month has thirty — so the day after the twenty-fourth is not in this month at all, and the next day the men are due is the first of next month, and the card says the seventh, and it is six days out.** That gives June: **the first is a Thursday and the others are the eighth, the fifteenth, the twenty-second and the twenty-ninth.** He says it out loud on the landing to Sun Meiling because he promised himself on the first of this month that he would do every figure out loud in that house until the day he did not, and **twenty-four of the sixty are gone and thirty-six are left and the certificate is dated the twenty-seventh of June and is not due today.** **Then one more date, which repairs a premise printed in Volume 4: the schedule goes up the working day before the certificate, the working day before it is the twenty-sixth, the twenty-seventh is a Tuesday, and the Office does not sit on the twenty-seventh of a month — has not in nineteen years of cards, and there is nothing about it on one. There is not one person in that office who will ever have to be standing at a counter on the day a certificate issues, and that is why it has never once felt like a deadline. It is going to start feeling like one today.** Then the post comes up sixty-one steps and **the third thing in it has his name on it and not the address**, typed in a hand he has never seen, with an office and a floor and a second address that is neither Ferrier Street nor the Fourth Branch. He stands in the doorway a minute and a half before opening it, **because not one person in four years had written to him, and a clerk told him on the nineteenth of May that this was what the indemnity bought, and this is that.** Inside: **DECLARATION OF MEMBERSHIP IN A STANDING HOUSEHOLD**, one side, cream weight, a form number, the premises filled in in the Office's type and the head's name filled in above it — **his own, typed, before he ever saw it.** Three questions per member; the head certifies the members and the count of persons of reading age; and at the foot, in a size made to be skipped, the clause about a certificate that does not agree with the schedule of record requiring the head to state **the figure and the reason.** Returnable at a named counter on or before the last working day of the month, and there is no thirty-first, so the last working day is the thirtieth, **and the intake shuts at four.** Nine days. He puts it on the kitchen table face up and Ines Duarte tells him to read the whole thing out loud including the small print, which takes him a minute and a half and which she found in four seconds standing behind him. **He ends by handing it to her and asking her to read it aloud, because it is her office's paper and it is his signature at the bottom and he has never been any good at a form.**

### Ch 202 — The Fifth Line
**The twenty-second.** The card comes down off its nail in the basement and gets read again, which he did not need to do, and he says out loud **why the last line has never been read by a living man: it is the line that tells the men when to come back and the card turns over before they do.** There is no repaint due on that wall for another two days and he has known it since the twenty-first. The week's sheet comes off the rail at the junction and one slot on the Reading wall is bare and has been since the twenty-sixth of April, and **a hole in that wall is not a list.** He comes up the sixty-one steps at half past six and Sun Meiling is on the landing in her coat with nothing in her hands, because a woman is at the door of the ground-floor flat and **she will not open it on her own, because the instruction on the doorframe is about papers and this is a woman.** Marit Kolb, district, forty-five, a card, nineteen years, entered to ask a roll kept at premises and to write down the hour, the person asked, the number of names and the number of spaces. **She is the third caller of the month, after the district clerk of the tenth and the man with the folded sheet of the eleventh who read a rule through a line as though the line were not there.** Sun Meiling volunteers the figure on the bottom step before she can be asked it, out of habit, from her mother — **six names and two spaces** — and it is the first time the figure has been right and she has been carrying five and two in her head for four days. Kolb says *you are the seventh row.* **Sun Meiling tells her she has heard it and is not going to say one word about it and is not going to be civil about it either, and Kolb writes six names, two spaces on the card without changing her hand, because changing your hand when you write a number somebody has corrected tells the number something about itself.** Then the declaration is on the table with two more papers beside it, the read-aloud of the four questions, Beatrix Halm's standing column empty in her own hand and Ines saying it is neither a forgery nor a preference and no office will send anybody for it in thirty years, and **Ines reading out that the third question is hers to answer and that it will go up to the audit intake at the end of the week inside a district envelope and not one person in that office is going to tell her about it before she reads it in the file.**

### Ch 203 — The Fourth Question
**The twenty-third, and the caller's second visit.** Kolb comes at ten past seven with three days' warning she says because she was told to and not because she thinks it good of her, and she comes up because she is told to, which is different. Four questions. One: the number of names and spaces — **six and two**, and Kolb writes it. Two: whether the roll carries a head's name and in whose hand — **it does, in his own hand, dated the twelfth.** Three: **the number of persons of reading age keeping an address at those premises**, which has been on a form since the twenty-second of March and was answered six both times since — and **the six look at one another and not one of them says a word, and the not saying takes four full minutes, and in four minutes a woman with a card works out a great deal.** Nadia Ferrous says from the kitchen door that Kolb has not asked it in a form where the only answer that finishes the card is a number, and the number is not the answer, and the answer is who. Kolb turns the card round, writes one line, and shows it. **Nobody in the house will answer it, because answering it hands a stranger the standing of a woman who is on the roll, and so the only person who can answer it correctly is the woman it is about.** Ines takes the card out of Kolb's hand without asking and writes on it in a small even hand: **Yes. One. Ines Duarte. Rank two. Auditor.** She does not put the office's name on it, because a card with an office's name on it is a card about an office. **It took her four seconds and it has taken her four years to arrange everything else in a working life so that she would never have to**, because the one advantage a woman who can read any record in this city has is that she is almost never a subject of one. Question four is not the district's: it asks whether any person keeping an address there holds any standing, office or entitlement in a body corporate, guild, exchange or office, and will produce the instrument. **Marguerite Vale answers it from the second stair with her gloves on — rank two of the Vant of Brokers, the instrument is a quarterly return at the Exchange, you may have the copy when it is lodged and not one hour before — and Kolb does not ask her a single follow-up and says she will be back in three weeks.** Nadia tells Kolb what she is carrying down sixty-one steps, in full, and says she would have asked. **The card goes up at the end of the week into her own division, which raised those premises on the twenty-first and has a rule that says an officer is not told about her own division's index sheets, and Ines Duarte stood on a landing and looked at an empty row and did not know any of it yet.**

### Ch 204 — The Quarterly Return
**The twenty-fourth, and the only Thursday in the batch, and nobody is standing at the bottom of a ladder for it.** The Vant's quarterly return goes in early, because she decided on the fourteenth of March never to leave a filing to the last day of a month and has not gone back on it in nineteen years. **She lodges six days early, and a return is due on the last working day of a month and every broker in this city lodges on that day and not one of them lodges early, so she has the north desk to herself** — four people at it, two of them clerks, and the clerk is Kirsten Mahler and she reads everything back. **Item one is a standing household at premises in Ferrier Street, members six, entered against the broker of record and not against the head of it** — and she notes aloud that that is unusual, and it is the only lawful way round, because where the examination is not entered against a standing it is not an examination, it is a letter. **The head has a name, and she types it, and she says his name out loud once and is not dramatic about it: no band, no standing, head of the household by an assumption signed at a counter on the nineteenth, and she tells the clerk she could go and read the schedule and would advise her not to because it is a four-page form and does not repay the walk.** That is the first time in nine years a man with no band at all has had his name entered in that book, and she says so, and says she took four days to decide whether she could live in a house with it in there, and that she can. Value: **open-ended, annual examination, renewable, no fixed term, and the second line underneath saying that on the death, resignation or replacement of the broker of record the examination passes to the holder.** Mahler says a clerk cannot enter whoever. Marguerite Vale makes her try, and the clerk types, in ninety seconds, what she could not have done in nineteen years: **THE HOLDER OF THIS BOOK ON THE ANNUAL SITTING, WHOEVER THAT IS.** Receipt, initialled. Mahler then says the one thing she is not supposed to have an opinion about: **a rank two bought an open-ended security out of her own entitlement three days after buying it out of her own entitlement and lodged it in her own book, and a security with no named holder has one buyer and it is whoever takes the book next, and it is worth about what a rank two is worth to a person who has never had one.** Marguerite Vale does the sum on the step outside, out loud, in the one place where nobody objects: **it makes her book worth more to her successor and worth less to her by about a rank two, the sign of that number has been the same since the seventeenth, she has been telling herself the difference was small, and the difference is four years.** She puts the receipt beside her copy of the indemnity in her coat, two pieces of the same shape and weight about four months apart in her life, comes up the sixty-one steps at one, tells the room in ninety seconds in a flat voice, and Beatrix Halm says nobody at the Exchange is going to read it and Marguerite says the Exchange reads everything and does not have to understand it, and **that one day a woman will be sitting at that desk who has never heard of any of them and will read her handwriting and price it against her successor.**

### Ch 205 — The Sheet She Would Not Complete
**The twenty-fifth, and Volume 5's first scene, and it is immovable. 3,553 words.** A message of four words and a time, READ IT ALL. TWENTY-FIFTH. ELEVEN. **Her rooms on Lantern Row, two floors above a laundry, eight feet wide, a table under the window, a chair on each side, and a shelf of eleven years of ledgers she bought herself.** She has not lived under his roof since the first week of April and has given no reason. **She has the whole of the household's record on her own floor in date order — twelve instruments across eleven dates — and two of them are in neither the notebook nor the house.** She reads all of it aloud, once, slowly, the way a person reads a document they intend to be cited from: the composition return of the twenty-second of March with the head's space empty; the re-issue; the four pages of the fourth; **Runa Sato's correction of the seventh, held up for three seconds with a thumb on the paragraph and set down without being read**; the invoice with RANK TWO in the value column; **the second line that frightens Marguerite and applies not to the woman signing the sheet but to whoever holds the book after her, which is a woman called Rask with four guilds who has never met any of them**; the assumption and the printed indemnity read aloud in a clerk's voice. **The two district sheets contradict each other and one of them is wrong: the tenth says five names and two spaces and the eleventh says six names and one space, and the eleventh is the miscount, because the man read the board the way a man reads a page and counted straight through the rule that went through the sixth row on the second of May, eight days before either caller came — so the roll did not change between the two visits and can now honestly be counted three ways by three people doing their jobs properly. The card says six names and two spaces, in the house's hand, and she wants that written down.** Then the thing her gift found: **her own division raised those premises on the twenty-first, and she is the auditor of record on it, and there is a rule that says an officer is not told.** And then her own line on page two, sixth up from the foot, where the instrument column has been empty for ten days because the member form with her name in the corner is lying face down in a drawer at Ferrier Street. **She does not say why. It is not about this house. It has never once been about this house. And you are going to let that stand for a long time.** **What she wants: to be entered on the declaration on his word rather than on an instrument — the weakest standing available to a person in this city, voidable, gone the day his plate goes, resting the certification of six women on a man who cleans six floors — because she has been so proud of not being in anybody's book for four years that it did not occur to her until the twenty-third that being outside a book is not the same as being safe in a room.** She says: *you are the first person who has ever offered to carry a piece of paper with my name on it; I would like to be carried.* And: **you are not going to keep me off that sheet, and it stops tonight, and the office is going to have to decide what to do about me in a room with a door on it.** Adults, awake, unrestrained, unmedicated, no draw spent on anybody, consent in plain statements before anything started, violence and sex in separate registers, and it is not careful and it is not fast and it goes on a great deal longer than either of them expected. Afterwards she counts out loud and it is eleven seconds and it was nine last time, and he says she can stop counting and she says it is the only thing she does. **Her last acts: she takes the member form out of her bag, says she is not filling it in and is not handing him a sheet with her name on the corner and nothing else in it, tears it once across and gives him the two halves; then she copies all twelve instruments into a book that is not the household's notebook and has nothing on its cover, makes one mistake, which is a comma, rubs it out, writes it again and initials the correction, and writes WITHDRAWN in the instrument column against her own line, and means the instrument and says so before he can ask.** The first copy of that record ever to exist outside that house is on a shelf in a flat on Lantern Row and she is the only person alive who can read either of them properly. **She ends on six or five: that is the only question left in this house, and he is going to answer it in daylight in front of all of them, and he is going to answer it wrong, because the right answer is whichever keeps the most people safe, and they will tell him what it costs them, and that is not the same thing, and it has never once been the same thing.**

### Ch 206 — Off The Strength
**The twenty-sixth.** Runa Sato goes to the Field division in Cordwain at nine because a division opens at eight and closes its counter to anything but returns at half past three, and she wanted a woman at the counter who had been there all night. **The file comes out on a string that has been retied twice.** Her correction is on the second leaf exactly as she wrote it. Then the clerk tells her the thing he is required to tell her before he is told to tell her: **everything on a page in that file has been read since the seventh, which is normal, and what is not normal is that on the twenty-first an audit intake asked this division for a document about a person, and we gave it to them because we were asked for it in the form, and this division has never been asked for a document by anybody who was not owed money by one of ours.** **Produced and returned means a copy, and the copy is on a district sheet, in a week he cannot name.** Eleven people in a corridor, a man about a pair of boots, and a bench. **And the answer to what is on the strength of that document: nothing — a standing you have got somewhere else, which this division does not hold and cannot check.** She says it out loud anyway: **she has got a man's answer at a counter; he is band five with eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight grains and a margin of three; and the household's standing is a guild broker's, because the examination is entered against the highest standing in the house, and in about four years the book goes to a woman called Rask and every one of them is standing on a document belonging to a woman who will not be here.** Then she goes home and makes the room read the second question out loud, and works out the **load order, bottom to top: a book, the examination entered against it, the broker who holds it, the broker's rank two, the head whose standing is nothing, then six members who rest on the head's signature** — and Sun Meiling asks her to stop saying her name in a sentence where it sounds like a favour, and Runa Sato asks to be at the bottom of the top: **if anything in this house comes off, it comes off her, and it does not come off Sun Meiling and it does not come off Ines Duarte, and she wants the whole chain written down, not a woman's confidence — her book.** Ines reads the foot of the sheet in a second and a half and tells her there is no box for a chain, because the form has three lines per member and nobody in this city expects an instrument to fail four years from now. **They have nine years and four years in the same kitchen and Ines does not say which of the two goes in the column.**

### Ch 207 — Three Grains
**The twenty-seventh.** Tessa Okonkwo comes in at ten past eight, off the hatch at a quarter of eight, nine years of an hour and a count, and reads the declaration from the foot up the way a clinician reads anything. **Eight thousand one hundred and thirty-eight, band five, three grains short of the band below your band.** A standing household is certified at the figure of its head because there is no other figure to certify at, so this is a band-five household with a tolerance of three and six members hanging off that three, which is not a number, it is the width of a plate. **When one member is challenged the certification does not fail for that member; it goes into question for all of them. Fifty-three days, from the fourth of May to a certificate, during which nothing in the household draws on anything. She has had to write that letter to eleven families in six years.** Then she says the hard thing: **the certificate does not make this household safe, it makes it visible; you cannot have a safe house and a certificate, because one is a document and the other is the absence of documents and this city cannot read a thing that is not there.** And she wants one anyway — **two hundred and three people go through that hatch every morning and not one of them is in anything, because the clinic has never once been entered, and a certified instrument in this address in June puts all of it into somebody's question**, and she says both sentences out loud on purpose. **Agnete Lindqvist is at the back of the room with the door open** and Tessa catches herself saying nine years and addresses her by name. Marguerite Vale, asked flatly as the bottom of the chain, gives the answer: **the examination passes to whoever holds the book, it cannot be un-entered, it has been in a quarterly return at the Exchange since the twenty-fourth with the head's name in her hand and the holder entered as whoever shall be holding the book at the sitting.** **And she is the load-bearing wall in a house with a three-grain margin and has made herself so in writing, in public, in a document a stranger will price in four years.** Nadia Ferrous says her line is a service file with a change of shift in it in ninety seconds and **it records where I work and not why, and this is not the document that fixes it.** Beatrix Halm counts it up: two lines that do not stand up, one with no instrument, one withdrawn in a room on Lantern Row, one arguing with a clinician, and a sixth row with a rule through it and a seventh row with nothing on it. **And the trade: six matches the certificate and gets Agnete's question asked by a stranger in a fortnight; five keeps the certificate clean and puts a sworn head's figure into a file beside four hundred words written in a queue, and the two documents disagree in public for eleven years.**

### Ch 208 — A List Of Six
**The twenty-eighth, and the batch's irreversible decision, and it is his, and it is about who is on the file.** He says the figure standing up in his coat before anybody has put anything on the table, with the subtraction attached because Ines has never once had to ask. **Thirty-one of the sixty gone, twenty-nine left, two days of intake, the counter shuts at four.** The figure is six and the line six is going to be a name and on the twenty-seventh of June there will be a certificate with six members on page two and that certificate does not care what he writes at a counter on the thirtieth. **And there are two figures and both of them cost Sun Meiling her row.** Six: his hand, his signature, her line is a member, and in eleven years a woman in an office reads a head's sworn answer beside a queue's statement in her own hand, and the head's answer wins — not because anybody read them and disagreed with her, but because there is a man's name at the top of one and not the other. Five: the sworn list contradicts a schedule of record forever and the only true reason available is **the four hundred words she wrote in a queue on the sixth of this month**, and he would have to write them out in his own hand. **Both ways the four hundred words come out of her hands and into his.** Runa says six and tells him to say the rest of it. Ines says six and puts it on the record of the room in her own words: **six agrees with a schedule of record that cannot now be amended by anybody, and five is the only figure on that sheet this office would treat as a second filing on an address, which is the thing that put a re-issue through the door on the first of this month.** Nadia says six. Beatrix says six and says do not choose five because you think it spares her, because that is what she did in April in nine seconds to spare herself and it was the same mistake. Marguerite says six is not hers to want. **And Sun Meiling says: you may not do it for me.** Not because he will hurt her — because she has priced it both ways and chosen the way in which he does not have to write down what she wrote in a queue, and dressed it as sparing her, and it is sparing him; **the document is hers, she filed it, she timed it, she stood in a line for it, and the sum he has been doing all month is not six against five, it is hers against mine.** She tells him to write five. He says no. **Not because six is better, but because a man who decides for six people once a month will decide for them again in September, and again at Christmas, and again when the guild sits — and because he would rather be the man who took her row off her than the man who took her words out of her hand and put them in his.** He says six. She says, *then do it on the thirtieth at about half past two so that you are early, because a man who is early looks like a man who is sure of himself and I am not going to give you the satisfaction of being early by accident.* **Thirty-one and twenty-nine, and there is no subtraction left to do, and after tomorrow there is a number on a document and it is going to disagree with every other number on that wall for as long as any of them is alive.**

### Ch 209 — The Seventh Row
**The twenty-ninth.** He goes up to the Reading square because the men had been on that wall five days ago, on the twenty-fourth, and he had not, and for four years he has stood at the bottom of that ladder on every morning they came. **There is one Thursday left in this month and it was five days ago.** The slot is still bare and it has been bare since the twenty-sixth of April and a hole in the wall is not a list. **At the foot of the ladder on the pavement is one of their buckets standing by itself, and the white-bearded one is sitting on the kerb beside it at half past eight, on his own time, about a bucket he should not have come back for, and he would rather not be asked about it.** And then the thing he has come up to say, once: **there is going to be a name in that slot. Not this week — this week's list has got nothing in it and the list is not wrong — but the slot belongs to a man who was at the certification bench at band seven and the bench does not empty itself, and when the tile goes in there every person on that pavement reads it at about a hundred and forty thousand people a week, and there is not one instrument in this city that takes a name off a ceramic tile.** He gets in at half past six. **Thirty-two of the sixty gone, twenty-eight left, one day of intake, four o'clock at the counter and the queue from ten past three.** At eleven he goes up and the seventh row has a name in it: **SUN MEILING**, in pencil, in her own hand, dated the twenty-ninth, and under it, in a slightly smaller hand because she ran out of room, **I ASKED FOR THIS ROW AND I FILLED IT AND IT IS MINE BY ASKING.** Four feet below, undated, with no name near it, is the sentence that has been on that wall since the tenth of April: a paper comes to me and I answer it in my own hand and I do not ask anybody else in this house what the answer is. **She answers a document in her own hand and does not ask anybody else, on the twenty-ninth, at about ten at night, with a pencil, nine hours before the counter shut and a day before he signs.** Why: **if the wall says five and the file says six, then a woman with a card comes up sixty-one steps and writes down five, and both numbers go into a district file, and in about six years somebody works out that there is a woman at Ferrier Street who is a member of a certified household and whose roll does not carry her — which is how it started in the middle of this year, with a woman whose regularity was in an office because she wrote her own name on a card in four minutes.** He says he was not going to touch that row. She says do not do the thing he did on the twentieth, standing on the wall telling the truth about her in front of the room and making it sound like a gift, **because in about nine years that is going to be the best thing anybody did in this house and he was going to be able to say so, and now she has taken it off him, because the roll has to agree with the file or the roll is a lie kept at premises and produced to a stranger.** She says she wants it written on the wall as well, by her, tonight, with the pencil, **because in eleven years the only person who is going to be believed about that row is her and she will be far too old to remember standing there and the pencil is not.** And: **you are going to sign a piece of paper tomorrow and I have filled in a row tonight, and those are two separate things with a day between them, and I have made sure of that and I have not told anybody which day.** The light still stops about two inches above the seventh row.

### Ch 210 — Six Names And No Spaces
**The thirtieth, the last working day of the month, and the declaration goes in.** He asks for four o'clock and gets half past two, because the last working day of a month is not a day anybody in that building works to a clock. Seven people in the queue at three, most of them with the look of a person who has been putting this off since the first of the month. **He fills it in standing up against the end of the counter with a pen off a string.** Six in the box, in the ordinary way, not the way a man writes a thing he has argued about. Then six lines and eighteen entries without stopping once. **One: Ines Duarte, rank two, the head's assumption of the nineteenth and the schedule of the fourth, office held: the audit division of this city — and he did not have to be told, because she wrote it on a district clerk's card on the twenty-third, the card is in an intake in Cobb Street, and on the twenty-fifth she read the whole of it out loud on her own floor in the flat voice of a clerk and he was in the room.** Two, three and four short. Five: Marguerite Vale, rank two, the quarterly return lodged on the twenty-fourth, item one, entered by hand against the broker of record, and the examination passing to the holder. **Six — Sun Meiling, no standing entered, the schedule of the fourth and the head's assumption, and no office, no standing and no entitlement in any body corporate, guild, exchange or office of this city** — which took four seconds and is the only one of the eighteen he had said out loud in a kitchen with a lamp on, in front of the room, two nights before. Then the count of persons of reading age keeping an address at the premises: **six, because it has been six since the twenty-second of March and it has never once been wrong.** His name in his own hand, the date, the hour — **two forty-one in the afternoon** — and a signature at the bottom that took longer than any other line, which Doyle would have read back to him and did not. A woman at the counter read all nine words back anyway, and noted that line six has nothing in the middle and one has a guild on it, and is not supposed to have opinions, and put the tick, and said **there is one tick on every form of this kind in the city that carries it and she has never seen two.** **The small print at the foot was the only part of the sheet he did not need**, and there is no line on that sheet anywhere saying a man had come within one name of putting his hand on a woman's own account, and in eleven years a woman will read a head's declaration that agrees with a certificate and have no way of knowing how close the two figures came to not agreeing. **Then a slip about two inches by one: 34-6-118, the date, and the word LUMPED. The household has a number in that building that is not his.** He reads it three times on the pavement, because **a number is how a thing is found, and a number is not a name, and a name is findable, and a number is findable in a way that a name is not because a number requires nobody to know that it is the right number.** It is the fastest way into a building anybody has ever thought of and he has gone and picked one up for himself in a queue. He comes up the sixty-one steps at twenty past three and Sun Meiling does not get up. **Six names, one head, and no spaces anywhere on that wall for the first time since the second of this month**, in pencil, in the two inches of dark under a light that has stopped in the same place every evening since the thirtieth of April. **He does not read it. He read it three times the night before, and a man who goes up sixty-one steps to look at a sentence he has got by heart is a man who has come up here to be seen.** **You cannot read that from the bottom of the stairs** — and neither can a district clerk, and in four days a woman with a card is going to have to come closer or ask somebody to hold a lamp, and that is going to be the first time in nineteen years that she has had to ask, and there will not be one column on that card for the reason. **Thirty-three of the sixty gone, twenty-seven left, twenty-seven days to a certificate with six members on page two of it, and one of them is me, and I did not have a say, and I asked for the row, and those are both of those things at once and they are going to stay both of those things at once.** And in an office two floors above that building, on the last working day of the month, a woman who raised his premises on the twenty-first reads the slip with a tick beside the sixth line, and because the rule she works under is that an officer does not certify a household she is a member of, opens a second book, and writes four words in the third column that have never been written in that building about a person who works in it. **SHE IS ONE OF OURS.**

---

## Volume 5, Batch 0002 — The Enforcer (Chapters 211–220)

### Ch 211 — The Monthly Call
**The first of June, a Thursday, and the day the May card turns over.** The card on the nail in the basement of the Fourth Branch reads JUNE. FIRST. EIGHTH. FIFTEENTH. TWENTY-SECOND. TWENTY-NINTH. AND THE FIRST OF NEXT MONTH, and **the last line is right this month**, because the twenty-ninth is the last Thursday in a month of thirty days — and last month's said the seventh of next month and was six days out, and the difference between the two cards is that one man has now read it. **At the Reading wall at nine, eight days after they were last there**, the white-bearded man tells him what nobody tells you: the ranks wall is the wall, and the **board at the side of it is eleven feet long and is painted once a month on the first, before the figures go up at ten, and carries the standing households** — number, standing, schedule date — two hundred and something lines where there were a hundred and eighty in the spring. Then **Hanne Voog**, assessment, nineteen years, comes up sixty-one steps at about eleven with a docket, because **a broker of record who has entered a security against a household must produce on the first of each month a copy of the roll of the persons answerable**, and the roll kept at premises is not an instrument and has no force, so the only answer to the call is a copy. She stops at the twelfth step because she was told, she copies six names and a head off the pencil board in ink at about eleven in the morning when the stair window is at its best, **the seventh row last, and she does not ask anybody to hold a lamp**. She values it, and says the number out loud on a landing because she is frightened of saying it in a room, and the number is the yield of a word — *produce* — said four times a year by whoever holds the book: six names at the top of a column and one at the bottom with a number beside it. Sun Meiling is not angry with him. She is angry that she was right. **What she bought in pencil on the twenty-ninth — that the wall and the file would not disagree by one name — is the only reason the copy is worth anything, and it is now in ink in a book a stranger will read on the fourth of every month.** Marguerite, at the foot of the stairs: *come up to the rooms at four, not the Exchange, my rooms, and I will tell you the one number in that book that is not in hers.*

### Ch 212 — The Best Room In Ostara
**The second, and the angry chapter, and she is right.** Her rooms on the third floor above the Exchange, two chairs, a table with nothing on it, shutters at half height.
