She entered the examination on the twenty-fourth because a house is not seizable; on the second of June a woman came up sixty-one steps and read six names off a wall in ink, and **a senior broker of the Vant of Brokers has made six named women the most liquid thing in a book that has been dead in eleven places since the nineties, and did not see the shape of it in four minutes at a window.** She is angry at herself and she hands him the reason to be angry at him: *you are the reason it is priced. Not me.* **A book is not worth what the securities in it are worth; it is worth what a man with four guilds and an afternoon will pay to be handed it in four years, and what he pays for is the ability to produce six names, one head and a standing at his own desk without a court, without a hearing and without anybody's agreement. And in thirty years she has never had a man with nothing at all make better security than a man with something — he is the best collateral she has ever entered in that book, and it is because he is worth nothing.** And then the underneath: **there is an account in that ledger entered against the broker of record in the year the Veil broke, never discharged, never written off, secured for eleven years by a person rather than by money**, and there is a name against the line, and it is not hers, and she will not say it today. She will say it on the fourth, and he will not like it, and he will not be able to do anything about it. On the sixty-one steps: *I am the only one of the six that copy carries with nothing under my name at all, and a blank in a copy is a question somebody is going to ask me in four years in an office I have never been in.*

### Ch 213 — The Woman In The Doorway
**The third, and ten past seven in the evening, which is the exact night Ch 210 named.** Marit Kolb, nineteen years, a card, four columns, up eleven steps and stopping at the twelfth both times. **Thirty-six of the sixty gone, twenty-four left.** She counts the roll twice at two different hours and comes up six names and nil spaces both times. She asks who filled the seventh row and is told, and is told *in pencil, on the twenty-ninth, in my own hand*, and enters it as a member, and does not stand there for eleven minutes asking a member why a member wrote her own name on a board in pencil instead of on a form. Then the fourth column, the premises, put twice — and the room does not answer, twice, and it is not silence, and a district officer who has written *put the question twice and got nothing* on three cards in nineteen years looks at the room at the back where the door is open and says she has never once written down what it was. She writes **six**. And then the woman in the doorway: **Agnete Lindqvist sleeps at this address and does not keep an address here, and Kolb explains exactly why she cannot enter her — the column asks how many keep an address, there is no second column, and a woman who does not keep an address is not an omission in my book, she is the correct application of it — and puts the pen away.** In nineteen years she has never left a line blank. She says she will come up those last six steps on the twenty-sixth, ten past seven, and read that row in the dark, because that is what the light has been doing to it since the thirtieth of April. **At the door, her fourth question, which is hers and not the district's: a roll is nothing, but a roll that says exactly what a head's sworn declaration says is a copy, and a copy can be produced out of the file by anybody holding the file, without anybody walking up sixty-one steps.** And nobody says one word about Ines Duarte, who came down four stairs and stopped, and is not going to be asked.

### Ch 214 — A Promise With No Maker
**The fourth, and Volume 5's second scene, and it is immovable. 3,890 words.** Petra Ilyin comes up sixty-one steps of the Exchange building at four and has been in a collecting room since about eleven that morning, and the door has been standing open the whole time. **She cannot be the first one out of a room — she can leave eleven times out of twelve, and on the twelfth her body takes her back to a table, and it does not feel like anything, because it feels like putting a cup down, and that is the part nobody has ever believed.** Eleven years. She cannot remember who made the promise. She has a docket with four ruled lines on it and asks him three questions and records the answers in her own hand, and **the third one is the one she cannot answer and asks anyway.** Marguerite Vale comes to the door with the chained book under her arm: an account entered against the broker of record in the year the Veil broke, carried nineteen years on money that stopped coming and eleven years on a woman, because it could not be paid and could not be written off and the guild had stopped carrying it; **I made a woman of thirty-four swear to me in a room in this building and I have let her sit in it for eleven years and I did not tell her.** She writes one line in the left-hand column in about eleven seconds with the pen chained to her wrist and does not stop to check it. Petra stands up, gets three steps, puts her hand on the frame four inches above the thumb-turn, and cannot leave an open room for about nine seconds — and says the true thing about her own condition in daylight in a corridor: *an oath of holding is not a bar. It binds my body and it binds nothing else. It has never once stopped me doing anything at all.* What she wants is not him. **What she wants is to be finished.** And then the hour, and then the scene, which is written on the page and was not, until the review repair pass, a paragraph of approach: she does not close the door, she takes her boots off because she means to stay, she tells him in her own voice before anything that she is not being collected, she makes him put his hand over her mouth if she starts pricing again, and Marguerite goes and stands somewhere else. **Then she turns the docket face down and squares it to the edge of the table, and says she is going to do this the way she takes stock of a room for the first four minutes and then stop, and that when she stops he is not to put it together again and make it tidy. She tells him to look at her and say what he sees, and then to say it again slower and not decently, and when he does she says *that is the instrument*. She goes at her own shirt and is bad at it and does it anyway; she gets him pushed down onto the bolted table and stands over him and hands him the inventory; she turns the wall lamp off with the back of her hand because she cannot do anything at all with a lamp on; she tells him to stop and then to start again, both instructions in the flat voice of a woman reading a clause out of a book, and the second time she puts her hand over his mouth to say it and takes it off herself. She will not be carried. She says out loud, in the room and not afterwards, that she has not been under a man in eleven years, and that she noticed herself saying it and went on anyway.** And then, on the edge of the table with her back to the open door, she stops in the middle of a sentence because **nothing has happened**: the thing her chest has done in a room with a door on it for eleven years, about four minutes in, did not happen once tonight, and she finds out that the only instruction she ever had that she could not write down was a clause in somebody else's book, and that is what she came up the sixty-one steps to find. Afterwards she says the coat is on that peg and has been since the day she came up here and she could not take it with her. She gets up at about six and does not look at the peg. Then she stops, and goes back, and takes the coat off the peg, and hangs it up again, and does not touch it. **That stays.** She walks out without it, does not touch the frame, and says his name once at the top of sixty-one steps, which is the first name she has said in a doorway in eleven years and then left through.

### Ch 215 — Eleven Years Of It
**The fifth, the morning after.** **Thirty-eight of the sixty gone, twenty-two left, twenty-two days to a
certificate.** Seven women in the kitchen, six of them keeping a notebook, and the seventh was written
into a guild's month book in ink on the sixth. Marguerite names the account in front of the room: entered
against the broker of record in the year the Veil broke, never discharged, never written off, secured
against a person eleven years ago — and **an officer of the Vant has been inside this building for eleven
years, four times that anybody knows of, on no roll and in no notebook, because a person inside an
obligation cannot also be a person inside a household, and Marguerite made that decision alone and
consulted nobody because there was nobody.** It sits under the whole chain — not a rung on it — and it is
in her own name. It came off the dead list on the fourth at about five in the afternoon and the security
against it is nothing, and **a live unsecured account in the senior broker's own name is the one thing in
that guild that does not sit still and wait for the sitting: they will require security, the demand is
dated the tenth, and there is nothing in that house to give them except a man.** She refuses to ask any
of them for a figure and says so out loud in front of seven women. Petra says she was not wronged — she was
owed a discharge and did not ask, because an officer does not ask — and that she has not asked what the
account is for and is not going to. Runa Sato puts the chain out loud and gets three things wrong in a
row. **Sun Meiling says no**: the certificate will issue on the twenty-seventh upon a standing a stranger
can call, and she is not going to watch the last of it go up a ladder in a different building, and she
tells him she knows the shape of what he is about to do and is not going to be angry in the third person
about it this time.

### Ch 216 — What The Line Does
**The sixth, and the quiet one.** Beatrix Halm comes up the sixty-one steps at seven in the evening with a
notebook, one ear still ringing from six hours in a cab, and puts it on the table face down. **Nine people
at eight in the morning going the wrong way, on the first and every day since, five days running.** She
can feel forty thousand people move on a good evening; she cannot tell you a name, only a direction, a
weight and whether somebody is in a hurry. She tells them what a condition is in that building — *a thing
you are not tested for*, which means you are never found to be all right and never found to be not all
right — and that she has been deaf in one ear since the Veil and it is a switch, not deafness, and that
the whole of the noise of a nine-hour shift comes into the other ear and stays there. Her hearing is a
signal operator's instrument and nobody in her division has ever once found out what she can do. She is
not angry with anybody. **What she wants is to be aimed.** She does not want to be told she is right; she
wants four sentences with anything in them about what to look for. She has been the person this house
finds out something from after it has already happened for four years, and the last four things she felt
got in first, and on the second she felt nine people and said nothing until the sixth. Nadia Ferrous writes
down that she has been in the building four years and not one person has ever once asked her what she
wanted before she told them what she felt. At the door, Agnete Lindqvist, in the ordinary way, in the
daytime, the way she has stood every day this month: *you are the woman a district officer left a line
blank for. Good. The other one made a man put his pen away, and the difference between the two is the
whole of what I do.*

### Ch 217 — Eight Thousand One Hundred And Thirty-Eight
**The seventh, and the batch's irreversible decision, and it is his, and it is the first time he has spent
anything.** The notebook on the kitchen table at a quarter to eight: 07:58. Off the north line. Satchel.
Slow. **Six days of nine and then one.** At nine, **Ortwin Halb**, discipline, twelve years, up eleven
steps and stopping at the twelfth, with a summons and not a demand, and the difference said out loud at the
top of the stairs: *a demand is dated, a summons is a person in a hallway.* At the top she goes to the
wall and puts her hand on the sixth name and takes the pencil off the shelf — **which is Sun Meiling's
pencil, from the twenty-ninth of last month, and is two inches long, and putting her name back in pencil
afterwards is not permitted.** Sun Meiling tells her to write it in ink, all six of them, in the book,
because being permanent on a wall is not the same thing as being permanent in a book and she would like to
find out which one she was actually afraid of. Runa Sato comes up four steps and puts her weight on the
rail without touching anybody, and Ortwin Halb tells her exactly what she would write down. **She writes
six names on the card at nine in the morning on the seventh and puts the pencil back on the shelf with a
flat lead.** Then the kitchen, and the figure said out loud with his hand out of his coat pocket:
8,138, floor 8,141, tolerance three, unmoved in five years, and before four this afternoon it goes into a
chained book at a desk on the ground floor of the Exchange and lodges against an account that has been
dead for thirty years. **Forty of the sixty gone, twenty left.** Three people answer at once and all three
are wrong. Then the desk: the second form, the one that takes a head. **A standing lodged under this head
is released with the book and not by the person who lodged it, may be called by the holder of the book at
any valuation, and the person who lodged it has no right of withdrawal between the lodging and the next
one. A month is a valuation, unless the book changes hands.** The clerk asks whether he has been told by
a person whose trade it is to know, and Marguerite says he has not corrected you. He signs at twenty to
four and a clerk reads the whole of it back to him across a counter, which is the second time in eight days
and the first time he has thought about it. **The copy goes into the chained book in her own hand and the
chain comes round her wrist.** In the street she refuses to say the sentence and makes him carry it: a
man with a plate at 8,138 has pledged the whole of it on an unsecured account in a chained book, and the
certificate on the twenty-seventh will issue, if it issues, upon a standing a stranger can call. And she
will not write the word *because* on the quarterly return.

### Ch 218 — The First Of The Month Was Painted
**The eighth, a Thursday, and everything the decision cost.** At seven on the second floor of the Fourth
Branch, **Lode**, who has cleaned beside him for three years, puts a cloth down and says the name of his
household is on a line in the ledger the man in the plaster hat took through the landing at six. He did not
say it unkindly, which is the part Lin Hao was not ready for. Half the landing has it by lunch, and Lode
says he did not ask about it and would like it on the record from him rather than from anybody on the
floor, because in about a week one of the men down at the gate is going to ask him about it and think he
is being interesting. Dewey gives him half an hour and owes him a free morning in April. **At five to ten
at the district office in Cordwain, the fifth of a series and the fourth postponement, Beatrix Halm's
rating hearing on her right ear: a woman at the counter went through her instrument in about ninety
seconds and there is a line on the back in the Office's type saying the supporting instrument is
encumbered.** A claim supported by the encumbered standing of a household cannot be heard. Postponed to
the first of July, which is the next valuation. **Twenty-three days.** She makes him keep his face under
control for about four seconds and then stop, and then says the thing that is the subject of the whole
month: nobody has told him what the standing is standing on since the nineteenth of May. Then the square.
**The board at the side of the wall is not the wall.** Eleven feet, painted once a month on the first by
a crew that is not the Thursday crew in a shade that is not the Thursday shade. Two hundred and something
lines. **34-6-118, four days up from the bottom, with a name typed small at the head line.** It went up
on the first at about a quarter to ten, by a man in a plaster hat who does not know the wall and paints
from the book, which is correct, and he painted it while his head was somewhere else in the city with his
coat off. The column says the schedule's date and not the certificate's. It does not come down, and if it
did it would leave a ghost, and a ghost on that board is a hole. Nadia Ferrous at the top of the stairs:
Sun Meiling has been going up to that landing with a chair since about ten past nine because in the first
week of June the sun comes round the back of that building and hits it at about a quarter past ten, and
she has not said so, and she is not checking that her name is still there.

### Ch 219 — The Complete Version
**The ninth, and Ines Duarte, and she does not say one word about the twenty-second of April.** A notice of
production is carried up the sixty-one steps just after eleven in an envelope with a form number on it,
addressed to Ferrier Street and not to Lantern Row, and Ines comes in behind it at eleven twenty having
told the man on the bottom stair that if she was going to be handed a document about her own premises it
would be handed to her in front of the people it concerned. She reads it standing up in the flat voice of a
clerk, and the third line names the auditor of record, and the second line says the officer holding that
schedule may not certify a household of which she is a member. She hands it to the room and lets the room
do it. **The certificate is dated the twenty-seventh and the schedule goes up on the twenty-sixth, and the
schedule cannot be certified, so on the twenty-sixth it does not go up and on the twenty-seventh there is
no certificate.** The only instrument in the office for removing a person from a certified schedule is for
an officer with a conflict, and she is the only person on that page with a conflict, and she has been
since the sixth of May, and she put herself on it anyway, and she will not discuss it. Then Sun Meiling
turns the notice over and finds the line above the address that the whole room read straight past: **the
office indexes every schedule a member of the division appears upon, and it has indexed this household
twice, and the two entries do not know about each other, and the rule that keeps them apart is a rule
that says an officer is not told about her own division's index sheets.** An office has built the only
complete version of that record in the city, and Cobb Street has never been inside the house and is never
going to be told what is in it, and the complete version now carries the head's pledged standing on the
foot of it. **That is the safest thing about the household and it is safe because of a form.** Ines asks
for the extract to be left on the table. She wants the copy in the notebook made, and she wants the two
documents to differ, and she wants the difference written down, because she is the only person alive who
can read both of them properly. She says, once, that she is going to ask to be removed from the schedule
— not today, not in a fit of anything, in a room with a door on it, in writing, with a reason attached —
and she asks the room for two working days' grace and for nothing to be asked her, and then asks for the
first standing instruction she has given in this house in four years: **if anything comes to that address
with a form number on it, whoever opens it tells her before anybody discusses it, and it goes in a book
that is not hers.** Nadia Ferrous writes it down and turns the notebook round.

### Ch 220 — Eleven Dockets
**The tenth, the last day of the demand.** At a quarter past ten the account has a standing against it and
the standing is a plate at 8,138, and it took four minutes at a desk and a clerk wrote one word and turned
the sheet round. The account is secured from the seventh; the examination stands; the book goes to the next
sitting in four years without a question about item one. **Beatrix Halm's hearing comes back on the first
of July in writing, at nine in the morning, and she does not celebrate and the household lets her alone,
and from the seventh until the first nothing in that house draws on anything — three weeks and three days,
which is twenty-four, of which twenty-one are still in front of the room and three are behind it,
and it is the first time in five years there has been a date on the good side of anything in that kitchen,
and it is on the good side because a man put a plate into a chained book.** At nine in the morning Petra
Ilyin went back to the Vant on her own, which was the first time in eleven years that going back had cost
her nothing, and came up the sixty-one steps at half past eleven with a coat over her arm that was not his.
The coat was on the peg in a room eleven feet by seven with a bolted table and four pegs and a high window
with a grille and a door standing open. **Eight days**, which is the second of the month to this one, and about four hundred rooms in them. Then she puts a bundle on the kitchen table.
**A hundred and thirty-two dockets, one a month for eleven years, folded twice, every one of them bearing a
production mark in Marguerite Vale's own hand on the second of the month at four seconds apiece, a hundred
and thirty-two marks in a hundred and thirty-two margins, and not one gap in any of them.** Eleven years,
four times in the building, not once. She wants the version in which she is a woman who trapped a girl in
a room to be on the table next to the true one, and she wants it said out loud by somebody who is not her.
Beatrix Halm says the thing nobody has been getting round to: she did not know either, and it took a docket
with a coat in it. Nadia Ferrous asks the only question she has ever asked anybody in that room, which list
are you on, and writes down **none of them**, and observes that between Petra Ilyin and Agnete Lindqvist
there are two people at Ferrier Street on no list of any kind and about twenty-two years of a life spent
on the wrong side of every form in this city. And then the twelfth docket, which is not on the table: she
took it out at nine o'clock that morning before she put the coat on, and it is dated the first of July, and
today is the tenth of June. **Twenty-one days early.** Somebody inside that building already knows what is
going to happen on the first of July, has written it out, and has put it in a coat pocket, addressed to
Ferrier Street, for a household of six with a head whose standing is lodged and answerable. **Seventeen
days to a certificate. Three weeks and three days is what his plate bought on the seventh, that window runs to the
first of July, and somebody inside that building has already written the paper for the day it runs out.**

---

## Volume 5, Batch 0003 — Chapters 221–230. THE NOTICE.

**221 The Weight At The Top.** Beatrix Halm has been aimed since the seventh and the notebook has five
mornings in it: **one line on the seventh at 07:58, off the north line, satchel, slow — and four mornings
of nothing.** Nine a day from the first to the sixth, one on the seventh, then the street went quiet, and
she has been not telling the household for four days. At half past eight at night she takes him to the
corner where the Ferrier line comes round from the north and tells him the thing she has not written down:
**at about twenty to four on the seventh something went into the top of Ferrier Street and it has not come
out, and it is not louder, it is heavier.** He has felt it too — on the Exchange rail at twenty to four,
and on a stair rail at the Fourth Branch on the eighth at about ten past seven with a mop in his hand, and
he told nobody and went back to the sinks. She can find it with a hand on the wall. **There are about forty
signal operators on that line and none of them is her.** What she wants is to be the only one who can find
it, and she has twenty days before the panel asks her what a signal operator who cannot hear in one ear is
doing in a cab.

**222 Seven Where It Says Six.** A sheet with a form number on it comes up the sixty-one steps at about ten
on the twelfth. **Nadia Ferrous opens it, reads the first line, and does not read the second; she goes down
sixty-one steps at a run, catches the tram to Lantern Row, and comes back at half past two — four hours,
because Ines Duarte's standing instruction of the ninth is that whoever opens it tells her first, and the
price of the instruction is that nothing in that kitchen moves until she does.** Ines arrives at twenty past
five, stands up, reads it twice, and says one word: **seven.** The index is constituted from the returned
lines of the district premises returns and not from the totals, and a returned line that is empty cannot be
struck out by anybody who has not got a name to strike it out with. **The seventh line was left blank by a
district officer on the third of June at ten past seven in the evening, the officer wrote six in the foot
of the column, and the office counted seven, because the office is not unkind. The office is exact.** Three
days and about twenty hours of it. The certificate is in fifteen days and forty-five of the sixty are gone.
Ines asks the room one question — is there a seventh person keeping an address at those premises — and he
says yes, and Sun Meiling says *say the name*, and he says it. **Nadia writes the word six in her
notebook and does not put the name next to it.**

**223 What The Division Did Not Tell.** Sun Meiling reads the foot of the correction at eight in the morning
on the thirteenth, and **every alteration of an indexed schedule is countersigned before it goes out** —
which nobody read on the twelfth. The counter-signature is **Wren Kallo**, counter-forensics, the
thirteenth, with no requisition number anywhere, because a correction raised by an office against its own
arithmetic has no author. Ines reads the position aloud: two instruments four days apart, one about the
schedule and one about the premises, **and the second one is about a district officer who left a line blank
and not about a man who wanted something.** Agnete Lindqvist stands in the open doorway and asks for the
one thing nobody can give — **if a name is ever written on that line, let it not be written by the Office
and not by any of you** — and is told, on the page, that a premises return is the officer's and that she
has been checking once a month since May whether there is a provision for a woman to write her own line on
one. The door stays open. What the lead wants is to know what Wren Kallo saw before she decides what to do
about it, and there is no address to write to and no list she is on.

**224 Six Persons Of Reading Age.** He writes six on the fourth page at about nine at night on the
fourteenth with the whole house in the room, because he said on the twelfth that he would do that and there
is no instrument in this city that lets a man go back on a thing he said in front of a room. The page
takes a figure and nothing else. **It is answered in the head's own name and is answerable against his
standing, four times in nine lines**, which means the lie goes to Cobb Street *and* to the chained book
that holds his plate until the first of July, and in about four years it belongs to a woman called Rask.
**Forty-seven of the sixty are gone and thirteen to a certificate.** Ines Duarte comes round the table,
reads it in about four seconds, and asks him one question — *is it six* — and he says *it is what I am
writing*, and she says *then it is six*, and goes up the stairs and says nothing else all night. Sun
Meiling says she did not have a vote and would have voted against it. Petra Ilyin says the price of this
page is that the head has made himself the only possible criminal in a document about this household, and
that on the first of July there is a valuation and a docket with an empty space at the foot of it and a
plate in a chained book, and any one of those is ordinary and all three on the same morning is a paper
somebody wrote out and put in a coat pocket three weeks early. **She carries the page to Cobb Street on
the sixteenth because she is on no roll of any kind.**

**225 A Bench Under The Wall.** The fifteenth is a Thursday and the crew is working the tile around the
mounted standing-household board, and a man on the ground keeps saying four words at a ladder. **Wren Kallo
is on a bench under the wall with a folder.** She countersigned the correction because that is the standing
order and a countersignature is a check and not a signature, and she read the history of his file afterwards
on her own time. **A correction raised by an office carries no requisition, no author, a finding, a figure
and a stamp, and the only thing on it with a person in it is her name. She cannot unsign it and she cannot
hold it, and those are two separate problems and she has solved neither in four years.** One person knows,
since the eighth, and she will not say who. She counts the household's lists out loud and gets five of the
eight out of a file. She asks to come back on the **eighteenth**, at night, with the folder, and not to the
house, because she is going to say things about people who will not be in the room. Then the man at the
foot of the ladder tells him that **a name cannot come off that board with paint, that it takes a
correction, and that a correction on that board leaves a hole about a yard long at the low end**, and that
the men who were on the crew when it happened still talk about it in the tea hut and not to them.
**He also says there is one woman in the city who can see what was painted over, that she works for nobody,
and that he saw her at the side of a whole building in a yard behind a laundry about two years ago. That is
Wren Kallo, and Ch 228 pays it off: she finds 34-6-118 on this wall with her hand, in the dark.**
**Forty-eight of the sixty gone, twelve to the certificate, sixteen to the valuation.**

**226 Nothing Moved In It.** Tessa Okonkwo's quiet chapter. He comes to the Vestry trade door at ten past
one on the sixteenth and she puts him on the chest and counts four ribs along from the angle with her own
fingers, out loud, because a clinician reads out loud — and then she does the thing she set aside on the
fourteenth of May and examines **her own** fourth rib for eleven minutes with both hands and finds nothing,
and writes it in the day book with her name and the time on it. **She is frightened: a clinician who
cannot find the thing is a clinician who has been treating a symptom, and a symptom is something you can
keep giving away.** Then the certificate question, four minutes, answered: **the correction is an index
made out of returned lines, and this clinic has never once been entered in anything, so it is safe the way
a hole in a wall is safe.** She has a card, on the back page of which she entered nine years of attendance
on the thirteenth of last month, and a card is not an instrument. **She has decided to enter the building
properly**, and to tell him in a room and not in his kitchen, and to put a clinic on the same page as his
answer of six in the same office under the same address — and to carry a crate with him on the eighteenth.

**227 The Space At The Foot.** Petra Ilyin takes the fourth page to Cobb Street at twenty past one on the
**sixteenth**, where a clerk enters the figure, initials it, and says nothing, and there is no copy; it is
indexed on the seventeenth, which is the run Ch 224 and Ch 225 already had. She does not sleep much after it. Then she
goes up sixty-one steps of the Vant with the bundle and reads the ninth page of the rule book for forty
minutes. **A docket is answered at the foot by the mark of the holder, in ink, in the holder's own hand; a
docket that reaches its date unmarked is not produced; an account not produced at a valuation goes on the
dead list on the fourth of that month and every security standing against it comes off on the day it goes
on.** And the thirteenth docket **was issued out of the June run and dated forward into July, with its
number entered afterwards in a smaller hand above a ruled line**, out of a run that is one a month for
eleven years with no gaps. Ortwin Halb meets her on the second landing, tells her the one thing she is
allowed to tell a person who is not an officer — **that on the ninth an office that is not this guild
asked this guild for the production file of that account, and it was refused in four minutes by a woman at
a counter who had never heard of it** — and sends her home. He has been standing at the bottom of the
steps for twenty minutes. **Four things, said from the fourth step down, and she will not say who wrote
the paper. Fourteen days to the first, and three after that.**

**228 The Shape Of An Edit.** **THE SCENE.** Wren Kallo, the eighteenth of June, the Reading Square after
ten at night, at the foot of the ranks wall under two lamps that go off on a clock at eleven. She has been
there since a quarter past nine. **She countersigned the correction, and she read the history of his file
at a quarter past eight in the morning on her own time, and she has written up what she found, and it goes
to the head of her division on the twenty-third and there is no version of her job in which it does not.**
The finding: **four edits, and every one of them a removal** — a figure off a band in March, a line out of
an instrument column and a half form in its place on the seventh of April, a quantity detached from an
account on the twenty-seventh, six lines onto a declaration on the nineteenth of May — **and not one
addition in five years, and a record like that is a record somebody has had access to, and it is not his.**
She asks for four hours and a half with nothing in it, which is the only thing she has ever wanted off
another person, and she explains why in her own instrument: she cannot keep a thing, it is structural, she
has carried his edit round like a stone in a shoe for four years, and she is so tired of reading the shape
of a subtraction in every person she touches that she has come out at a quarter past nine to sit on a wall
before he got there. **The scene happens on the kerb in coats and then not in coats, with two hundred yards
of empty square between them and the Reading doorway, and it is explicit and unhurried after the first
hour and a half in which neither of them says anything, and nothing in it turns on an impaired state.**
**He says yes after eleven minutes and says the terms back to her so that she has heard them said, and
offers to stop knowing he will not be told whether she is stopping; she answers yes and says she is
frightened of it and that the two are the same thing tonight. The act is run through her discipline: she
reads the history of a record for a living and cannot stop reading one in a man, **holding still is a
different shape from not moving**, and she stops him with a hand over his mouth twice because she is
listening to what happens underneath the words. His want is stated once — he has spent four years being
the thing other people edit him through and wanted somebody to look for what was taken out of him and fail
to find it.** She reads him with her hands for an hour and finds nothing over and over. Afterwards, on her back on the
kerb under four feet of dark tile, she tells him the thing he does not want to hear: **on the twenty-sixth
the schedule does not go up and on the twenty-seventh there is no certificate, and he is going to have to
lose somebody in the next nine days.** Then she stands up, finds his name in the dark wall with her hand in
about two seconds, and gives him the reason: **there is no light on that wall after eleven o'clock and there
has not been for twenty years, and darkness is a thing a city switches off at eleven, and it switched it
off on him for the last four hours while she was standing there.** She will not say who she has told since
the eighth. She says his name once, at the end, as a clerk reads a line out of an index.

**229 The Roll Comes Down.** A guild's envelope sits under a salt dish from eleven until twenty past two on
the nineteenth because everybody in the house walks past it. **Hanne Voog of the assessment side has valued
the security early, because an unpriced security cannot be carried a year running, and a security cannot be
valued without a description of what it secures.** Marguerite Vale signs it in ninety seconds in the
kitchen and reads six names out loud in order and the last one is her own. Sun Meiling says *two* — two
documents in eight days, both naming the women on that floor, both made by institutions, neither asked for
— and then says the part that is hers: **on the seventh she got a chair and made a wall and a file agree
with each other so that neither of them was a lie, and that is what let them do it.** She goes up and comes
back with the roll and cuts it at the ruled lines in about eleven seconds, gives him the head line, keeps
the seventh row in her own pocket, and puts one piece of paper in front of each of them. **A roll kept at
premises has no force, and three copies that agree with each other are one document in three places, and
after this afternoon there is not one copy of this household in this city that anybody in this house
controls.** Nadia Ferrous, who has kept six notebooks for four years and has said *which list are you on*
about twice a month for a month, writes in hers: **the lists were never the problem. The lists were the only
thing in this city that belonged to us.**

**230 The Twenty-Sixth.** A bare nail and a rectangle nine inches by fourteen of paint that has not been
repainted since the twenty-ninth of last month. Ines Duarte comes in at half past seven and reads the
position out loud without being asked anything: **three copies of the record exist and the household now
holds none, which means a head who answers for six with a roll in the room has made a false statement with
the roll as the proof, and a head who answers for six with no roll in the room has made a statement that
cannot be checked, and in this city those are the same piece of paper.** Nine sentences, all about numbers,
none about herself. Sun Meiling works out what Wren Kallo's finding actually indicts, and the answer is
this floor. Petra Ilyin puts the thirteenth docket on the table and refuses to argue about the date — **the
mark goes in on the first of July, in the real book, in ink, in the broker's own hand, at nine in the
morning because she has never once in thirty years done anything to a real book before the building was
full** — and Marguerite Vale tells her to come down the sixty-one steps with her and **stand at the bottom
of them at ten to nine with a bundle under her arm, because she is the only person in this city who is on
no roll of any kind and has a reason to be standing on a pavement at eight in the morning.** Runa Sato does
the count one last time and gets it right without stopping. Nadia Ferrous asks the list question and gives a
full answer for the first time — seven on the roll, six with an address, six with a notebook, one in pencil
in a coat pocket, one in the house on no roll, the eighth on nothing at any time, one asleep behind a door
in a building she has not left in eleven years, and the blank line a district officer left on the third of
this month, which is the reason the whole month is happening.

## Volume 5, Batch 0004 — Chapters 231–240. THE TWENTY-SIXTH AND THE TWENTY-SEVENTH.

> **REPAIRED ON INDEPENDENT REVIEW.** An independent review of this batch returned 23 findings. All
> are fixed; the full adjudication is in `reviews/volume-05/batch-0004.md`. Six duplicated blocks were
> removed (232, 236 ×2, 238, 240, plus a spliced sentence in 236); the dead list is on the fourth in
> both places it had been wrong and in the outline; the invented term *hearing tail* is gone; the
> *He understood … He also understood* construction is cut from 7 uses to 0 and *no person* from 30 to
> 0; the list device runs once instead of six times; **236 is retitled from `Two Working Days` to
> `An Hour Late` because the twenty-sixth is a Friday and the certificate a Saturday**; and all ten
> chapters now sit between 1,767 and 1,958 words. The batch's arc, calendar spine and irreversible
> decision are unchanged.

**231 The Empty Room.** The twenty-first. The landing nail and the nine-by-fourteen rectangle. Bread,
work, the weeping valve, and the second-basin exchange — the one time this batch plays it in full. Sun
sews the seventh row shut (eleven stitches). Ines goes to Cobb Street. Runa counts aloud: five to the
schedule, six to the certificate, ten to the valuation. Nadia fixes the lists. He is frightened of the
twenty-third and says so to no one. Runa's hearing is on the first and the dead list on the fourth.

**232 What The Board Carries.** The twenty-second, Thursday, repaints. The ranks wall under the
Thursday crew at nine; the side board untouched (schedule of the fourth of last month, repainted only
on the first). Beatrix and the weight she will not write down. Wren on the step below the sixty-one:
her paper goes in on the morrow. Ines takes a sheet with a form number face-down for tomorrow. His
ordinary afternoon is a man asking whether the third-floor landing light is on all night.

**233 Named In A Third Line.** The twenty-third. The finding goes to Hadewig Mers, head of
counter-forensics, at ten: four edits, all removals, no addition in five years — a record someone has
had access to, and it is not his. A copy of the correction, produced from the index on the eighteenth,
arrives for the head. Agnete's counsel: do not go to a desk that has just got a paper. Tessa will enter
her clinic after the schedule, at Runa's arithmetic. Nadia rewrites rather than recites; the discipline
outlives the roll call.

**234 The Recital.** The twenty-fourth. Runa's anger — the batch's, and not a broker's. Her hearing
paper cannot be answered while a called standing cannot be drawn against: twenty-four days of frozen
entitlement, seven of them already gone. He carries the bundle to the Field division counter at
Cordwain, which closes at half past three; it goes through with a minute to spare and carries his
false six into a fourth office. **On the step, she does not explain herself, and he works out that he had
two seconds to stop it and said nothing.** Four offices hold him now where three held him that morning,
and there is her handwriting under his number in one of them. She tells him to tell Tessa to wait.

**235 The Bare Line.** The twenty-fifth. The quiet one. A day with Agnete: the pail, the yard, soup,
his brother spoken of for the first time in six months. At the second bucket she admits that being
found is the one thing she actually wants and that she has been too proud to arrange it. Tessa held
past the schedule. The district bag brings nothing. The blank of the third stays blank.

**236 An Hour Late.** The twenty-sixth — the last day the Office sits before the weekend closes over
it. The schedule goes up at ten on the side board: six members, his name at the head, no auditor on
its face. **Beatrix writes her six down in the square before a panel can ask her for it, and tells him
what is not on the board: four women who are not on it and eat at her table.** At eleven the removal
extract arrives — Ines taken off the correction of the twelfth, not the certification. An hour late and
a paper wide. She does not discuss it. The certificate will issue on the morrow, **a day the Office
does not sit on**, upon a schedule its own auditor is a member of, carrying six with no roll behind it.
Nadia leaves the extract face up and writes nothing down.

**237 The Account Remains Open.** The twenty-seventh. The certificate arrives at noon by carrier; Sun
signs the book. Six names, his hand carried forward, stamp and date. The Office does not sit and the
carrier sits for it. **Beatrix on what the carrier's silence on the step already means.** A second line
in the carrier's book: a notice of production for the thirty-year account at the valuation. Agnete
looks at the sheet without coming closer: is it mine. There is no answer in the room and there cannot
be one. The whole roll is about her and none of it has her name. Nadia writes nothing at all.

**238 A Number That Finds You.** The twenty-eighth. The man from Cobb Street brings the return slip;
he signs in his own hand — plate pledged, six written, receipt signed, the third act, and the batch's
irreversible decision. **The slip has a printed line under the signature for an object or a remark. He
leaves it blank, and the whole household watches him do it.** That blank is his, it goes into a file
with his name at the top, and the kitchen cannot say afterwards that he was not given the chance. The
certificate is now received and answerable. Sun: three papers that agree. Agnete: a room with two
certified papers wants to take testimony.

**239 The Frame And Her Hand.** The twenty-ninth, Thursday, repaints. Petra asks him to carry the
bundle while she looks at the wall — not the schedule but the frame and the clerks' hands. Eleven
years an officer, a fortnight discharged, hands that marked a hundred and thirty-two dockets. **A
stranger asks him whether the number on the board is a street number, and the answer costs him
nothing, and that is how a board works.** Petra on what a city knows and what an officer never asked.
Tessa's entry sheet read and true. At the landing he finds he was glad of the pale rectangle and does
not trust it. **At the foot of the steps on the way home his want becomes a plan: to be the one who
answers when the asking comes.**

**240 An Office With A Door On It.** The thirtieth. Ines asks for the room and says one procedural
thing: an office with a door on it, this afternoon, in writing, with a reason, in the way it is done —
without saying which office or which reason, and the instruction holds. **Lin Hao hears her say it in
her own words from the ninth of this month, word for word, twenty-one days late and nothing changed.**
Sun on the difference between a pocket sewn shut so a row cannot fall out and one sewn shut so it
cannot be seen. Tessa carries her clinic into the returned lines and asks that her reasoning be
remembered on the sixteenth and not worked out in a corridor later. Runa will not separate herself
from the household's standing. Agnete: a door that stays open is not a door that hides; it has not yet
been asked to shut. The house eats bread. The question waits behind an open door.

## Volume 5, Batch 0005 — Chapters 241–250. THE FIRST OF JULY AND AFTER. VOLUME 5 CLOSED IN PROSE.

> **REPAIRED ON INDEPENDENT REVIEW.** An independent review returned 11 findings and 5 minors; all are
> fixed, and the full adjudication is in `reviews/volume-05/batch-0005.md`. **Four of the summaries below
> were wrong and are corrected here:** 243's decision was priced against a deadline that had already
> passed and has been re-dated to the first of August; 245's title was a clock time; the Wren day counts
> drifted by one from 247 on and 250 contradicted itself; and 249's word count and staging were stale. The
> batch's arc, its calendar spine and its planned ending are unchanged.

**241 The Second Reading Room.** The first of July. Valuation at nine with the hundred and thirty-third mark in ink in the broker's own hand, Petra at ten to nine with the bundle. Two hearings in the morning, side board repainted by the monthly crew. A slip names one person of reading age for the second reading room at four. Agnete gives one true sentence voluntarily, her name and keeping, nothing compelled. **Volume 5's fourth scene, 2,730 words.** That night she shuts the back-room door herself for the first time in eleven years and asks him to stay. Explicit, consensual, her discipline throughout.

**242 The First Valuation.** The second. The mark is in, the board carries the twenty-sixth, both hearings kept their papers beyond the morning unasked. Tessa counted from the first, two hundred and three a morning. Wren's finding nine days on Hadewig's desk. Agnete open by day, shut once by night. A board persuades nobody and only makes the dear question easy to ask.

**243 What The Book Is Worth.** The third. At the north desk Marguerite offers him the only thing she may offer a head: say the word on or before **the twelfth of July** and she will leave the **first of August docket unmarked** — run broken at the hundred and thirty-fourth place, dead list on the fourth of August, plate back, Beatrix off the guild's tail, the fourth page struck and burned. After the twelfth she will not offer again in the life of the account. **He refuses in writing, standing instruction, the hour under it, two officers in the queue.** The option closes in advance and permanently. The book is worth the word produce, and he has told the only woman who can mark it that he will be at the desk every time.

**244 The Hundred And Thirty-Third.** The fourth. The dead list goes up quiet without the account. One hundred and thirty-three marks with no gaps. The plate stays lodged, the securities stay, Beatrix stays encumbered. Wren eleven days. A quiet list is still a list, and men read what is not on it by evening.

**245 What She Had Worked Out.** The fifth. Tessa's hatch run, the house mending, Ines's bag packed by an open door. Sun's twenty-two stitches and three years of waiting on a question never put to her. Wren twelve days. The Thursday crew due on the morrow. The quiet day before the leaving, and the only day of the batch with no new paper on the table.

**246 What She Filed.** The sixth, Thursday, repaints. Asked in a room with a door on it to state her review position, Ines files eleven minutes in her own hand: index seven, certificate six, auditor-member cannot certify. **The officer tells her a member may not report on a return her own name stands on, and that the entry goes up with her report and does not go up alone.** She says she would rather be a subject than a reader and files it anyway. **True report filed. She walks out of a Ferrier flat she is still in on paper for her own rooms on Lantern Row.** Not a death, not a betrayal.

**247 The Room She Left.** The seventh. The empty room swept and kept, its name on every paper, her notebook shut on the shelf with her name on the corner. Five debts pencilled, and **Marguerite produces the instrument for giving up a room, which would price the household at five — he does not put it in front of Ines**, because asking her to be the reason a paper says five is asking her to sign first. Wren fourteen days. The division must answer for having produced both seven and six.

**248 The Day Book.** The eighth. Tessa's day book with his initials on the first line of the month and no number. The wall, the rail, the steps kept by hand. Wren fifteen days, going to the desk on the morrow. Two crews and two shades, and he can tell them apart now without stopping to think.

**249 The Report That Is True.** The ninth. At Lantern Row Ines reads the ordinary record and keeps it, then tells him what the division has written against her and that she cannot pay for two rooms on a fortnight's answering. **Volume 5's fifth scene, 2,795 words, gap 241 to 249 eight, zero 12-gram overlap with 241 at twelve and ten words.** Staged against 241 entirely: a washstand, a bed under an open shutter, a chair and a stool, a worktable, no curtain, no shaded window, no pale room. Explicit, consensual, in her own rooms with the door shut by her own hand and opened by it. **She sets a term: no paper is read to her in her own rooms. He agrees in the dark.** Afterwards she stays in her rooms, still on roll and address on paper, signing for the household when signing is what it needs — and he goes home and puts the three papers in a drawer under the bread.

**250 What Was Owed To Whom.** The tenth. Five debts priced plain, the one woman who can price them out with a true document behind her. **Marguerite names the sixth line, which is not a debt: the standing instruction, with no figure against it and no office that will ever invoice it.** He wants rank nine in the same words, costing more. Wren seventeen days, the true report four. Volume 5 closes on the filed answer waiting for creditors — and on a date two days past the close of the batch: **the twelfth of July.**

---

## Volume 6 — The Broker's Terms

> In this volume, **Lin Hao must call in every debt he owes before the creditors call in him**, or **the Vant's books put a price on the reservoir that he cannot refuse**.

### Volume 6, Batch 0002 — Chapters 261–270. THE TWENTY-FIRST TO THE THIRTIETH OF JULY.

**Ten chapters, one a day. One scene, at 261, and it is immovable and it is Volume 6's first.** The last
scene was Chapter 249, so 249 → 261 = 12, and the next is owed at 269 and is **not** written here; the batch
prompt is explicit that there is an explicit scene inside 261 and nowhere else. Read
`reviews/volume-06/batch-0002.md` before inheriting any date from these summaries.

**The batch performs the second half of the volume's sentence. Batch 0001 called the debts in; this batch
deals with the account they landed in, which is now in a stranger's book.**

#### Ch 261 — The Verse She Did Not Choose
**SCENE. IMMOVABLE. Isolde Marchetti, 39, singer at the Bellhouse.** A verse with no name and no street and
a number in it, bought at a box office for twenty pence on the eighteenth of July by a man in a good coat
who said *say it exactly, and do not change the number*. It is in repertory with no date on the card.
**He buys a seat at the back of the fifth house and lets it happen to him, because he is the man in it and
there was one room left in the city that did not have him in it.** He takes the date off her — the eighteenth,
a man in a good coat at the office end of a bar — and she tells him he could have taken the card down in four
minutes and has not, and he cannot give her a reason she would accept. **The scene** is in the dressing room
over a stage door: eight feet by seven, a cot, a jug, a bar of painted iron, a mirror with eleven bulbs and
eight lit. **She tests it and it does not happen — the first room in twenty years in which a room that hears
her does not agree with her — and she says plainly that what moves her is him and not her gift**, and that she
will sing something of her own on the twenty-third and will not tell him what it is and will not have him in
the house. **Ends: the card says IN REPERTORY and does not say who bought it, and in two days she sings to
nine hundred people and he is not there.** Plate untouched. Ledger untouched. Zero panels.

#### Ch 262 — The Room He Will Not Give Up
A guild's weekly reconciliation on the twentieth shows an entry against premises 34-6-118 at an establishment
the guild does not keep, and four initials: **S.R.** **Marguerite Vale is a woman who has been told her client
went to her rival, and she comes to the north desk standing up.** She puts the instrument on the counter and
tells him it was never what he thought: **a head may give up a room without an auditor at all** — it takes a
week and her signature, and he had been standing on a false reason for fifteen days because she let him.
**The price is Ines Duarte going off the paper forever: five in August, no seventh anywhere, nothing to
contest.** He does not sign the figure. Sun Meiling works it out at the kitchen table in ninety seconds and
says the sentence the batch is built on: *it is not about me, it has been about her since the seventh of July
and I have been pleased about it.* **She asks whether she would be in it and is told she would — in the
conditional, if he signed — and the conditional is the whole of the offer.** **Ends: the week runs to the
twenty-ninth and there is nothing he can do on the twenty-eighth, and nothing on the twenty-ninth.**

#### Ch 263 — The First Room That Agreed With Her
Agnete Lindqvist goes out at night for the second time in eleven years and takes a front seat in the gallery
to hear the answer. **She does not want to be told what the song is. She wants to know whether she is safe in
a room.** Isolde sings the verse first because the house expects it, then sings something of her own, and the
answer names the block by its two names for itself and puts **34-6-118 into nine hundred mouths** without ever
saying a name. **The household is public, and it happened because a woman chose a tune.** Afterward the
agreement goes out of the house like a lamp and Agnete tells him that eleven years of holding a number in a
room came out of her hands like water out of a cracked jug, and that a chaplain is a person who has worked
out what it costs a room to say a number out loud. **Petra Ilyin comes up the last four steps and says the
whole of it: there is no difference between her and Agnete, and that is the difference.** **Wren Kallo writes
the date against it in her own book — the first date anyone in this city has ever written against anything
that has happened to Agnete Lindqvist — and says that is a door and not a protection, and that she did it
without asking and would again.** **Ends: a woman asleep against a warm wall, a date in a clerk's hand on a
page where nothing else is, a verse, a song with a door number in it, and nine days to the first.**

#### Ch 264 — The Mark In The Broker's Hand
The twenty-fourth, four days after a Thursday, and Solveig Rask is at the table because keepers work every day.
**The standing he lodged on the seventh of June was called on the twentieth of July at twenty-five minutes
past four** — while he was swearing seven in a room at Cobb Street. The mark is a device cut into a
pen-holder and a device can be cut again by anybody who has held the pen, so Rask cannot name a hand. **What
she can do is reconcile, and she tells him the price before he asks: a dated page in a real book saying a call
was entered and that the entry and the broker's own account do not agree, in four divisions by Friday, and the
woman it is about cannot produce the pen.** He says *do it* once. **The reservoir is one sentence away from
description and she does not write a figure on it** — thirty-one lines in a column and, under the column,
one page with a figure on it and two letters at the foot that are his. **Four hundred and nineteen days.**
**Ends: he walks out into a clear morning with a page of his own making coming on Thursday.**

#### Ch 265 — The Half He Carried In His Coat
Cordwain, the Field counter, ten to three, and he has carried one half of a two-part carbon in a coat since
the eleventh without looking at it. He has come to have it priced: **a priced hearing is a secured hearing
and a secured hearing survives a dead list.** Runa Sato says the counter has offered her the identical
instrument once a week for four weeks and she has said no four times, **because a security is what the guild
reached for over Beatrix and she will not buy herself out of the household's risk.** She pushes it back
across the counter: *a thing in your coat for a fortnight and in my bag for a fortnight has been in neither
of our hands for a fortnight, and after the ninth of a month this street is a street with no press in it.*
He gives it to her. **The hearing goes stale at midnight on the thirtieth and is not resolved.** **Ends: two
halves of a reconciliation in the same folder, never together and never going to be.**

#### Ch 266 — The Form Behind A Pencil Row
The charge's own fourteen days run out at Cobb Street and nothing arrives, because a charge a panel has
carried is an item and not a charge. **He has been asking the wrong woman for a signature: the impediment is
a bar on the certificate, not a bar to certifying, and it comes off by removing the person it is on.** A change
of auditor of record is a household office and a head may change it in writing. **He signs it on the
twenty-sixth, in his own hand, with the date under it, at a bare kitchen table, and says out loud, because Sun
Meiling made him, that he is doing it for the row.** Nadia Ferrous puts her own name on the witness line for
the first time in eleven years. **He had set himself the thirtieth as the day to sign, worked out in front of
them that the division acknowledges on the first either way and that the four days therefore bought nothing,
and signed that night instead — four days before his own date — when Sun Meiling told him he had already had
eleven of those days and she had asked him for every one.** **A change is acknowledged on the first; the first
is a valuation morning; a month has thirty days and no thirty-first — so Sun's row goes into ink on the first
of September, and the price is the office that made Ines Duarte certifiable.** **Ends: there is no lawful
way to give a woman notice of something he has just taken from her.**

#### Ch 267 — Beatrix Reading A Wall Aloud
Thursday. The crew comes at nine and the figures go up at ten and a wall is read before it is painted.
**A published security binds everybody who takes from the account afterwards, so the guild posted its hold on
Beatrix Halm's hearing, and a foreman reads it out loud in a public square** — the first time in four years
anything about her has been in more hands than her own. She writes it down word for word, wants it left and
not struck, and then **goes and stands under the ranks wall so that the song is over it**, which is the
second time in nine days something in this household has been put in front of a room that cannot argue with
it. At the north end he reads the whole lower half of the wall for the first time and finds four words about
himself, and a man on the ground tells him what the last working day of the quarter will bring. **Ends: plate
8,138 against a floor of 8,141 on the bottom band, WITHIN, and three days.**

#### Ch 268 — The Book In Her Own Rooms
Marguerite Vale comes at nine with a leather bag and puts it on the bare table: **her working book, her
attestation book, and the key to the chained room, all of it out of this building since the fourteenth of
July.** She knows what hand made the entry of the twentieth and **will not tell him**: she will say it to a
panel, under her own signature, before the end of the month, and she is asking him to promise never to ask
her again. **He promises.** The choice is the disagreement or the eleven-day gap and Sun Meiling sends the
bag back onto the fourth floor at nine minutes past nine. **Ends: he has spent a broker's standing in four
divisions, and there is still no lawful way to tell the woman whose office he signed away on the
twenty-sixth.**

#### Ch 269 — A Cage With A Door On Both Sides
The instrument dies at midnight of its own week and he writes the refusal out late, in his own hand, with the
hour, because a silence and a refusal are different documents in four years' time. **Nadia Ferrous, the lift,
eleven at night.** She has been writing a second copy out by hand one page a night since the nineteenth —
eleven pages — and she has not skipped a day and cannot write this one. **The position: the only record in
this city no office can be served on lives in a cage with a door at each end and forty people going through
it a night, and holding it is not a safe place for a person to be, and she is putting it there anyway.** Her
want is the only one in this book nobody has said: not to be on the roll, **to be in it.** **No scene is
written in this chapter. The scene is owed inside 269 and Batch 0003 opens on it.** **Ends: a blank page and a
promise not to help her write it.**

#### Ch 270 — Eight Thousand One Hundred And Forty-Two
**The quarterly bands go up on the last working day of the quarter by a different office in a shade the
square has never had**, and a foreman reads the wall out loud, and the floor moves from 8,141 to **8,142**
and the word on the bottom band changes from *within* to **out of band**, with his address and his plate on
it. **He is four under. Rask said on the thirteenth that this would take four quarters and it took seventeen
days.** A woman of forty asks him if he is all right. Sun Meiling is glad and says so honestly; Petra Ilyin
counts herself out loud; **Agnete Lindqvist is in a Square in daylight and asks not to be walked home.** He
says it on a corner at twenty past nine, in daylight, in front of the women in that building: he will be at
the bottom of the steps at ten to nine tomorrow. **Then the post comes — a division's notice that the
composition is out of band as from the thirtieth, that a re-issue delivered on the twenty-sixth is
acknowledged on the first, and that the composition is heard on the second of August at ten with the former
auditor of record required to attend.** **Ends: 8,138 against 8,142 on a wall in a blue this square has not
had before, and at ten to nine tomorrow a hundred and thirty-fourth mark and the answer to the standing he
lodged on the seventh of June go into a chained book with one pen in about eleven seconds.**

---

### Volume 6, Batch 0001 — Chapters 251–260. THE ELEVENTH TO THE TWENTIETH OF JULY.

**Ten chapters, one a day, no scene.** Batch 0001 inherits `outline/volume-05.md` §7 in full, because there
was no `outline/volume-06.md`; one is now written, from these ten chapters, and its §1–§5 are written fact.
**The batch has since been independently reviewed and repaired — seven chapter-level defects and four
minors, all fixed, no plot moved. Read `reviews/volume-06/batch-0001.md` §6 before inheriting any date from
these summaries. The five that change what a later batch would otherwise derive wrongly: the kitchen table
is **bare from the fourteenth only** and held the house from the twenty-second of March · the clinic count
**begins on the first of July** and Tessa's note on its back is dated **the ninth of July** · **a room holds
eight women, four on the roll and four on nothing**, and seven are looking at him at 258:101 · the **ranks
wall was never finished above the middle** · Beatrix's price died on the third and he carried it **sixteen
days**.

**251 The One He Named First.** The eleventh. He has one day left to say what he owes before somebody else
does, and the first name is the one he wrote in his own hand. At the bottom of the sixty-one steps, at ten
to seven, he tells **Runa Sato** that the six on the sworn page is his, that he wrote it on the fourteenth
of June, and that it is the reason her answer went into a fourth office — **twenty-seven days she has
carried it.** She asks what he wants her to do with it. **Nothing.** He is not asking her to file it, and
that is the cost, and he says so out loud on a stair he chose because at the table a thing becomes a record
and Nadia writes it down. **She produces a two-part carbon reconciliation slip she has had all along,
fills it in, dates it the eleventh and does not send it, and gives him the bottom half: neither half can be
produced without the other.** Four people knew the figure was his. He made it five, on a stair, in the
rain. Petra thirty-seven days on no roll and tells him she will be told before the first of August or not
at all. **Ends at the foot of Lantern Row's stairs at half past seven with the valuation copy rolled in
his hand, and he does not go up, and the twelfth is tomorrow.** *Repaired on independent review — the
chapter previously had the kitchen table bare for five months and the household eating round a bare table on
the eleventh. The table is **not** bare on the eleventh: the broker's account lies square on it where it
has lain since the nineteenth of June.*

**252 The Charge Against The Schedule.** The twelfth. **The offer expires by the operation of a date.** He
watches the door all day and is ashamed of watching and says so to Beatrix, who asks why, and who is told:
*I said no to something on the third of July and I would like very much to see it arrive anyway.* At half
past four a form-numbered envelope: **a reconciliation charge against the certified schedule, for the audit
that found seven where the schedule says six, falling on the head of the schedule, which is him.** Fourteen
days, to the twenty-sixth. He does not pay it, because a paid reconciliation is a schedule that agrees
with itself at six. **At seven a guild boy brings four words in Marguerite's hand — the account runs to the
hundred and thirty-fourth mark on the first of August, bring the plate. That is the proof of the expiry:
the four words are not the offer.** The standing is spent. It cannot be bought, cannot be asked for, and
will not be offered again in the life of a thirty-year account. **Marguerite tells him plainly there is
another book in the city and a woman who keeps it who has never met one of them, and that if he goes
through that door he will get a number and lose the only person who answers for what happens to him.** A
blank remark line on a receipt and a blank remark line on a charge, and he leaves both.

**253 The Keeper Of The Real Books.** The thirteenth, Thursday, the pass on the ranks wall. **The debts
have to be priced before the first of August** — an unpriced security cannot be carried a year running —
and he has no pricer, so he goes to the other book. **SOLVEIG RASK, 42, ENTERS.** Two floors under the
Exchange sales floor, no window, a stone floor, four books with unwritten spines. *The guild's ledger is a
beautiful lie and earns everybody in it a living. Mine are the movements.* She prices four of five and
refuses the fifth, and the refusal is the point of the chapter: **a dispute is not a movement, it is the
absence of one, and until a transaction happens the only debt he has is free, and free is not the same as
small.** The open security is worth **a week he has not fallen** — 8,138 against a floor of 8,141 with a
tolerance of three, floors rising every quarter. Then she asks him what stands behind the plate, and tells
him she has had his word in her book for four years under a column of thirty-one and left one dated,
initialled page in on purpose. **She refuses to price it: I do not price a thing I cannot describe. When I
do price it there will be a date on the page and you will not be able to refuse it, because a dated figure
in a real book is not an offer. It is a fact.** And she charges him for the hour, against his own account,
because a stranger's question in that room is a movement. **She and Marguerite price the same thing in the
same words, and the only difference between them is which book it is in.**

**254 The Table Bare.** The fourteenth. **Marguerite knows by nine** — guilds are small — and comes up the
sixty-one steps with her gloves on and no pen and does not sit down. She takes the valuation copy, the dead
list copies and the five pencil lines off the table in about eleven seconds and gives the reason in her own
discipline: *a broker of record stands answerable for what is in her book, and yesterday a stranger put a
question into a book that reconciles and it came out of this house.* She prices the debts once more in her
own hand before she goes, and the figure is very nearly what the stranger charged, **and the horror is
that the number was never in dispute — only the book.** On the stair she says the thing that costs: *take
the six off your own schedule and give me the account at six, and there is a woman in four years holding my
book with a name at the top of it that a stranger chose on a Thursday*, and that this guild prints no
instrument by which a broker may hold a client's pricing and has printed none since she was twenty-two.
**Beatrix: then there is nothing on the table to disagree with.** **Nadia writes the day with the hour
against it and enters her own name against the line, for the fifth time.** Runa leaves her folder on the
bare table. Petra forty days on no roll asks what the mark on the first of August is keeping. Wren twenty-one
days. **Ends at the drawer under the bread: four papers he knew about, all of them the house, and a table
that had held this house since the twenty-second of March holding nothing at all.** *Repaired on
independent review — the table was previously dated as bare for five months, and `254:107` had claimed
nothing had ever been laid on it at all.*

**255 Twenty-Two Stitches.** The fifteenth. The third debt. **Sun Meiling, out loud, at the table:** *I owe
you three years of a question nobody put to you.* She takes the coat off the chair, shakes it, and the
pencil row comes out of the lining on twenty-two stitches — **and she wants it counted.** Not corrected,
not footnoted: a row. She says yes is the certificate's fault and says it fast and gladly. **He says yes in
front of the room.** The price lands in the same minute: a re-issue at seven needs the head's sworn answer
and **the auditor of record's hand**, and the auditor of record is a woman in two rooms above a laundry
who will not be asked yet. **The household holds an answered yes that cannot be effected.** Nadia writes it
with the hour and signs for it and says she is a witness and not a keeper. Runa tells her the truth about
what it does to a lie she carried into a fourth office, and goes out of the front door without slamming it.
Beatrix puts a saucer over the row. **Sun: after the first of August I am not putting it back in a coat
for anybody, including the head of this household.** Ends with him opening the drawer under the bread for
the first time since March for a clean sheet and finding at the back of it **a fifth paper, which is Tessa's
count from the first of July, which came into the flat on the back of a household return and which he has
never once opened: a blank answer line, and a note on the back in her own hand dated the ninth of this
month.** *Repaired on independent review — the note previously read "the sixteenth of last month", which
predates the sheet it is written on.*

**256 Nine Years In A Line.** The sixteenth. From a quarter of eight at the market hatch, two hundred and
three a morning. He offers his plate number against the answer line of a counted clinic return and she
says **yes in the first minute**, and the reason is not courage: *nine years I have kept a hatch that no
one in this city could point at, and that was a defence and a good one, and I was hiding behind it.
Answerable is the difference between a person and a hatch.* **He writes 8,138, dates it the sixteenth,
and signs his own name because the return wanted a hand and not a number.** **THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE
DECISION.** A plate on a Bureau line is a plate that has been looked at, and he has been looked at by
nobody for five years. **It cannot be got off: a return is a return and a re-issue takes a division's
season.** Tessa says he will regret it inside a month and does not care. Lode says the only strange thing
about this building is finished, and that everything else in it will hear inside a week. Beatrix refuses to
put it in the ordinary record and hands it to Nadia's book instead. Petra, forty-two days on no roll, asks
what a man is supposed to be after three signatures. **August's first line of the day book is to get a
number on it.** And he is not sorry, **and that was new.** *Tessa's note on the back of the count is dated
the **ninth of July** and she took the sheet off his bare kitchen table at six that morning; all repaired
on independent review.*

**257 The Only Room She Owns.** The seventeenth. He goes to Lantern Row at half past seven with six slips
and a form-numbered envelope and **does not go up.** She opens the door and comes down four steps and stops
on the seventh, in her stockings, with a candle end behind her, because the rooms are hers and the stair is
not, and because if he comes up four more steps she will go back in and shut the door and be right to.
**He reads her four figures from the step below, with the paper in his hand, and that is the whole of the
term.** She tells him the thing the term costs: **a price is not a figure, a price is a figure against a
security held by a named holder**, and to price his debts she would have to hold the plate, which puts her
inside the account she walked out of on the sixth. **And she makes him do the working for the twentieth:
the sixth, the entry that went up with it, a fortnight as the ordinary answer, six and fourteen. The
twentieth.** She named about a month on the ninth and she says she was being generous to herself and wrong
in the direction of hoping. **She also tells him he has not asked her for Sun's re-issue, that the term is
paper and the term is not her name, and that in three days the line he has been standing on will be gone.**
She will not be thanked for the stair and does not pretend it is free. **He goes home and counts the days
to the first of August off the side board, which still says six.**

**258 One Of The Nine.** The eighteenth. After the washing. **The kitchen holds eight women — four on the
cut roll and four on nothing at all — and he tells the room he keeps a ledger.** A hundred and eighteen
lines, faces and streets and hours, never deeds. Nine turned — people who have felt
something cold from his hand and started keeping track. **The ninth is himself**, and he does not explain
it, and the room notices. **Agnete: you have done the whole of my discipline in my kitchen in eleven
seconds and you have not once stood in my room — both of those are the same thing and you have spent five
years believing the difference is what you have.** **Runa: the first debt was the one you have been
carrying since April, and I want to know which of those was for me and which of those was for the room.
That was for the room. And that is the answer and I hate it.** **Petra asks one question and it is the
enforcer's: how many of the nine know they are on it.** **Marguerite refuses, in front of everyone, to
price it, because a thing with no deed in it has no yield and a security with no yield is a rumour.**
**Wren gives the batch its threat: nine is not a rumour, nine is a route, and every instrument that reads
it is on a list that Rask's books reconcile against.** Nadia writes the whole of it with the hour and her
own name against it. Beatrix refuses to put it in the ordinary record, for the first time in four years.
**He cannot get it back, and it is now in three written places in one kitchen and will be in a fourth by
morning.** *Repaired on independent review — `258:7` and `258:101` previously counted the room two ways.*

**259 The Price She Was Never Shown.** The nineteenth. The debt he could not begin without telling Beatrix
what he had cost her — **sixteen days after the third of July, not a fortnight; repaired on independent
review.** **On the third of July the broker offered to leave the first of August docket
unmarked — the account struck out on the fourth of August, the plate back, and every security standing
against the account coming off the same day, which is the rule, and one of them is what the guild holds
over her hearing.** He refused it in a queue. He told her on the third that there was no price left on the
table. **He never told her a price had been on the table with her name on it, and it expired at seven on
the twelfth.** She asks what the price was and then asks the question under the price: **would you have
said yes.** He says *I do not know*, which is not the answer she wanted and is the true one, and the
batch's sharpest sentence is his: *I would like to tell you I would have let that go. I cannot.* She
